   619			if node == nil {
   620				return nil
   621			}
   622		} else if _, ok := builtin.Index[token.Value]; ok && (p.config == nil || !p.config.Disabled[token.Value]) && !isOverridden {
   623			node = p.createNode(&BuiltinNode{
   624				Name:      token.Value,
   625				Arguments: p.parseArguments(arguments),
   626			}, token.Location)
   627			if node == nil {
   628				return nil
   629			}
   630	
   631		} else {
   632			callee := p.createNode(&IdentifierNode{Value: token.Value}, token.Location)
   633			if callee == nil {
   634				return nil
   635			}
   636			node = p.createNode(&CallNode{
   637				Callee:    callee,
   638				Arguments: p.parseArguments(arguments),
   639			}, token.Location)
   640			if node == nil {
   641				return nil
   642			}
   643		}
   644		return node
   645	}
   646	
   647	func (p *Parser) parseArguments(arguments []Node) []Node {
   648		// If pipe operator is used, the first argument is the left-hand side
   649		// of the operator, so we do not parse it as an argument inside brackets.
   650		offset := len(arguments)
   651	
   652		p.expect(Bracket, "(")
   653		for !p.current.Is(Bracket, ")") && p.err == nil {
   654			if len(arguments) > offset {
   655				p.expect(Operator, ",")
   656			}
   657			if p.current.Is(Bracket, ")") {
   658				break
   659			}
   660			node := p.parseExpression(0)
   661			arguments = append(arguments, node)
   662		}
   663		p.expect(Bracket, ")")
   664	
   665		return arguments
   666	}
   667	
   668	func (p *Parser) parsePredicate() Node {
   669		startToken := p.current
   670		withBrackets := false
   671		if p.current.Is(Bracket, "{") {
   672			p.next()
   673			withBrackets = true
   674		}
   675	
   676		p.depth++
   677		var node Node
   678		if withBrackets {
   679			node = p.parseSequenceExpression()
   680		} else {
   681			node = p.parseExpression(0)
   682			if p.current.Is(Operator, ";") {
   683				p.error("wrap predicate with brackets { and }")
   684			}
   685		}
   686		p.depth--
   687	
   688		if withBrackets {
   689			p.expect(Bracket, "}")
   690		}
   691		predicateNode := p.createNode(&PredicateNode{
   692			Node: node,
   693		}, startToken.Location)
   694		if predicateNode == nil {
   695			return nil
   696		}
   697		return predicateNode
   698	}
   699	
   700	func (p *Parser) parseArrayExpression(token Token) Node {
   701		nodes := make([]Node, 0)
   702	
   703		p.expect(Bracket, "[")
   704		for !p.current.Is(Bracket, "]") && p.err == nil {
   705			if len(nodes) > 0 {
   706				p.expect(Operator, ",")
   707				if p.current.Is(Bracket, "]") {
   708					goto end
   709				}
   710			}
   711			node := p.parseExpression(0)
   712			nodes = append(nodes, node)
   713		}
   714	end:
   715		p.expect(Bracket, "]")
   716	
   717		node := p.createNode(&ArrayNode{Nodes: nodes}, token.Location)
   718		if node == nil {
   719			return nil
   720		}

     1	package lexer
     2	
     3	import (
     4		"strings"
     5	
     6		"github.com/expr-lang/expr/parser/utils"
     7	)
     8	
     9	type stateFn func(*Lexer) stateFn
    10	
    11	func root(l *Lexer) stateFn {
    12		switch r := l.next(); {
    13		case r == eof:
    14			l.emitEOF()
    15			return nil
    16		case utils.IsSpace(r):
    17			l.skip()
    18			return root
    19		case r == '\'' || r == '"':
    20			l.scanString(r)
    21			str, err := unescape(l.word())
    22			if err != nil {
    23				l.error("%v", err)
    24			}
    25			l.emitValue(String, str)
    26		case r == '`':
    27			l.scanRawString(r)
    28		case (r == 'b' || r == 'B') && (l.peek() == '\'' || l.peek() == '"'):
    29			quote := l.next()
    30			l.scanString(quote)
    31			str, err := unescapeBytes(l.word()[1:]) // skip 'b'
    32			if err != nil {
    33				l.error("%v", err)
    34			}
    35			l.emitValue(Bytes, str)
    36		case '0' <= r && r <= '9':
    37			l.backup()
    38			return number
    39		case r == '?':
    40			return questionMark
    41		case r == '/':
    42			return slash
    43		case r == '#':
    44			return pointer
    45		case r == '|':
    46			l.accept("|")
    47			l.emit(Operator)
    48		case r == ':':
    49			l.accept(":")
    50			l.emit(Operator)
    51		case strings.ContainsRune("([{", r):
    52			l.emit(Bracket)
    53		case strings.ContainsRune(")]}", r):
    54			l.emit(Bracket)
    55		case strings.ContainsRune(",;%+-^", r): // single rune operator
    56			l.emit(Operator)
    57		case strings.ContainsRune("&!=*<>", r): // possible double rune operator
    58			l.accept("&=*")
    59			l.emit(Operator)
    60		case r == '.':
    61			l.backup()
    62			return dot
    63		case utils.IsAlphaNumeric(r):
    64			l.backup()
    65			return identifier
    66		default:
    67			return l.error("unrecognized character: %#U", r)
    68		}
    69		return root
    70	}
    71	
    72	func number(l *Lexer) stateFn {
    73		if !l.scanNumber() {
    74			return l.error("bad number syntax: %q", l.word())
    75		}
    76		l.emit(Number)
    77		return root
    78	}
    79	
    80	func (l *Lexer) scanNumber() bool {
    81		digits := "0123456789_"
    82		// Is it hex?
    83		if l.accept("0") {
    84			// Note: Leading 0 does not mean octal in floats.
    85			if l.accept("xX") {
    86				digits = "0123456789abcdefABCDEF_"
    87			} else if l.accept("oO") {
    88				digits = "01234567_"
    89			} else if l.accept("bB") {
    90				digits = "01_"
    91			}
    92		}
    93		l.acceptRun(digits)
    94		end := l.end
    95		if l.accept(".") {
    96			// Lookup for .. operator: if after dot there is another dot (1..2), it maybe a range operator.
    97			if l.peek() == '.' {
    98				// We can't backup() here, as it would require two backups,
    99				// and backup() func supports only one for now. So, save and
   100				// restore it here.
   101				l.end = end
   102				return true
   103			}
   104			l.acceptRun(digits)
   105		}
   106		if l.accept("eE") {
   107			l.accept("+-")
   108			l.acceptRun(digits)
   109		}
   110		// Next thing mustn't be alphanumeric.
   111		if utils.IsAlphaNumeric(l.peek()) {
   112			l.next()
   113			return false
   114		}
   115		return true
   116	}
   117	
   118	func dot(l *Lexer) stateFn {
   119		l.next()
   120		if l.accept("0123456789") {

REASONING_CONTENT
Good. The lexer treats keywords as Identifier. So `try`, `catch`, `finally`, `throw`, `retry`, `errtype` are all Identifiers.

Now, I need to decide how to implement this. Let me design carefully, focusing on correctness.

## AST Nodes

I'll add:
- `TryNode` - represents try/catch/finally.
  ```go
  type TryNode struct {
      base
      Try     Node   // body
      Catch   Node   // catch body (handler)
      CatchName string // optional name for binding error
      CatchTest Node // optional substring expression (string) to match error message
      Finally Node   // optional finally body
  }
  ```
  Or separate CatchNode/FinallyNode. Let me look at the actual upstream AST. I recall expr added:
  ```go
  type TryNode struct {
      base
      Try   Node
      Catch Node
      Finally Node
  }
  type CatchNode struct {
      base
      Node Node
      Name string
      Is   Node
  }
  type FinallyNode struct {
      base
      Node Node
  }
  type ThrowNode struct {
      base
      Node Node
  }
  ```
  And `RetryNode`? Actually `retry` is just an identifier resolved specially.

Let me design my own:
```go
type TryNode struct {
    base
    Try     Node
    Catch   *CatchNode
    Finally *FinallyNode
}
type CatchNode struct {
    base
    Name string
    Is   Node  // substring condition
    Node Node  // handler body
}
type FinallyNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}
type ThrowNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}
type RetryNode struct {
    base
}
```

Actually simpler: TryNode with Catch/Finally fields as Nodes and names. But separate nodes make checker and compiler cleaner.

The `try(expr, fallback)` function form: parser creates `TryNode{ Try: expr, Catch: &CatchNode{Node: fallback} }` (no catch name). This is equivalent
to `try { expr } catch { fallback }`.

I'll add:
- `ThrowNode`
- `TryNode` (with `Catch` and `Finally`)

Let me define:
```go
type TryNode struct {
    base
    Try     Node
    Catch   Node   // nil if no catch
    Finally Node   // nil if no finally
    CatchName string  // if non-empty, bind caught error
    CatchIs  Node     // substring condition
}
```

Hmm, but I'd rather keep a CatchNode so the parser is cleaner and the checker/compiler can pattern-match. Let me use:
```go
type TryNode struct {
    base
    Try     Node
    Catch   Node
    Finally Node
}
type CatchNode struct {
    base
    Name string
    Is   Node
    Node Node
}
type FinallyNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}
type ThrowNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}
type RetryNode struct {
    base
}
```

This matches upstream style. Let me check the AST visitor to add cases.

     1	package ast
     2	
     3	import "fmt"
     4	
     5	type Visitor interface {
     6		Visit(node *Node)
     7	}
     8	
     9	func Walk(node *Node, v Visitor) {
    10		if *node == nil {
    11			return
    12		}
    13		switch n := (*node).(type) {
    14		case *NilNode:
    15		case *IdentifierNode:
    16		case *IntegerNode:
    17		case *FloatNode:
    18		case *BoolNode:
    19		case *StringNode:
    20		case *BytesNode:
    21		case *ConstantNode:
    22		case *UnaryNode:
    23			Walk(&n.Node, v)
    24		case *BinaryNode:
    25			Walk(&n.Left, v)
    26			Walk(&n.Right, v)
    27		case *ChainNode:
    28			Walk(&n.Node, v)
    29		case *MemberNode:
    30			Walk(&n.Node, v)
    31			Walk(&n.Property, v)
    32		case *SliceNode:
    33			Walk(&n.Node, v)
    34			if n.From != nil {
    35				Walk(&n.From, v)
    36			}
    37			if n.To != nil {
    38				Walk(&n.To, v)
    39			}
    40		case *CallNode:
    41			Walk(&n.Callee, v)
    42			for i := range n.Arguments {
    43				Walk(&n.Arguments[i], v)
    44			}
    45		case *BuiltinNode:
    46			for i := range n.Arguments {
    47				Walk(&n.Arguments[i], v)
    48			}
    49		case *PredicateNode:
    50			Walk(&n.Node, v)
    51		case *PointerNode:
    52		case *VariableDeclaratorNode:
    53			Walk(&n.Value, v)
    54			Walk(&n.Expr, v)
    55		case *SequenceNode:
    56			for i := range n.Nodes {
    57				Walk(&n.Nodes[i], v)
    58			}
    59		case *ConditionalNode:
    60			Walk(&n.Cond, v)
    61			Walk(&n.Exp1, v)
    62			Walk(&n.Exp2, v)
    63		case *ArrayNode:
    64			for i := range n.Nodes {
    65				Walk(&n.Nodes[i], v)
    66			}
    67		case *MapNode:
    68			for i := range n.Pairs {
    69				Walk(&n.Pairs[i], v)
    70			}
    71		case *PairNode:
    72			Walk(&n.Key, v)
    73			Walk(&n.Value, v)
    74		default:
    75			panic(fmt.Sprintf("undefined node type (%T)", node))
    76		}
    77	
    78		v.Visit(node)
    79	}

    12	func (n *NilNode) String() string {
    13		return "nil"
    14	}
    15	
    16	func (n *IdentifierNode) String() string {
    17		return n.Value
    18	}
    19	
    20	func (n *IntegerNode) String() string {
    21		return fmt.Sprintf("%d", n.Value)
    22	}
    23	
    24	func (n *FloatNode) String() string {
    25		return fmt.Sprintf("%v", n.Value)
    26	}
    27	
    28	func (n *BoolNode) String() string {
    29		return fmt.Sprintf("%t", n.Value)
    30	}
    31	
    32	func (n *StringNode) String() string {
    33		return fmt.Sprintf("%q", n.Value)
    34	}
    35	
    36	func (n *BytesNode) String() string {
    37		return fmt.Sprintf("b%q", n.Value)
    38	}
    39	
    40	func (n *ConstantNode) String() string {
    41		if n.Value == nil {
    42			return "nil"
    43		}
    44		b, err := json.Marshal(n.Value)
    45		if err != nil {
    46			panic(err)
    47		}
    48		return string(b)
    49	}
    50	
    51	func (n *UnaryNode) String() string {
    52		op := n.Operator
    53		if n.Operator == "not" {
    54			op = fmt.Sprintf("%s ", n.Operator)
    55		}
    56		wrap := false
    57		switch b := n.Node.(type) {
    58		case *BinaryNode:
    59			if operator.Binary[b.Operator].Precedence <
    60				operator.Unary[n.Operator].Precedence {
    61				wrap = true
    62			}
    63		case *ConditionalNode:
    64			wrap = true
    65		}
    66		if wrap {
    67			return fmt.Sprintf("%s(%s)", op, n.Node.String())
    68		}
    69		return fmt.Sprintf("%s%s", op, n.Node.String())
    70	}
    71	
    72	func (n *BinaryNode) String() string {
    73		if n.Operator == ".." {
    74			return fmt.Sprintf("%s..%s", n.Left, n.Right)
    75		}
    76	
    77		var lhs, rhs string
    78		var lwrap, rwrap bool
    79	
    80		if l, ok := n.Left.(*UnaryNode); ok {
    81			if operator.Unary[l.Operator].Precedence <
    82				operator.Binary[n.Operator].Precedence {
    83				lwrap = true
    84			}
    85		}
    86		if lb, ok := n.Left.(*BinaryNode); ok {
    87			if operator.Less(lb.Operator, n.Operator) {
    88				lwrap = true
    89			}
    90			if operator.Binary[lb.Operator].Precedence ==
    91				operator.Binary[n.Operator].Precedence &&
    92				operator.Binary[n.Operator].Associativity == operator.Right {
    93				lwrap = true
    94			}
    95			if lb.Operator == "??" {
    96				lwrap = true
    97			}
    98			if operator.IsBoolean(lb.Operator) && n.Operator != lb.Operator {
    99				lwrap = true
   100			}
   101		}
   102		if rb, ok := n.Right.(*BinaryNode); ok {
   103			if operator.Less(rb.Operator, n.Operator) {
   104				rwrap = true
   105			}
   106			if operator.Binary[rb.Operator].Precedence ==
   107				operator.Binary[n.Operator].Precedence &&
   108				operator.Binary[n.Operator].Associativity == operator.Left {
   109				rwrap = true
   110			}
   111			if operator.IsBoolean(rb.Operator) && n.Operator != rb.Operator {
   112				rwrap = true
   113			}
   114		}
   115	
   116		if _, ok := n.Left.(*ConditionalNode); ok {
   117			lwrap = true
   118		}
   119		if _, ok := n.Right.(*ConditionalNode); ok {
   120			rwrap = true
   121		}
   122	
   123		if lwrap {
   124			lhs = fmt.Sprintf("(%s)", n.Left.String())
   125		} else {
   126			lhs = n.Left.String()
   127		}
   128	
   129		if rwrap {
   130			rhs = fmt.Sprintf("(%s)", n.Right.String())
   131		} else {
   132			rhs = n.Right.String()
   133		}
   134	
   135		return fmt.Sprintf("%s %s %s", lhs, n.Operator, rhs)
   136	}
   137	
   138	func (n *ChainNode) String() string {
   139		return n.Node.String()
   140	}
   141	
   142	func (n *MemberNode) String() string {
   143		node := n.Node.String()
   144		if _, ok := n.Node.(*BinaryNode); ok {
   145			node = fmt.Sprintf("(%s)", node)
   146		}
   147	
   148		if n.Optional {
   149			if str, ok := n.Property.(*StringNode); ok && utils.IsValidIdentifier(str.Value) {
   150				return fmt.Sprintf("%s?.%s", node, str.Value)
   151			} else {
   152				return fmt.Sprintf("%s?.[%s]", node, n.Property.String())
   153			}
   154		}
   155		if str, ok := n.Property.(*StringNode); ok && utils.IsValidIdentifier(str.Value) {
   156			if _, ok := n.Node.(*PointerNode); ok {
   157				return fmt.Sprintf(".%s", str.Value)
   158			}
   159			return fmt.Sprintf("%s.%s", node, str.Value)
   160		}
   161		return fmt.Sprintf("%s[%s]", node, n.Property.String())
   162	}
   163	
   164	func (n *SliceNode) String() string {
   165		if n.From == nil && n.To == nil {
   166			return fmt.Sprintf("%s[:]", n.Node.String())
   167		}
   168		if n.From == nil {
   169			return fmt.Sprintf("%s[:%s]", n.Node.String(), n.To.String())
   170		}
   171		if n.To == nil {
   172			return fmt.Sprintf("%s[%s:]", n.Node.String(), n.From.String())
   173		}
   174		return fmt.Sprintf("%s[%s:%s]", n.Node.String(), n.From.String(), n.To.String())
   175	}
   176	
   177	func (n *CallNode) String() string {
   178		arguments := make([]string, len(n.Arguments))
   179		for i, arg := range n.Arguments {
   180			arguments[i] = arg.String()
   181		}
   182		return fmt.Sprintf("%s(%s)", n.Callee.String(), strings.Join(arguments, ", "))
   183	}
   184	
   185	func (n *BuiltinNode) String() string {
   186		arguments := make([]string, len(n.Arguments))
   187		for i, arg := range n.Arguments {
   188			arguments[i] = arg.String()
   189		}
   190		return fmt.Sprintf("%s(%s)", n.Name, strings.Join(arguments, ", "))
   191	}
   192	
   193	func (n *PredicateNode) String() string {
   194		return n.Node.String()
   195	}
   196	
   197	func (n *PointerNode) String() string {
   198		return fmt.Sprintf("#%s", n.Name)
   199	}
   200

     1	package ast
     2	
     3	import (
     4		"fmt"
     5		"reflect"
     6		"regexp"
     7	)
     8	
     9	func Dump(node Node) string {
    10		return dump(reflect.ValueOf(node), "")
    11	}
    12	
    13	func dump(v reflect.Value, ident string) string {
    14		if !v.IsValid() {
    15			return "nil"
    16		}
    17		t := v.Type()
    18		switch t.Kind() {
    19		case reflect.Struct:
    20			out := t.Name() + "{\n"
    21			for i := 0; i < t.NumField(); i++ {
    22				f := t.Field(i)
    23				if isPrivate(f.Name) {
    24					continue
    25				}
    26				s := v.Field(i)
    27				out += fmt.Sprintf("%v%v: %v,\n", ident+"\t", f.Name, dump(s, ident+"\t"))
    28			}
    29			return out + ident + "}"
    30		case reflect.Slice:
    31			if v.Len() == 0 {
    32				return t.String() + "{}"
    33			}
    34			out := t.String() + "{\n"
    35			for i := 0; i < v.Len(); i++ {
    36				s := v.Index(i)
    37				out += fmt.Sprintf("%v%v,", ident+"\t", dump(s, ident+"\t"))
    38				if i+1 < v.Len() {
    39					out += "\n"
    40				}
    41			}
    42			return out + "\n" + ident + "}"
    43		case reflect.Ptr:
    44			return dump(v.Elem(), ident)
    45		case reflect.Interface:
    46			return dump(reflect.ValueOf(v.Interface()), ident)
    47	
    48		case reflect.String:
    49			return fmt.Sprintf("%q", v)
    50		default:
    51			return fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
    52		}
    53	}
    54	
    55	var isCapital = regexp.MustCompile("^[A-Z]")
    56	
    57	func isPrivate(s string) bool {
    58		return !isCapital.Match([]byte(s))
    59	}

   183	func (v *Checker) visit(node ast.Node) Nature {
   184		var nt Nature

   249	func (v *Checker) identifierNode(node *ast.IdentifierNode) Nature {
   250		for i := len(v.varScopes) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
   251			if v.varScopes[i].name == node.Value {
   252				return v.varScopes[i].nature
   253			}
   254		}
   255		if node.Value == "$env" {
   256			return Nature{}
   257		}
   258	
   259		return v.ident(node, node.Value, v.config.Strict, true)
   260	}

   262	// ident method returns type of environment variable, builtin or function.
   263	func (v *Checker) ident(node ast.Node, name string, strict, builtins bool) Nature {
   264		if nt, ok := v.config.Env.Get(&v.config.NtCache, name); ok {
   265			return nt
   266		}
   267		if builtins {
   268			if fn, ok := v.config.Functions[name]; ok {
   269				nt := v.config.NtCache.FromType(fn.Type())
   270				if nt.TypeData == nil {
   271					nt.TypeData = new(TypeData)
   272				}
   273				nt.TypeData.Func = fn
   274				return nt
   275			}
   276			if fn, ok := v.config.Builtins[name]; ok {
   277				nt := v.config.NtCache.FromType(fn.Type())
   278				if nt.TypeData == nil {
   279					nt.TypeData = new(TypeData)
   280				}
   281				nt.TypeData.Func = fn
   282				return nt
   283			}
   284		}
   285		if v.config.Strict && strict {
   286			return v.error(node, "unknown name %s", name)
   287		}
   288		return Nature{}
   289	}

   314			return v.error(node, "unknown operator (%s)", node.Operator)
   315		}
   316	
   317		return v.error(node, `invalid operation: %s (mismatched type %s)`, node.Operator, nt.String())
   318	}

   320	func (v *Checker) binaryNode(node *ast.BinaryNode) Nature {

   444				}

   455				return v.config.NtCache.FromType(boolType)

   500			}

   502	
   503		default:
   504			return v.error(node, "unknown operator (%s)", node.Operator)
   505	
   506		}
   507	
   508		return v.error(node, `invalid operation: %s (mismatched types %s and %s)`, node.Operator, l.String(), r.String())
   509	}

   778	func (c *compiler) CallNode(node *ast.CallNode) {
   779		fn := node.Callee.Type()
   780		if fn.Kind() == reflect.Func {
   781			fnInOffset := 0
   782			fnNumIn := fn.NumIn()
   783			switch callee := node.Callee.(type) {
   784			case *ast.MemberNode:
   785				if prop, ok := callee.Property.(*ast.StringNode); ok {
   786					if _, ok = callee.Node.Type().MethodByName(prop.Value); ok && callee.Node.Type().Kind() != reflect.Interface {
   787						fnInOffset = 1
   788						fnNumIn--
   789					}
   790				}
   791			case *ast.IdentifierNode:
   792				if t, ok := c.config.Env.MethodByName(c.ntCache, callee.Value); ok && t.Method {
   793					fnInOffset = 1
   794					fnNumIn--
   795				}
   796			}
   797			for i, arg := range node.Arguments {
   798				c.compile(arg)
   799	
   800				var in reflect.Type
   801				if fn.IsVariadic() && i >= fnNumIn-1 {
   802					in = fn.In(fn.NumIn() - 1).Elem()
   803				} else {
   804					in = fn.In(i + fnInOffset)
   805				}
   806	
   807				c.derefParam(in, arg)
   808			}
   809		} else {
   810			for _, arg := range node.Arguments {
   811				c.compile(arg)
   812			}
   813		}
   814	
   815		if ident, ok := node.Callee.(*ast.IdentifierNode); ok {
   816			if c.config != nil {
   817				if fn, ok := c.config.Functions[ident.Value]; ok {
   818					c.emitFunction(fn, len(node.Arguments))
   819					return
   820				}
   821			}
   822		}
   823		c.compile(node.Callee)
   824	
   825		if c.config != nil {
   826			isMethod, _, _ := checker.MethodIndex(c.ntCache, c.config.Env, node.Callee)
   827			if index, ok := checker.TypedFuncIndex(node.Callee.Type(), isMethod); ok {
   828				c.emit(OpCallTyped, index)
   829				return
   830			} else if checker.IsFastFunc(node.Callee.Type(), isMethod) {
   831				c.emit(OpCallFast, len(node.Arguments))
   832			} else {
   833				c.emit(OpCall, len(node.Arguments))
   834			}
   835		} else {
   836			c.emit(OpCall, len(node.Arguments))
   837		}
   838	}
   839	
   840	func (c *compiler) BuiltinNode(node *ast.BuiltinNode) {
   841		switch node.Name {

   638	func (v *Checker) callNode(node *ast.CallNode) Nature {
   639		// Check if type was set on node (for example, by patcher)
   640		// and use node type instead of function return type.
   641		//
   642		// If node type is anyType, then we should use function
   643		// return type. For example, on error we return anyType
   644		// for a call `errCall().Method()` and method will be
   645		// evaluated on `anyType.Method()`, so return type will
   646		// be anyType `anyType.Method(): anyType`. Patcher can
   647		// fix `errCall()` to return proper type, so on second
   648		// checker pass we should replace anyType on method node
   649		// with new correct function return type.
   650		if typ := node.Type(); typ != nil && typ != anyType {
   651			return *node.Nature()
   652		}
   653	
   654		// $env is not callable.
   655		if id, ok := node.Callee.(*ast.IdentifierNode); ok && id.Value == "$env" {
   656			return v.error(node, "%s is not callable", v.config.Env.String())
   657		}
   658	
   659		nt := v.visit(node.Callee)
   660		if nt.IsUnknown(&v.config.NtCache) {
   661			return Nature{}
   662		}
   663	
   664		if nt.TypeData != nil && nt.TypeData.Func != nil {
   665			return v.checkFunction(nt.TypeData.Func, node, node.Arguments)
   666		}
   667	
   668		fnName := "function"
   669		if identifier, ok := node.Callee.(*ast.IdentifierNode); ok {
   670			fnName = identifier.Value
   671		}
   672		if member, ok := node.Callee.(*ast.MemberNode); ok {
   673			if name, ok := member.Property.(*ast.StringNode); ok {
   674				fnName = name.Value
   675			}
   676		}
   677	
   678		if nt.Nil {
   679			return v.error(node, "%v is nil; cannot call nil as function", fnName)
   680		}
   681	
   682		if nt.Kind == reflect.Func {
   683			outType, err := v.checkArguments(fnName, nt, node.Arguments, node)
   684			if err != nil {
   685				if v.err == nil {
   686					v.err = err
   687				}
   688				return Nature{}
   689			}
   690			return outType
   691		}
   692		return v.error(node, "%s is not callable", nt.String())
   693	}

  1263			}

  1268		v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]

  1283	func (v *Checker) conditionalNode(node *ast.ConditionalNode) Nature {

  1294			return t2
  1295		}

  1297			return t1
  1298		}
  1299		if t1.Nil && t2.Nil {
  1300			return v.config.NtCache.NatureOf(nil)
  1301		}
  1302		if t1.AssignableTo(t2) {
  1303			if t1.IsArray() && t2.IsArray() {
  1304				e1 := t1.Elem(&v.config.NtCache)
  1305				e2 := t2.Elem(&v.config.NtCache)
  1306				if !e1.AssignableTo(e2) || !e2.AssignableTo(e1) {
  1307					return v.config.NtCache.FromType(arrayType)
  1308				}
  1309			}
  1310			return t1
  1311		}
  1312		return Nature{}
  1313	}

  1315	func (v *Checker) arrayNode(node *ast.ArrayNode) Nature {

  1317		allElementsAreSameType := true
  1318		for i, node := range node.Nodes {

  1321				if curr.Kind != prev.Kind {
  1322					allElementsAreSameType = false
  1323				}
  1324			}
  1325			prev = curr
  1326		}

  1330		return v.config.NtCache.FromType(arrayType)
  1331	}

REASONING_CONTENT
Now I have a good understanding. Let me plan the implementation in detail.

## Detailed Implementation Plan

### 1. New AST nodes in `ast/node.go`

```go
// TryNode represents a try/catch/finally expression.
type TryNode struct {
    base
    Try     Node
    Catch   Node
    Finally Node
}

// CatchNode represents a catch clause.
type CatchNode struct {
    base
    Name string
    Is   Node
    Node Node
}

// FinallyNode represents a finally clause.
type FinallyNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}

// ThrowNode represents a throw(value) expression.
type ThrowNode struct {
    base
    Node Node
}

// RetryNode represents a retry expression.
type RetryNode struct {
    base
}
```

### 2. AST visitor (`ast/visitor.go`)
Add cases:
```go
case *TryNode:
    Walk(&n.Try, v)
    if n.Catch != nil { Walk(&n.Catch, v) }
    if n.Finally != nil { Walk(&n.Finally, v) }
case *CatchNode:
    if n.Is != nil { Walk(&n.Is, v) }
    Walk(&n.Node, v)
case *FinallyNode:
    Walk(&n.Node, v)
case *ThrowNode:
    Walk(&n.Node, v)
case *RetryNode:
```

### 3. AST String methods (`ast/print.go`)
Add String() methods for these nodes.

### 4. Parser changes

In `parseSecondary`, handle `try`:
- When token is Identifier "try":
  - If next token is `{`: parse try block form.
  - If next token is `(`: parse `try(expr, fallback)` — it should require exactly two args. But note `parseCall` might handle `try` as a normal call;
I'll intercept before.
  - If `try` used as identifier in other contexts, e.g. `try` alone, error "unexpected token" or treat as identifier? It's probably reserved, so
error.

When token is Identifier "throw":
- If next is `(`: parse `throw(value)` — requires exactly one arg. But `throw` might also be a user function name? The spec says `throw(value)` throws
a custom error from any value, requires exactly one argument. I'll treat it as reserved when it looks like a call. To be safe, intercept `throw` as a
special function call.

When token is Identifier "errtype":
- If next is `(`: parse `errtype(err)` — requires exactly one argument. It's a function call but should always be treated as builtin.

When token is Identifier "retry":
- If used as an identifier (not followed by `(`), create `RetryNode`. Actually `retry` can also be a variable name? The spec says "retry - usable
inside catch blocks... Using retry outside a catch block raises a runtime error." So retry is a keyword. I'll create RetryNode when token is `retry`
and not followed by `(`. If followed by `(`, it might be a user function call named retry... but to be safe, treat `retry(...)` as a normal call
(user-defined). Actually, to be safe, only reserve `retry` as identifier. Let me create RetryNode always when identifier is `retry` and not followed
by `(`.

`catch` and `finally` are handled as part of try parsing, not as standalone expressions. If they appear alone, they'll become identifiers, which is
fine (or error at compile time for unknown name). Actually if someone writes `catch` as an identifier it would just be an identifier. I won't reserve
them.

Now parsing `try` block form:

```go
func (p *Parser) parseTry() Node {
    tryToken := p.current // "try"
    p.next() // skip "try"

    if p.current.Is(Bracket, "(") {
        // function form: try(expr, fallback)
        args := p.parseArguments(nil)
        if len(args) != 2 {
            p.error("try requires exactly two arguments")
            return nil
        }
        return p.createNode(&TryNode{
            Try: args[0],
            Catch: p.createNode(&CatchNode{Node: args[1]}, ...),
        }, tryToken.Location)
    }

    if !p.current.Is(Bracket, "{") {
        p.error("expected ( or { after try")
        return nil
    }
    p.expect(Bracket, "{")
    tryBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
    p.expect(Bracket, "}")

    var catchNode *CatchNode
    var finallyNode *FinallyNode

    if p.current.Is(Identifier, "catch") {
        p.next()
        catchNode = &CatchNode{}
        // optional catch name
        if p.current.Is(Identifier) {
            catchNode.Name = p.current.Value
            p.next()
            // optional "is <expr>"
            if p.current.Is(Operator, "is") {
                p.next()
                catchNode.Is = p.parseExpression(0)
            }
        }
        p.expect(Bracket, "{")
        catchNode.Node = p.parseSequenceExpression()
        p.expect(Bracket, "}")
        catchNode = p.createNode(catchNode, ...).(*CatchNode)
    }

    if p.current.Is(Identifier, "finally") {
        p.next()
        p.expect(Bracket, "{")
        finallyBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
        p.expect(Bracket, "}")
        finallyNode = p.createNode(&FinallyNode{Node: finallyBody}, ...).(*FinallyNode)
    }

    return p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: tryBody, Catch: catchNode, Finally: finallyNode}, tryToken.Location)
}
```

package utils

import (
	"unicode"
	"unicode/utf8"
)

func IsValidIdentifier(str string) bool {
	if len(str) == 0 {
		return false
	}
	h, w := utf8.DecodeRuneInString(str)
	if !IsAlphabetic(h) {
		return false
	}
	for _, r := range str[w:] {
		if !IsAlphaNumeric(r) {
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

func IsSpace(r rune) bool {
	return unicode.IsSpace(r)
}

func IsAlphaNumeric(r rune) bool {
	return IsAlphabetic(r) || unicode.IsDigit(r)
}

func IsAlphabetic(r rune) bool {
	return r == '_' || r == '$' || unicode.IsLetter(r)
}

The VM executes in a single loop with a handler stack. Design:

```

```

then re-throw the original error.

#### Alternative: Compile finally by direct code duplication + use exception mechanism

Maybe a cleaner approach: implement try/catch using Go-style `defer`-like semantics entirely with bytecode. Let me design:

Layout for `try { T } catch e { C } finally { F }`:

```
    OpTry catchAddr
    <T>
    OpJump finallyEntry      ; success path -> finally
catchAddr:
    OpPushError             ; push caught error
    OpStore e               ; bind
    OpCatch                 ; pop handler frame (mark handled)
    <C>
finallyEntry:
    <F>
    OpEndTry
```

For uncaught error with finally (no catch or catch test fails): We need to run F then rethrow. I'll implement this in the VM's error handling: when an
error is caught and the handler is found:
- If the frame has a finally block and (no catch or catch test mismatch), then instead of jumping to catchIP, set `frame.pendingError = err`, set
`frame.mode = "finally"`, restore stack, and jump to `frame.finallyIP`. Then after F executes, `OpEndTry` sees `pendingError != nil` and re-panics
with it.
- If the frame has a catch and matches, restore stack, push error, set `frame.mode = "catch"` (so errors inside catch propagate outward, i.e., don't
get caught by this same frame), jump to catchIP.
- If no catch and no finally, just re-panic (propagate to outer frame/out).

Now for the success path and normal catch path, after F executes, `OpEndTry` pops the frame normally.

But wait: on success path, the frame is still on the tryStack (we haven't popped it). On catch path, the frame is still on the tryStack too
(mode="catch"). So `OpEndTry` pops it in both cases. For uncaught-error path, after F, `OpEndTry` sees pendingError, re-panics. But re-panicking
inside `OpEndTry` means the outer handler (if any) catches it. Good. But what if F itself throws? Then the VM's error handling looks at the tryStack;
the current frame is still on top (mode="finally"), so if we don't skip it, F's error would be caught by... the same frame's catch? But we're in
finally; per spec, "if the finally body throws, that error propagates (overriding any prior result)". So errors in finally should propagate outward,
not be caught by the same try's catch. So when entering finally (mode="finally"), the frame should be inactive for catching. And if F throws while
mode="finally", the VM should pop this frame and rethrow to outer. So in the VM error handler, when we find a frame in mode "finally", we should pop
it (and all frames above it?) and continue searching outward. But also, the finally might belong to an inner try; if an error occurs in the inner
finally, the outer finally should still run (if any). Hmm.

This is essentially exception handling with finally. Let me think again.

Actually, for our purposes, we may not need to handle errors inside finally with nested try/finally perfectly. But the spec says "if the finally body
throws, that error propagates (overriding any prior result)." So a finally body that throws must propagate outward, i.e., to an outer try/catch or out
of the program. It should NOT be caught by the same try's catch (since that would create weird loops).

Let me define frame modes:
- `modeNormal`: try body executing; errors are catchable.
- `modeCatch`: catch body executing; errors are NOT caught by this frame (propagate outward).
- `modeFinally`: finally body executing; errors are NOT caught by this frame (propagate outward), and any pending error is overridden.

When the VM's error handler processes a panic:
- Search the tryStack from top down.
- If frame.mode != "normal", skip it (but keep it on stack so its finally still runs? Wait, if we're in an inner catch body and it throws, the inner
try's finally (if any) should still run. If we skip the inner frame, how does its finally run? We need to handle that.)

Hmm. This is getting into full exception semantics. Let me simplify with a design where:
- When an error propagates and there's a finally that needs to run, we "unwind" to that finally. Nested finallys should also run in sequence.

For example:
```
try {           // outer, with finally Fout
  try {         // inner, with finally Fin
    throw(1)
  } catch e {
    throw(2)    // catch body throws
  }
} catch e { ... } finally { Fout }
```
Semantics: inner catch throws 2; inner finally Fin should run; then outer catch catches 2? Actually the inner try's finally is associated with inner
try/catch, so when inner catch body throws, the inner finally should run, and then 2 propagates to outer. So we need to run Fin and then continue
unwinding.

This requires unwinding multiple frames and running their finallys. That's complex but doable.

However, I suspect the actual test cases for this task are simpler. But to be robust, I should implement proper semantics.

Let me design a proper exception mechanism:

VM has `tryStack []tryFrame`. Each frame:
```go
type tryFrame struct {
    catchIP   int
    stackSize int
    finallyIP int
    endIP     int   // where to continue after finally completes (for normal flow, this is the instruction after OpEndTry; for uncaught error path, we
rethrow instead)
    retries   int
    state     uint8 // 0=normal, 1=catch, 2=finally
}
```

Opcodes:
- `OpTry`: push frame. catchIP/finallyIP/endIP are set from compiler? Actually each OpTry instruction is specific to one try block. I can encode
catchIP, finallyIP, endIP as arguments (multiple arguments? The opcode has only one `arg int`). I'll need to encode the addresses. Options:
  1. Emit `OpTry` with no meaningful arg and store a constant struct with addresses in program.Constants. `OpTry` arg = constant index of a `*tryInfo`
struct containing catchIP, finallyIP, endIP, stackSize. But stackSize is runtime (stack depth), can't be in constant. So constant has static IPs.
  2. Use multiple opcodes to set up. E.g., `OpTry` arg=finallyIP? and `OpCatch` etc.

Let me use a constant `*runtime.TryInfo`:
```go
type TryInfo struct {
    CatchIP  int
    FinallyIP int
    EndIP    int
}
```
Compiler emits `OpPush` tryInfo constant then `OpTry`? That pushes extra value on stack which is awkward. Better: `OpTry` arg = constant index of
TryInfo. VM does `vm.tryStack = append(vm.tryStack, tryFrame{...program.Constants[arg].(*TryInfo)})`.

Let me define `TryInfo` in vm package (not runtime, since it references bytecode addresses; can be in vm).

Then:
- `OpTry <constIdx>`: pushes frame with static info from constant and current stack size, state=normal.
- `OpEndTry <constIdx?>`: The end of the try/catch/finally region. It pops the top frame (should match). If frame.pendingError != nil, re-panic. If
frame was in finally mode due to uncaught error, this rethrows.
- `OpCatch`: called at the start of catch body. Sets frame.state = catch. (The frame stays on stack so OpEndTry pops it.)
- `OpFinally`: called at start of finally body. Sets frame.state = finally.
- `OpPushError`: pushes the caught error onto stack (when entering catch).
- `OpThrow`: panic with a runtime.Error from the top of stack.
- `OpRetry`: re-executes the try body.
- `OpErrType`: pops an error and pushes its type string.

Now the VM error handler:

```go
func (vm *VM) handlePanic(r any, program *Program) (handled bool) {
    // r is the panic value. Convert to runtime error if not already.
    // Search from top of tryStack.
    for i := len(vm.tryStack) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
        f := &vm.tryStack[i]
        switch f.state {
        case stateFinally:
            // An error in finally propagates outward, overriding pending error.
            // But before propagating, we need to unwind: if there are outer frames
            // that need finally, they'll be handled by continuing the search.
            // Pop this frame? Actually leave it until OpEndTry? No - if error in finally,
            // we won't reach OpEndTry normally; we'll jump to outer handler. But then
            // the frame stays on stack. We need to pop it.
            // Let's pop it and continue searching upward (for outer finally/handler).
            vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]  // remove this frame (and any above? none above because this is top-most active)
            continue
        case stateCatch:
            // Error in catch body propagates outward; pop this frame and continue.
            vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
            continue
        case stateNormal:
            // Error in try body. Handle catch/finally.
            // If there's a finally, we need to run it before propagating uncaught errors.
            if f.finallyIP >= 0 {
                // jump to finally with pending error
                f.pendingError = r
                f.state = stateFinally
                vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize] // restore stack
                vm.ip = f.finallyIP
                return true
            }
            if f.catchIP >= 0 {
                // jump to catch
                f.state = stateCatch
                vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
                vm.push(r)  // push error
                vm.ip = f.catchIP
                return true
            }
            // no catch, no finally: pop frame and continue searching outward
            vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
            continue
        }
    }
    // no handler found
    return false
}
```

Wait, but if there's both a catch and a finally, and the error matches the catch, we should run catch then finally. The above does: jump to catch,
state=catch. After catch body, normal flow continues to finally (compiled after catch). The compiler emits `OpFinally` at start of finally, setting
state=finally. Then after finally, `OpEndTry` pops the frame. Good.

If the error doesn't match the catch (substring mismatch), then we should skip catch and run finally, then rethrow. The VM's error handler doesn't
know about substring matching. So I need to handle the substring test inside the catch block. Approach: the catch block begins with `OpPushError`,
then `OpStore e`, then if there's a substring test, compile:
```
OpPushError        ; push error
OpStore e          ; bind name (if any)
<compile test expression>  ; e.g. "substring"
OpCallContains? or use a builtin contains
OpJumpIfFalse <noMatchAddr>
<catch body>
OpJump <afterCatch>
noMatchAddr:
OpThrow             ; rethrow the error (need it on stack)
```
For rethrow, I need the original error on the stack. I can push it via `OpLoadVar e` (if named) or keep a copy. Since I store it in variable e, I can
re-load it and `OpThrow`. If no catch name, I still need a way to rethrow. I can store it in a temp variable even without a name, or push it twice:
`OpPushError` then `OpDup`? There's no dup opcode. I'll store it in a hidden variable always when there's a substring test. Actually for simplicity,
when there's a `is` test, always bind to a variable (even if no name), then `OpLoadVar` to rethrow. Let me always allocate a variable for the caught
error when the catch block is entered; if `Name` is non-empty, use that name; otherwise allocate a hidden variable for internal use.

But wait, the substring matching: `catch <name> is "substring"` — "catches only errors whose message contains the substring". The test expression is a
string constant usually. I'll evaluate it and check `strings.Contains(errorMessage, substring)`.

How to implement the contains check in bytecode? I can compile a call to a builtin `contains` function or use `OpContains` opcode directly.
`OpContains` expects `a, b` on stack and does `strings.Contains(a.(string), b.(string))`. But the error message needs to be converted to string. If
the error is a runtime.Error, I can use its `Error()` message. But `OpContains` would do `a.(string)` and panic if a isn't string. I need the error's
string conversion.

Simplest: implement the substring check in the VM via a new opcode `OpCatchTest`? Or push a string and use a helper. Let me add a new opcode
`OpErrContains`? Actually I can make `OpPushError` push the error as a *string* (its message) onto the stack, not the error value. But then `catch e {
errtype(e) }` wouldn't work because e would be a string. Hmm.

The spec says `catch <name>` binds the error, and `errtype(err)` classifies it. So the bound value should be the error object, not just the message.
But `errtype(e)` needs the error object.

For the `is` test, I can do: after binding `e` (error object), evaluate test expression, then call a special function that takes (error, substring)
and returns bool. Or add an opcode `OpErrIs` that pops (substring, error) and checks. Let me add `OpErrContains` or handle it in the VM.

Actually, let me reconsider. Maybe the `is` test expression is not necessarily a substring constant; it might be any expression yielding a string. The
catch body test should be `strings.Contains(err.Error(), testString)`. I'll implement with a new opcode `OpErrContains` that takes two stack operands:
substring on top, error below, and pushes bool. But to match the compiler pattern, easier: compile the test expression, then push the error (again)
and emit `OpErrContains`. Wait order: `OpPushError` pushes error; `OpStore e` removes it. Then compile test expression (pushes substring). Then I need
the error again. If named, `OpLoadVar e`; if not named, I stored it in a hidden var. So sequence:
```
OpPushError
OpStore errVar           ; binds e (or hidden var)
<compile test expr>      ; -> substring
OpLoadVar errVar         ; -> error
OpErrContains            ; pops error, substring; pushes bool
OpJumpIfFalse noMatch
<catch body>
OpJump afterCatch
noMatch:
OpLoadVar errVar
OpThrow
afterCatch:
```
`OpErrContains`: `sub := vm.pop(); err := vm.pop(); vm.push(strings.Contains(errString(err), subString))`. Need to stringify the error. For
runtime.Error, use .Error(). For other panic values (strings), use fmt.Sprintf.

Actually, maybe simpler to make the substring test expression itself handle it via a function call. But let me keep the opcode approach.

Wait, but what about `catch <name> is "substring"` where the name is required? If there's an `is` test, there's always a name (per syntax). So I'll
always have errVar available. Good.

Now, the retry mechanism. `retry` inside catch: re-executes try body. Automatic limit of three retries before raising a distinct exhaustion error.

How to implement retry:
- The try frame has a `retries` counter.
- When `OpRetry` executes, it should:
  1. Increment frame.retries.
  2. If retries > 3 (or >= 3?), throw a retry exhaustion error (type "retry").
  3. Otherwise, restore stack to frame.stackSize, reset frame.state to normal, and jump back to the start of the try body.
- Need the try body start IP in the frame.

But `retry` inside catch: catch body has popped... wait, in my design, the frame stays on the stack during catch body (state=catch). So `OpRetry` can
find the frame. Good.

But there's a subtlety: `retry` might be inside a nested catch (catch within catch). The `retry` should refer to the innermost enclosing try/catch.
Since the frame is on the stack, `OpRetry` uses the topmost frame. But if the catch body contains another try/catch, the topmost frame is the inner
one; `retry` inside the inner catch should retry the inner try. That's correct.

However, when we retry, we must also handle finally semantics: if the first attempt threw and we go to catch, and catch calls retry, should the
finally run before retrying? Per spec, "retry - usable inside catch blocks, re-executes the try body". Typically finally should run before retrying
(like a loop iteration). Hmm, but the spec doesn't explicitly say. Let me think: `try { risky() } catch { retry } finally { cleanup() }`. Each retry
re-executes try body. Does finally run on each attempt? I'd say yes — finally always executes after try/catch completes. But when retry causes
re-execution, the catch hasn't "completed" normally; it's a control transfer. In many languages, `continue`/`retry` would run finally? Not
necessarily.

The spec only says finally "optional clause that always executes after try/catch". I think simplest semantics: retry jumps back to try body start,
resetting state to normal, without running finally. The retries are tracked in the frame. When all retries exhausted (3 retries), a distinct
exhaustion error is raised.

But wait: "automatic limit of three retries before raising a distinct exhaustion error". This suggests each retry increments a counter; if the try
body keeps throwing and catch keeps calling retry, after 3 retries total, instead of retrying the 4th time, raise exhaustion error. So:
- Attempt 1 (initial). Error -> catch. retry: retries=1, jump to try.
- Attempt 2. Error -> catch. retry: retries=2, jump to try.
- Attempt 3. Error -> catch. retry: retries=3, jump to try.
- Attempt 4. Error -> catch. retry: retries would become 4 > 3, so raise exhaustion error.

Or: "automatic limit of three retries" means at most 3 retries (total 4 attempts). I'll implement: when `OpRetry` is called and `frame.retries >= 3`,
throw retry exhaustion error; otherwise increment and jump. So attempts = 1 + 3 = 4 max.

What's the "distinct exhaustion error" message? I'll use something like `"retry limit exceeded"` or `"retry: maximum number of retries exceeded"`. And
its type is "retry". Let me define `runtime.Throw(runtime.RetryError, "retry limit exceeded")` or similar.

Now, where does the retry error surface? If inside catch, `retry` throws the exhaustion error (with type retry). That error propagates out of the
catch (since state=catch) and could be caught by an outer try. If no outer catch, it propagates out of the program. `errtype(err)` would return
"retry". Good.

But wait: the exhaustion error should probably not be catchable by the same catch (since retry is called inside catch and we're in state=catch, it
propagates outward). Good.

Now `throw(value)`:
- Compiles value expression, then `OpThrow`.
- `OpThrow` pops value, stringifies it, panics with a runtime.Error of type CustomError and message = string conversion.
- "throw(value) - throws a custom error from any value (the error message is its string conversion)".
- `throw` can be used anywhere (not just in try). If not caught, it propagates out and `Run` returns it as a file.Error.

Now `errtype(err)`:
- Takes exactly one argument. Returns:
  - `"index"` for out-of-range/bounds
  - `"conversion"` for type-conversion failures
  - `"type"` for type-mismatch/assertion errors
  - `"nil"` for nil-pointer/reference errors
  - `"retry"` for retry-exhaustion errors
  - `"custom"` for all other errors including those from throw
  - `"none"` when the input is nil.
- Implement as a builtin function or an opcode. Since it's a special function taking any, I'll implement as a builtin in `builtin` package? But it
needs access to runtime error types. Could be in vm. Simpler: treat `errtype` as a BuiltinNode in parser, and add a builtin function in
`builtin/builtin.go` that classifies. But the builtin package is separate from vm/runtime. I can have the builtin call `runtime.ErrType(err)`.

Actually, the builtin functions are defined in `builtin` package. Let me check how builtins are defined and whether they can import vm/runtime.

package builtin

import (
	"reflect"
)

type Function struct {
	Name      string
	Fast      func(arg any) any
	Func      func(args ...any) (any, error)
	Safe      func(args ...any) (any, uint, error)
	Types     []reflect.Type
	Validate  func(args []reflect.Type) (reflect.Type, error)
	Deref     func(i int, arg reflect.Type) bool
	Predicate bool
}

func (f *Function) Type() reflect.Type {
	if len(f.Types) > 0 {
		return f.Types[0]
	}
	return reflect.TypeOf(f.Func)
}

   840	func (c *compiler) BuiltinNode(node *ast.BuiltinNode) {
   841		switch node.Name {
   842		case "all":
   843			c.compile(node.Arguments[0])
   844			c.derefInNeeded(node.Arguments[0])
   845			c.emit(OpBegin)
   846			var loopBreak int
   847			c.emitLoop(func() {
   848				c.compile(node.Arguments[1])
   849				loopBreak = c.emit(OpJumpIfFalse, placeholder)
   850				c.emit(OpPop)
   851			})
   852			c.emit(OpTrue)
   853			c.patchJump(loopBreak)
   854			c.emit(OpEnd)
   855			return
   856	
   857		case "none":
   858			c.compile(node.Arguments[0])
   859			c.derefInNeeded(node.Arguments[0])
   860			c.emit(OpBegin)
   861			var loopBreak int
   862			c.emitLoop(func() {
   863				c.compile(node.Arguments[1])
   864				c.emit(OpNot)
   865				loopBreak = c.emit(OpJumpIfFalse, placeholder)

package builtin

import (
	"fmt"
	"reflect"
	"time"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/internal/deref"
)

var (
	anyType      = reflect.TypeOf(new(any)).Elem()
	integerType  = reflect.TypeOf(0)
	floatType    = reflect.TypeOf(float64(0))
	arrayType    = reflect.TypeOf([]any{})
	mapType      = reflect.TypeOf(map[any]any{})
	timeType     = reflect.TypeOf(new(time.Time)).Elem()
	locationType = reflect.TypeOf(new(time.Location))
)

func kind(t reflect.Type) reflect.Kind {
	if t == nil {
		return reflect.Invalid
	}
	t = deref.Type(t)
	return t.Kind()
}

func types(types ...any) []reflect.Type {
	ts := make([]reflect.Type, len(types))
	for i, t := range types {
		t := reflect.TypeOf(t)
		if t.Kind() == reflect.Ptr {
			t = t.Elem()
		}
		if t.Kind() != reflect.Func {
			panic("not a function")
		}
		ts[i] = t
	}
	return ts
}

REASONING_CONTENT
I can add `errtype` as a builtin with `Types: types(new(func(any) string))`. The checker will use `checkFunction` with Types, so it will validate one
argument of any type and return string. The parser already creates BuiltinNode for `errtype` if I add it to builtin.Index. But I also need it to not
be shadowable? It can be a builtin.

Wait, the parser's `parseCall` checks `builtin.Index[token.Value]` to create a BuiltinNode. So if I add `errtype` to builtin.Builtins, the parser will
automatically create a BuiltinNode for `errtype(...)` and the checker/compiler will handle it. No special parser handling needed! Great. Similarly,
`throw` could be a builtin, but I want it to compile to `OpThrow` (which is different — it panics), so I'll handle `throw` specially in the
parser/compiler.

Actually, could `throw` be a builtin with a `Func` that panics? Yes! A builtin `Func func(args ...any) (any, error)` could panic with a runtime error.
Then `throw(1)` would compile as a normal builtin call, evaluate the argument, call the function which panics. That's simpler. But the builtin `Func`
is invoked via `OpCall0/1/2/3` which panics on error return. If the function itself panics, the VM recover catches it. That works fine. And the
compiler's `emitFunction` will handle it. So `throw` can be a builtin too! Same for `retry`? No, retry needs access to VM state (try stack), so it
must be an opcode.

Let me think: `throw(value)` as builtin:
```go
{
    Name: "throw",
    Types: types(new(func(any) any)),
    Func: func(args ...any) (any, error) {
        runtime.Throw(runtime.CustomError, fmt.Sprintf("%v", args[0]))
        return nil, nil
    },
}
```
The checker will accept one arg of any type and return any. The compiler compiles it as a function call. The function panics with `runtime.Error`.
Great.

But wait — the spec says "throw(value) - throws a custom error from any value (the error message is its string conversion); requires exactly one
argument." If someone overrides `throw` via env or config, should it be overridable? The spec implies it's a language feature. I'll add it as a
builtin; users could theoretically override via config.Functions. That's probably fine. But to be safe, I could make it non-overridable by
special-casing in parser (like predicates). Hmm. The existing parser checks `isOverridden` for builtins. If the user defines `throw` in env, it would
override. That's acceptable behavior.

Actually, there's a subtlety with `throw` as a builtin: the VM's `OpThrow` currently expects `vm.pop().(error)`. If I make throw a builtin function
that panics with runtime.Error, the existing OpThrow opcode becomes unused for throw() — but I might still use it for the catch substring mismatch
rethrow. Let me keep OpThrow and implement it to take any value and panic with runtime.Error (custom type). For the builtin throw, I can use the same
runtime.Throw function directly.

Now `retry` — it needs VM access. I'll implement as a special AST node `RetryNode` compiled to `OpRetry`.

`errtype` — builtin.

Now, the runtime error classification. I need to convert existing panics in the runtime package (and VM) into typed errors. This is the bulk of the
work:
- `runtime.Fetch` index out of range -> IndexError.
- `runtime.ToInt`, `ToInt64`, `ToFloat64`, `ToBool` invalid operation -> ConversionError.
- `runtime.Add`, etc. type mismatch -> TypeError.
- nil pointer dereference / "cannot get field from nil" -> NilError.
- `throw` -> CustomError.
- retry exhaustion -> RetryError.
- all other panics (e.g., calling non-function, invalid opcode, stack underflow) -> CustomError (default).

The issue says runtime errors currently cause unrecoverable panics; we need to catch them. The current VM Run recovers and returns a file.Error. So
panics are not truly "unrecoverable" at the Go level — they're converted to errors. But the expression language has no `try/catch`. The "unrecoverable
panics" refers to within the language: any runtime error aborts the whole expression; there's no way to handle them.

The key requirement: try/catch must catch these runtime errors. So when an error occurs inside a try body, the VM must route it to the catch handler
rather than propagating to the top.

For this, the panic values need to be typed so `errtype` can classify them. I'll introduce `runtime.Error` and change all runtime panics to use
`runtime.Throw(Type, ...)`.

Let me design `runtime/error.go`:

```go
package runtime

import (
    "fmt"
    "reflect"
)

type Type int

const (
    IndexError Type = iota
    ConversionError
    TypeError
    NilError
    RetryError
    CustomError
)

func (t Type) String() string {
    switch t {
    case IndexError: return "index"
    case ConversionError: return "conversion"
    case TypeError: return "type"
    case NilError: return "nil"
    case RetryError: return "retry"
    case CustomError: return "custom"
    default: return "unknown"
    }
}

type Error struct {
    Type    Type
    Message string
    Err     error
}

func (e *Error) Error() string {
    if e.Message != "" {
        return e.Message
    }
    if e.Err != nil {
        return e.Err.Error()
    }
    return "runtime error"
}

func (e *Error) Unwrap() error { return e.Err }

func NewError(t Type, format string, args ...any) *Error {
    return &Error{Type: t, Message: fmt.Sprintf(format, args...)}
}

func Throw(t Type, format string, args ...any) {
    panic(NewError(t, format, args...))
}

func ErrType(v any) string {
    if v == nil {
        return "none"
    }
    if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
        return e.Type.String()
    }
    // Existing Go errors or other panic values.
    return "custom"
}
```

Wait, but existing Go code panics with `string` (e.g. `panic(fmt.Sprintf(...))`) and with `error` (e.g. `panic(err)`). To catch and classify these, I
need to convert them when they're caught by the VM. I'll add a normalization function:

```go
func NormalizePanic(v any) *Error {
    if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
        return e
    }
    if err, ok := v.(error); ok {
        return &Error{Type: CustomError, Message: err.Error(), Err: err}
    }
    return &Error{Type: CustomError, Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)}
}
```

But to get accurate classification, I should replace the actual `panic(fmt.Sprintf(...))` calls in runtime with `runtime.Throw(...)` so they get
proper types. I'll do that for the important ones.

Now, what about panics from within user-provided Go functions (via OpCall)? When a user function returns `(val, err)` and err != nil, the VM panics
with `err`. When caught, NormalizePanic classifies as CustomError. If the user function itself panics with some value, also CustomError. Good.

Now the `errtype` classification:
- `"index"` for out-of-range/bounds errors -> runtime.IndexError.
- `"conversion"` for type-conversion failures -> runtime.ConversionError.
- `"type"` for type-mismatch/assertion errors -> runtime.TypeError.
- `"nil"` for nil-pointer/reference errors -> runtime.NilError.
- `"retry"` for retry-exhaustion errors -> runtime.RetryError.
- `"custom"` for all other errors including those from throw -> runtime.CustomError.
- `"none"` when input is nil.

Good. I can update both the generator template and the generated file. Since the generator is a template, I'll update the template so `go generate`
produces the same. I'll replace the panic lines in the template and generated file with `Throw(TypeError, ...)`.

Now let me think about the VM's Run function and how to integrate try/catch. I'll restructure Run to:
1. Keep the outer recover for converting uncaught errors to file.Error.
2. Add an internal mechanism for catch handling.

The cleanest way: extract the main execution loop into a helper method `vm.run(program, env)` and have a recover inside it that consults
`vm.tryStack`. If a handler handles the error, continue the loop; otherwise re-panic to the outer recover.

Let me design `run`:

```go
func (vm *VM) Run(program *Program, env any) (_ any, err error) {
    defer func() {
        if r := recover(); r != nil {
            e := runtime.NormalizePanic(r)
            var location file.Location
            if vm.ip-1 < len(program.locations) {
                location = program.locations[vm.ip-1]
            }
            f := &file.Error{
                Location: location,
                Message:  e.Error(),
            }
            f.Wrap(e)
            err = f.Bind(program.source)
        }
    }()
    // reset state
    ...
    vm.run(program, env)
    // return stack
}
```

And `vm.run(program, env)`:
```go
func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
    defer func() {
        if r := recover(); r != nil {
            if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
                // continue executing from new ip
                vm.run(program, env)  // recursion?
                return
            }
            panic(r)
        }
    }()
    for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) { ... }
}
```

Recursion on every caught error could be deep (each retry/catch). Better to use a loop with a flag. But recursion is simpler and depth is bounded by
number of errors (retries could be 3 per frame). Actually errors can be many (nested try with many catches). But each catch handling recursion is fine
— depth bounded by program structure. Let me instead write a loop:

```go
func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
    for {
        func() {
            defer func() {
                if r := recover(); r != nil {
                    if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
                        // handled: continue outer loop
                        return
                    }
                    panic(r)
                }
            }()
            vm.execute(program, env)
        }()
        if vm.done { break }
    }
}
```

Hmm, but `execute` needs to signal completion. Let me use a simpler pattern: the main loop function returns only when ip >= len(bytecode). If an error
is handled, it sets vm.ip to a new value and we need to restart the loop. I'll use a boolean flag:

```go
func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
    for {
        handled := false
        func() {
            defer func() {
                if r := recover(); r != nil {
                    if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
                        handled = true
                        return
                    }
                    panic(r)
                }
            }()
            vm.executeLoop(program, env)
        }()
        if !handled {
            return
        }
    }
}
```

Where `executeLoop` runs the for loop until ip >= len(bytecode). When an error is handled, the deferred recover sets handled=true, and `executeLoop`'s
loop was interrupted by the panic. Then we loop again, re-running executeLoop from the new vm.ip. Good.

But there's a subtlety: the `fnArgsBuf` is local to Run currently. If I move the loop to a helper, fnArgsBuf must be shared across restarts. I'll keep
it in Run and pass it, or make it a field. Let me make it a local in Run and have `executeLoop` take it as parameter and return the new buffer (like
it does currently). Actually the current code has `var fnArgsBuf []any` in Run. Since `executeLoop` may be restarted after a catch, and fnArgsBuf is
used across calls, I'll pass a pointer or return it. Let me make executeLoop return the fnArgsBuf.

Now, `handlePanic`:

```go
func (vm *VM) handlePanic(r any, program *Program) bool {
    for i := len(vm.tryStack) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
        f := &vm.tryStack[i]
        switch f.state {
        case stateCatch, stateFinally:
            // Errors in catch/finally propagate outward, but before unwinding
            // we should run any finally blocks belonging to outer frames?
            // Actually we are at frame i, and frames above i... wait, we search from top,
            // so there are no frames above i that are active. Frames below are outer.
            // Pop this frame and continue outward.
            vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
            continue
        case stateNormal:
            if f.finallyIP >= 0 {
                f.pendingError = r
                f.state = stateFinally
                vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
                vm.ip = f.finallyIP
                return true
            }
            if f.catchIP >= 0 {
                f.state = stateCatch
                vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
                vm.push(runtime.NormalizePanic(r))
                vm.ip = f.catchIP
                return true
            }
            vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
            continue
        }
    }
    return false
}
```

Wait, there's a bug: in the `stateCatch`/`stateFinally` cases, I pop frame i but there might be frames above i? No — since we iterate from top, at the
point we examine frame i, all frames above i (indices > i) have already been examined and either handled (returned true) or popped. In the
stateCatch/stateFinally case, we pop frame i and continue to i-1 (outer). But what about an inner frame above i that was in stateNormal and had a
finally? If an error occurs inside an inner catch, the inner frame is in stateCatch (we're inside its catch body). We pop it. But the inner frame's
finally — should it run? Yes, if the inner try had a finally, it should run when the inner catch throws. But by popping the inner frame without
running its finally, we skip it. That's a semantic gap.

Let me handle this: when an error occurs in a catch body (stateCatch) or finally body (stateFinally), and the frame has a finally that hasn't run yet,
we should run that finally before propagating. For stateCatch: the frame's finally may not have run yet (it runs after catch body). So we should set
state=finally, pendingError=r, jump to finallyIP. For stateFinally: the finally is already running; if it throws, we should override pending error and
propagate outward — pop frame and continue.

So:
```go
case stateCatch:
    if f.finallyIP >= 0 {
        f.pendingError = r
        f.state = stateFinally
        vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
        vm.ip = f.finallyIP
        return true
    }
    vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
    continue
case stateFinally:
    vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
    continue
```

Good. This handles nested finallys: if inner catch throws, we jump to inner finally. If inner finally throws, we pop inner frame and search outer;
outer frame (if stateNormal) will run its finally/catch. If outer is stateCatch, etc.

Now, what about `OpEndTry` semantics:
- When we reach OpEndTry normally (success or catch completed), the top frame should be in state normal (success path) or state catch (catch path) or
state finally (uncaught error path with pendingError).
- `OpEndTry`:
  - Pop the top frame.
  - If frame.pendingError != nil, re-panic with it.
  - Otherwise continue.
But wait, on the uncaught error path, when we jump to finally, the finally body executes and then OpEndTry pops frame and re-panics with pendingError.
Good.
- On success path: try body executes, jumps to finally, finally executes, OpEndTry pops (no pending error). Good.
- On catch path: catch body executes (state=catch), then falls into finally (state=finally), finally executes, OpEndTry pops (no pending error). Good.

But on success path, the frame state is still `normal` when OpEndTry is reached. On catch path, `OpFinally` sets state=finally before finally.
Actually I said I'd emit `OpFinally` at the start of the finally block to set state=finally. Let me define:
- `OpFinally`: sets top frame state = finally.
- `OpCatch`: sets top frame state = catch.

Now for the retry: `OpRetry`:
```go
case OpRetry:
    if len(vm.tryStack) == 0 {
        panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, "retry is not allowed outside catch block"))
    }
    f := &vm.tryStack[len(vm.tryStack)-1]
    if f.retries >= 3 {
        panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.RetryError, "retry limit exceeded"))
    }
    f.retries++
    f.state = stateNormal
    f.pendingError = nil
    vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
    vm.ip = f.tryIP
```

I need `tryIP` in the frame — the start of the try body. I'll store it in TryInfo.

But wait: if `retry` is inside a catch body, the top frame is that try's frame (state=catch). Good. If retry is used in a nested try's catch, top
frame is the nested one. Good.

What about retry used inside a finally? "usable inside catch blocks" — only in catch. In finally, top frame state is finally; I'll still allow retry?
Spec says usable inside catch blocks; using outside raises error. Finally is not catch, so it should raise an error. I'll check `f.state ==
stateCatch`; if not, panic with "retry is not allowed outside catch block". Actually simpler: just allow it in catch; if state != catch, error.

Now, what about `retry` used inside catch when there's no enclosing try (i.e., top frame is nil)? Error.

Now the `OpThrow` opcode: currently `panic(vm.pop().(error))`. I'll change it to:
```go
case OpThrow:
    v := vm.pop()
    if e, ok := v.(error); ok {
        panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, "%v", e)) // or keep the error type?
    }
    panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, "%v", v))
```
Wait, if the value is already a runtime.Error (e.g., rethrow from catch mismatch), we should preserve its type. `OpThrow` is used for `throw(value)`
(custom) and rethrow after substring mismatch (rethrow original error, which is a runtime.Error). So:
```go
case OpThrow:
    v := vm.pop()
    if e, ok := v.(*runtime.Error); ok {
        panic(e)
    }
    panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)))
```

Now, the `OpPushError` opcode: pushes the current caught error. But how does the VM know "current caught error"? The error was pushed onto the stack
by handlePanic when jumping to catch. Actually, in handlePanic, I do `vm.push(runtime.NormalizePanic(r))` and set ip=catchIP. So the error is already
on the stack when the catch block starts. Then `OpPushError` is redundant — the error is already on the stack!

Wait, but the compiler emits `OpPushError` at the start of catch to push the error, then `OpStore`. If the error is already on the stack from
handlePanic, then OpPushError would push a second error. Let me reconsider: I can have handlePanic NOT push the error, and instead let `OpPushError`
push it from a stored location. Or have handlePanic push and not emit OpPushError.

Simplest: handlePanic pushes the error onto the stack. The compiler emits catch block starting with `OpStore e` (if named) directly. But we also need
the error for the `is` test and for rethrow. If catch has no name but has an `is` test, we need a hidden variable. Let me design:

Catch block bytecode:
```
catchAddr:
   ; error is on stack
   [if named: OpStore e]
   [if has is-test:
        OpStore hiddenErrVar? -- but if named, error already stored; need it on stack for test too]
```

Let me restructure: always store the error to a variable first (either the named one or a hidden one), then for the `is` test, load it back.

```
catchAddr:
    OpStore errVar          ; error on stack -> store to var (named or hidden)
    <if is-test>
        <compile is expr>   ; -> substring
        OpLoadVar errVar    ; -> error
        OpErrContains       ; -> bool
        OpJumpIfFalse noMatch
        <catch body>
        OpJump end
    noMatch:
        OpLoadVar errVar
        OpThrow
    end:
```

But wait — if the catch is entered because an error was thrown, handlePanic pushed the error onto the stack and set ip=catchAddr. The first
instruction `OpStore errVar` consumes it. Good. For catch with no name and no is-test, we don't even need to store it; the catch body just executes
with no error binding, and the error value is discarded (it's on the stack and would need popping). Actually if we don't consume it, it'll remain on
the stack and mess up the catch body. So for unnamed catch without is-test, we should pop the error: `OpPop`.

Hmm, but actually the `catch { fallback }` from `try(expr, fallback)` — the error should be discarded, so `OpPop` at catch start. Let me compile:
- If catch.Name != "": emit OpStore var.
- Else if catch.Is != nil: emit OpStore hiddenVar.
- Else: emit OpPop.

For the `is` test with a named catch, we use the named var. With is-test but no name (is that allowed? Syntax requires name before is, so is-test
implies name), but I'll handle generally: if Is != nil, we need the error stored somewhere; if no name, use hidden var.

Now, when does the error get pushed? In handlePanic when jumping to catch. Good.

Now, what about the success path and the `OpJump` around catch? Compiler emits:
```
OpTry <tryInfoConst>          ; push frame
<compile try body>
OpJump toFinally              ; success path
catchAddr:
<catch block>
toFinally:
<compile finally if present>
OpEndTry
```

But wait — the catch block is entered by handlePanic which sets ip=catchAddr. If there's no catch (e.g., only finally), catchIP=-1 and there's no
catchAddr. Then handlePanic goes to finally path (if finally exists) or propagates.

For a try with only finally (no catch):
```
OpTry <info>       ; info has catchIP=-1, finallyIP=F
<try body>
OpJump toEnd
F:
OpFinally
<finally body>
toEnd:
OpEndTry
```
On error in try body: handlePanic sees finallyIP >= 0, sets pendingError, state=finally, jumps to F. Finally runs, OpEndTry pops frame and re-panics.
Good.

For a try with no catch and no finally — that's pointless (try { } without catch/finally). Does the parser allow `try {}`? Probably should allow but
it's useless. I'll allow it; it just executes the body (with a frame that catches nothing and immediately re-panics... actually if no catch/finally,
handlePanic pops frame and propagates, so behavior is same as without try). Fine.

Now the TryInfo constant:
```go
type TryInfo struct {
    CatchIP   int
    FinallyIP int
    EndIP     int
    TryIP     int
}
```
But these are bytecode addresses known at compile time. The compiler emits `OpTry` with arg = constant index of TryInfo. Since TryInfo is mutable? No,
it's immutable after compile. Good.

However, there's an issue: `OpTry` pushes a frame but the frame's stackSize must be set at runtime (current stack depth). That's fine.

Now, `OpRetry` needs `tryIP` from the frame. I'll store TryIP in TryInfo.

Now, the `OpCatch` opcode: emitted at the very start of the catch block? Actually handlePanic already sets state=catch when jumping to catch. So I
don't need OpCatch. Let me set state in handlePanic. But then if a panic occurs in the catch body, handlePanic will see state=catch and handle (run
finally/propagate). Good. So no OpCatch needed.

`OpFinally`: needed to set state=finally when entering finally via normal flow (success or catch). On the uncaught-error path, handlePanic already
sets state=finally before jumping. So I'll emit `OpFinally` at the start of the finally block to handle the normal-flow case. It just sets top frame
state=finally. Good.

Now, `OpEndTry`:
```go
case OpEndTry:
    if len(vm.tryStack) == 0 {
        panic("OpEndTry without OpTry")
    }
    f := vm.tryStack[len(vm.tryStack)-1]
    vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:len(vm.tryStack)-1]
    if f.pendingError != nil {
        panic(f.pendingError)
    }
```

Now, one subtle issue: when handlePanic restores the stack to `f.stackSize`, it discards any intermediate values. But what about scopes (`vm.Scopes`)?
If the try body started loops/begin scopes, those scopes might not be cleaned up on error. The `OpBegin`/`OpEnd` push/pop scopes. If an error occurs
inside a loop inside try, the scope stack would be left in a bad state. To be safe, I should also record `scopeSize` in the frame and restore it on
catch. Let me add `scopeSize int` to tryFrame and restore `vm.Scopes` to that length (and update currScope). Similarly for retry. This prevents stale
scopes from leaking into the catch handler.

Let me add:
```go
type tryFrame struct {
    info        TryInfo
    stackSize   int
    scopeSize   int
    retries     int
    state       byte
    pendingError any
}
```

On OpTry: frame.stackSize = len(vm.Stack); frame.scopeSize = len(vm.Scopes).

In handlePanic when jumping to catch/finally: restore both Stack and Scopes.

In OpRetry: restore both.

Now, `OpErrContains`:
```go
case OpErrContains:
    sub := vm.pop()
    err := vm.pop()
    msg := runtime.ErrorMessage(err)
    s, ok := sub.(string)
    if !ok {
        panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.TypeError, "invalid operation: catch is %T, expected string", sub))
    }
    vm.push(strings.Contains(msg, s))
```

Wait, order: compiler emits `<compile is expr>` (substring) then `OpLoadVar errVar` (error). So stack top is error, below is substring.
`OpErrContains` pops error (top), then substring. I wrote `sub := vm.pop()` then `err := vm.pop()` — that gives sub = error, err = substring, wrong.
Let me define order clearly: I'll emit `OpLoadVar errVar` first, then `<compile is expr>`, so stack is [error, substring], then OpErrContains pops
substring then error:
```
OpLoadVar errVar       ; error
<compile is expr>      ; substring
OpErrContains          ; pops substring, error -> bool
```
So in VM: `sub := vm.pop(); err := vm.pop(); vm.push(strings.Contains(errMsg(err), subString(sub)))`.

Now `errMsg` helper in runtime:
```go
func ErrorString(v any) string {
    if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
        return e.Error()
    }
    if e, ok := v.(error); ok {
        return e.Error()
    }
    return fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
}
```

Now, the runtime error classification for existing panics. Let me enumerate all panic sites and update:

- line 51: index out of range -> `Throw(IndexError, ...)`.
- line 101: cannot fetch (struct missing field or map?) -> `Throw(TypeError, ...)` — but if from is nil pointer, should be NilError. Since `Fetch`

f := &file.Error{Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", r)}
if err, ok := r.(error); ok { f.Wrap(err) }

package optimizer

import (
	"fmt"
	"reflect"

	. "github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/conf"
)

func Optimize(node *Node, config *conf.Config) error {
	Walk(node, &inArray{})
	for limit := 1000; limit >= 0; limit-- {
		fold := &fold{}
		Walk(node, fold)
		if fold.err != nil {
			return fold.err
		}
		if !fold.applied {
			break
		}
	}
	if config != nil && len(config.ConstFns) > 0 {
		for limit := 100; limit >= 0; limit-- {
			constExpr := &constExpr{
				fns: config.ConstFns,
			}
			Walk(node, constExpr)
			if constExpr.err != nil {
				return constExpr.err
			}
			if !constExpr.applied {
				break
			}
		}
	}
	Walk(node, &inRange{})
	Walk(node, &filterMap{})
	Walk(node, &filterLen{})
	Walk(node, &filterLast{})
	Walk(node, &filterFirst{})
	Walk(node, &predicateCombination{})
	Walk(node, &sumRange{})
	Walk(node, &sumArray{})
	Walk(node, &sumMap{})
	Walk(node, &countAny{})
	Walk(node, &countThreshold{})
	return nil
}

var (
	boolType    = reflect.TypeOf(true)
	integerType = reflect.TypeOf(0)
	floatType   = reflect.TypeOf(float64(0))
	stringType  = reflect.TypeOf("")
)

func patchWithType(node *Node, newNode Node) {
	switch n := newNode.(type) {
	case *BoolNode:
		newNode.SetType(boolType)
	case *IntegerNode:
		newNode.SetType(integerType)
	case *FloatNode:
		newNode.SetType(floatType)
	case *StringNode:
		newNode.SetType(stringType)
	case *ConstantNode:
		newNode.SetType(reflect.TypeOf(n.Value))
	case *BinaryNode:
		newNode.SetType(n.Type())
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("unknown type %T", newNode))
	}
	Patch(node, newNode)
}

func patchCopyType(node *Node, newNode Node) {
	t := (*node).Type()
	newNode.SetType(t)
	Patch(node, newNode)
}
