package optimizer

import (
	"math"

	. "github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/file"
)

type fold struct {
	applied bool
	err     *file.Error
}

func (fold *fold) Visit(node *Node) {
	patch := func(newNode Node) {
		fold.applied = true
		patchWithType(node, newNode)
	}
	patchCopy := func(newNode Node) {
		fold.applied = true
		patchCopyType(node, newNode)
	}

	switch n := (*node).(type) {
	case *UnaryNode:
		switch n.Operator {
		case "-":
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*IntegerNode); ok {
				patch(&IntegerNode{Value: -i.Value})
			}
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*FloatNode); ok {
				patch(&FloatNode{Value: -i.Value})
			}
		case "+":
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*IntegerNode); ok {
				patch(&IntegerNode{Value: i.Value})
			}
			if i, ok := n.Node.(*FloatNode); ok {
				patch(&FloatNode{Value: i.Value})
			}
		case "!", "not":
			if a := toBool(n.Node); a != nil {
				patch(&BoolNode{Value: !a.Value})
			}
		}

	case *BinaryNode:
		switch n.Operator {
		case "+":
			{
				a := toInteger(n.Left)
				b := toInteger(n.Right)
				if a != nil && b != nil {
					patch(&IntegerNode{Value: a.Value + b.Value})
				}
			}
			{
				a := toInteger(n.Left)
				b := toFloat(n.Right)
				if a != nil && b != nil {
					patch(&FloatNode{Value: float64(a.Value) + b.Value})
				}
			}
			{
				a := toFloat(n.Left)
				b := toInteger(n.Right)
				if a != nil && b != nil {
					patch(&FloatNode{Value: a.Value + float64(b.Value)})
				}
			}
			{
				a := toFloat(n.Left)
				b := toFloat(n.Right)
				if a != nil && b != nil {
					patch(&FloatNode{Value: a.Value + b.Value})
				}
			}
			{
				a := toString(n.Left)

   191		return func(c *conf.Config) {
   192			delete(c.Disabled, name)
   193		}
   194	}
   195	
   196	// WithContext passes context to all functions calls with a context.Context argument.
   197	func WithContext(name string) Option {
   198		return func(c *conf.Config) {
   199			c.Visitors = append(c.Visitors, patcher.WithContext{
   200				Name:      name,
   201				Functions: c.Functions,
   202				Env:       &c.Env,
   203				NtCache:   &c.NtCache,
   204			})
   205		}
   206	}
   207	
   208	// Timezone sets default timezone for date() and now() builtin functions.
   209	func Timezone(name string) Option {
   210		tz, err := time.LoadLocation(name)
   211		if err != nil {
   212			panic(err)
   213		}
   214		return Patch(patcher.WithTimezone{
   215			Location: tz,
   216		})
   217	}
   218	
   219	// MaxNodes sets the maximum number of nodes allowed in the expression.
   220	// By default, the maximum number of nodes is conf.DefaultMaxNodes.
   221	// If MaxNodes is set to 0, the node budget check is disabled.
   222	func MaxNodes(n uint) Option {
   223		return func(c *conf.Config) {
   224			c.MaxNodes = n
   225		}
   226	}
   227	
   228	// Compile parses and compiles given input expression to bytecode program.
   229	func Compile(input string, ops ...Option) (*vm.Program, error) {
   230		config := conf.CreateNew()
   231		for _, op := range ops {
   232			op(config)
   233		}
   234		for name := range config.Disabled {
   235			delete(config.Builtins, name)
   236		}
   237		config.Check()
   238	
   239		tree, err := checker.ParseCheck(input, config)
   240		if err != nil {
   241			return nil, err
   242		}
   243	
   244		if config.Optimize {
   245			err = optimizer.Optimize(&tree.Node, config)
   246			if err != nil {
   247				var fileError *file.Error
   248				if errors.As(err, &fileError) {
   249					return nil, fileError.Bind(tree.Source)
   250				}
   251				return nil, err
   252			}
   253		}
   254	
   255		program, err := compiler.Compile(tree, config)
   256		if err != nil {
   257			return nil, err
   258		}
   259	
   260		return program, nil

package conf

import (
	"fmt"
	"reflect"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/ast"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/builtin"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/checker/nature"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/vm/runtime"
)

var (
	// DefaultMemoryBudget represents default maximum allowed memory usage by the vm.VM.
	DefaultMemoryBudget uint = 1e6

	// DefaultMaxNodes represents default maximum allowed AST nodes by the compiler.
	DefaultMaxNodes uint = 1e4
)

type FunctionsTable map[string]*builtin.Function

type Config struct {
	EnvObject    any
	Env          nature.Nature
	Expect       reflect.Kind
	ExpectAny    bool
	Optimize     bool
	Strict       bool
	ShortCircuit bool
	Profile      bool
	MaxNodes     uint
	ConstFns     map[string]reflect.Value
	Visitors     []ast.Visitor
	Functions    FunctionsTable
	Builtins     FunctionsTable
	Disabled     map[string]bool // disabled builtins
	NtCache      nature.Cache
	// DisableIfOperator disables the built-in `if ... { } else { }` operator syntax
	// so that users can use a custom function named `if(...)` without conflicts.
	// When enabled, the lexer treats `if`/`else` as identifiers and the parser
	// will not parse `if` statements.
	DisableIfOperator bool
}

// CreateNew creates new config with default values.
func CreateNew() *Config {
	c := &Config{
		Optimize:     true,
		ShortCircuit: true,
		MaxNodes:     DefaultMaxNodes,
		ConstFns:     make(map[string]reflect.Value),
		Functions:    make(map[string]*builtin.Function),
		Builtins:     make(map[string]*builtin.Function),
		Disabled:     make(map[string]bool),
	}
	for _, f := range builtin.Builtins {
		c.Builtins[f.Name] = f
	}
	return c
}

// New creates new config with environment.
func New(env any) *Config {
	c := CreateNew()
	c.WithEnv(env)
	return c
}

func (c *Config) WithEnv(env any) {
	c.EnvObject = env
	c.Env = EnvWithCache(&c.NtCache, env)
	c.Strict = c.Env.Strict
}

func (c *Config) ConstExpr(name string) {
	if c.EnvObject == nil {
		panic("no environment is specified for ConstExpr()")
	}
	fn := reflect.ValueOf(runtime.Fetch(c.EnvObject, name))
	if fn.Kind() != reflect.Func {
		panic(fmt.Errorf("const expression %q must be a function", name))
	}
	c.ConstFns[name] = fn
}

type Checker interface {
	Check()
}

func (c *Config) Check() {
	for _, v := range c.Visitors {
		if c, ok := v.(Checker); ok {
			c.Check()
		}
	}
}

func (c *Config) IsOverridden(name string) bool {
	if _, ok := c.Functions[name]; ok {
		return true
	}
	if _, ok := c.Env.Get(&c.NtCache, name); ok {
		return true
	}
	return false
}

command:
cat > vm/runtime/error.go <<'EOF'
package runtime

import (
	"fmt"
)

// Type represents the classification of a runtime error.
type Type int

const (
	// IndexError represents out-of-range/bounds errors.
	IndexError Type = iota
	// ConversionError represents type-conversion failures.
	ConversionError
	// TypeError represents type-mismatch/assertion errors.
	TypeError
	// NilError represents nil-pointer/reference errors.
	NilError
	// RetryError represents retry-exhaustion errors.
	RetryError
	// CustomError represents all other errors, including errors from throw().
	CustomError
)

func (t Type) String() string {
	switch t {
	case IndexError:
		return "index"
	case ConversionError:
		return "conversion"
	case TypeError:
		return "type"
	case NilError:
		return "nil"
	case RetryError:
		return "retry"
	case CustomError:
		return "custom"
	default:
		return "unknown"
	}
}

// Error is a runtime error carrying a classification.
type Error struct {
	Type    Type
	Message string
	Err     error
}

func (e *Error) Error() string {
	if e.Message != "" {
		return e.Message
	}
	if e.Err != nil {
		return e.Err.Error()
	}
	return "runtime error"
}

func (e *Error) Unwrap() error {
	return e.Err
}

// NewError creates a new classified runtime error.
func NewError(t Type, format string, args ...any) *Error {
	return &Error{
		Type:    t,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf(format, args...),
	}
}

// Throw panics with a classified runtime error.
func Throw(t Type, format string, args ...any) {
	panic(NewError(t, format, args...))
}

// NormalizePanic converts any panic value into a classified runtime error.
func NormalizePanic(v any) *Error {
	if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
		return e
	}
	if e, ok := v.(error); ok {
		return &Error{
			Type:    CustomError,
			Message: e.Error(),
			Err:     e,
		}
	}
	return &Error{
		Type:    CustomError,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", v),
	}
}

// ErrorString returns the string conversion of an error-like value.
func ErrorString(v any) string {
	if v == nil {
		return ""
	}
	if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
		return e.Error()
	}
	if e, ok := v.(error); ok {
		return e.Error()
	}
	return fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
}

// ErrType classifies a caught error value.
func ErrType(v any) string {
	if v == nil {
		return "none"
	}
	if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
		return e.Type.String()
	}
	if _, ok := v.(error); ok {
		return CustomError.String()
	}
	return CustomError.String()
}
EOF
echo done

Now let's add the new AST node types.
CALL s36_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_qmp4Pk3EkiVCJTIICb5s5243
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && cat >> ast/node.go <<'EOF'

// TryNode represents a try/catch/finally expression.
// Example:
//
//	try { expr } catch e { handler } finally { cleanup }
type TryNode struct {
	base
	Try     Node
	Catch   Node
	Finally Node
}

// CatchNode represents a catch clause of a try expression.
// It can optionally bind the caught error to a name and filter
// errors by a substring of their message.
type CatchNode struct {
	base
	Name string
	Is   Node
	Node Node
}

// FinallyNode represents a finally clause of a try expression.
type FinallyNode struct {
	base
	Node Node
}

// ThrowNode represents a throw(value) expression.
type ThrowNode struct {
	base
	Node Node
}

// RetryNode represents a retry expression used inside catch blocks.
type RetryNode struct {
	base
}
EOF
echo done

CALL s38_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_1x8mT3V5RPltLgLwQtrG2699
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && cat >> ast/print.go <<'EOF'

func (n *TryNode) String() string {
	var s strings.Builder
	s.WriteString("try { ")
	s.WriteString(n.Try.String())
	s.WriteString(" }")
	if n.Catch != nil {
		s.WriteString(" ")
		s.WriteString(n.Catch.String())
	}
	if n.Finally != nil {
		s.WriteString(" ")
		s.WriteString(n.Finally.String())
	}
	return s.String()
}

func (n *CatchNode) String() string {
	var s strings.Builder
	s.WriteString("catch")
	if n.Name != "" {
		s.WriteString(" ")
		s.WriteString(n.Name)
	}
	if n.Is != nil {
		s.WriteString(" is ")
		s.WriteString(n.Is.String())
	}
	s.WriteString(" { ")
	s.WriteString(n.Node.String())
	s.WriteString(" }")
	return s.String()
}

func (n *FinallyNode) String() string {
	return fmt.Sprintf("finally { %s }", n.Node.String())
}

func (n *ThrowNode) String() string {
	return fmt.Sprintf("throw(%s)", n.Node.String())
}

func (n *RetryNode) String() string {
	return "retry"
}
EOF
echo done

Now update the parser. Let me modify `parseSecondary` to handle `try` and `retry`, and `parseCall` to special-case `try`, `throw`. Also `errtype` will
be handled as a builtin (I'll add to builtin list), so no parser special-case needed for errtype.

Let me first add the `try` block parsing method. I'll insert after `parseConditionalIf`. Let me modify parser.go.

Plan for `parseSecondary`:
```go
case Identifier:
    p.next()
    switch token.Value {
    case "true": ...
    case "false": ...
    case "nil": ...
    case "try":
        if p.current.Is(Bracket, "{") {
            node = p.parseTryBlock(token)
            if node == nil { return nil }
            return p.parsePostfixExpression(node)
        }
        if p.current.Is(Bracket, "(") {
            node = p.parseCall(token, []Node{}, true)
            return p.parsePostfixExpression(node)
        }
        p.error("unexpected token %v after try, expected { or (", p.current)
        return nil
    case "retry":
        if p.current.Is(Bracket, "(") {
            // treat retry(...) as a regular call
            node = p.parseCall(token, []Node{}, true)
        } else {
            node = p.createNode(&RetryNode{}, token.Location)
            if node == nil { return nil }
        }
        return p.parsePostfixExpression(node)
    default:
        ...
    }
```

And `parseCall` special-case for try and throw:
```go
func (p *Parser) parseCall(token Token, arguments []Node, checkOverrides bool) Node {
    var node Node
    ...
    switch token.Value {
    case "try":
        args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
        if len(args) != 2 {
            p.error("try requires exactly two arguments")
            return nil
        }
        catchNode := p.createNode(&CatchNode{Node: args[1]}, token.Location)
        if catchNode == nil { return nil }
        node = p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: args[0], Catch: catchNode}, token.Location)
        return node
    case "throw":
        args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
        if len(args) != 1 {
            p.error("throw requires exactly one argument")
            return nil
        }
        node = p.createNode(&ThrowNode{Node: args[0]}, token.Location)
        return node
    }
    ... existing
```

Wait, but `parseCall` also checks `isOverridden` and builtin.Index. I need to add the special-cases before the predicate/builtin checks so `try` and
`throw` are always reserved. But `throw` is also going to be a builtin; if I special-case it as ThrowNode, I won't add it to builtins (or I could
still add it but never use the builtin). Let me handle `throw` in parseCall specially and NOT add it to builtins. Actually, simpler to add `throw` as
a builtin and not special-case it — then the compiler just calls the function. But then `throw` could be overridden by user config. Hmm.

Let me check: if `throw` is in builtin.Index, the parser creates a BuiltinNode. The checker validates via Types. The compiler calls `emitFunction`,
which calls `f.Func` via `program.functions[arg]`. The function panics with runtime.Throw. That's fine and simpler. But should `throw` be overridable?
The spec says "throw(value) - throws a custom error". It's a language feature. I think it's fine to make it a builtin; overriding it via
`expr.Function("throw", ...)` would be unusual and might be intended to be disallowed, but the task probably doesn't test that.

However, there's one consideration: the compiler's `emitFunction` wraps functions in `OpCall0/1/2/3`, and `OpCall1` does:
```go
out, err := program.functions[arg](args...)
if err != nil { panic(err) }
vm.push(out)
```
If `throw`'s Func panics, the recover catches it. Good.

Let me implement `throw` as a builtin to reduce parser complexity, and `errtype` as a builtin. `try` and `retry` need special handling.

Actually wait — if I add `throw` as a builtin, then the parser's `parseCall` will create a BuiltinNode. But the existing `OpThrow` opcode would only
be used for the catch mismatch rethrow. That's fine.

Let me reconsider whether `throw` as a builtin with `Types: types(new(func(any) any))` works with the checker. `checkFunction` with Types: it iterates
types, calls `checkArguments` with `fn.Type()`. For `func(any) any`, checkArguments should validate one arg of any type and return any. Should work.

But there's a subtle issue: `checkArguments` might require the arg count to match. Let me check checkArguments to ensure it validates count.

  1175	func traverseAndReplaceIntegerNodesWithFloatNodes(node *ast.Node, newNature Nature) {
  1176		switch (*node).(type) {
  1177		case *ast.IntegerNode:
  1178			*node = &ast.FloatNode{Value: float64((*node).(*ast.IntegerNode).Value)}
  1179			(*node).SetType(newNature.Type)
  1180		case *ast.UnaryNode:
  1181			unaryNode := (*node).(*ast.UnaryNode)
  1182			traverseAndReplaceIntegerNodesWithFloatNodes(&unaryNode.Node, newNature)
  1183		case *ast.BinaryNode:
  1184			binaryNode := (*node).(*ast.BinaryNode)
  1185			switch binaryNode.Operator {
  1186			case "+", "-", "*":
  1187				traverseAndReplaceIntegerNodesWithFloatNodes(&binaryNode.Left, newNature)
  1188				traverseAndReplaceIntegerNodesWithFloatNodes(&binaryNode.Right, newNature)
  1189			}
  1190		}
  1191	}

CALL s45_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_vBLQwEt0Hc1XRbVs4yz35637
FUNCTION bash

cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='parser/parser.go'
s=open(p).read()

# Add parseTryBlock after parseConditionalIf
anchor = '''	return &ConditionalNode{
		Cond: nodeCondition,
		Exp1: expr1,

'''
assert anchor in s
insert = '''	return &ConditionalNode{
		Cond: nodeCondition,
		Exp1: expr1,
		Exp2: expr2,
	}

}

func (p *Parser) parseTryBlock(tryToken Token) Node {
	p.expect(Bracket, "{")
	tryBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
	p.expect(Bracket, "}")

	var catchNode *CatchNode
	var finallyNode *FinallyNode

	if p.current.Is(Identifier, "catch") {
		p.next()
		catch := &CatchNode{}

		// Optional catch name: catch <name> is "substring" { ... }
		if p.current.Is(Identifier) && !p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
			catch.Name = p.current.Value
			p.next()
		}

		// Optional substring filter: catch <name> is "substring" { ... }
		if p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
			p.next()
			catch.Is = p.parseExpression(0)
		}

		p.expect(Bracket, "{")
		catch.Node = p.parseSequenceExpression()
		p.expect(Bracket, "}")

		catchNode = p.createNode(catch, p.current.Location).(*CatchNode)
	}

	if p.current.Is(Identifier, "finally") {
		p.next()
		p.expect(Bracket, "{")
		finallyBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
		p.expect(Bracket, "}")
		finallyNode = p.createNode(&FinallyNode{Node: finallyBody}, p.current.Location).(*FinallyNode)
	}

	if catchNode == nil && finallyNode == nil {
		p.error("try block requires catch or finally clause")
		return nil
	}

	return p.createNode(&TryNode{
		Try:     tryBody,
		Catch:   catchNode,
		Finally: finallyNode,
	}, tryToken.Location)
}

'''
s=s.replace(anchor, insert)

# Update parseCall: add special cases at the beginning
old_call = '''func (p *Parser) parseCall(token Token, arguments []Node, checkOverrides bool) Node {
	var node Node

	isOverridden := false
'''
new_call = '''func (p *Parser) parseCall(token Token, arguments []Node, checkOverrides bool) Node {
	var node Node

	switch token.Value {
	case "try":
		args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
		if len(args) != 2 {
			p.error("try requires exactly two arguments")
			return nil
		}
		catchNode := p.createNode(&CatchNode{Node: args[1]}, token.Location)
		if catchNode == nil {
			return nil
		}
		node = p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: args[0], Catch: catchNode}, token.Location)
		if node == nil {
			return nil
		}
		return node
	case "throw":
		args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
		if len(args) != 1 {
			p.error("throw requires exactly one argument")
			return nil
		}
		node = p.createNode(&ThrowNode{Node: args[0]}, token.Location)
		if node == nil {
			return nil
		}
		return node
	}

	isOverridden := false
'''
assert old_call in s
s=s.replace(old_call, new_call)

open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")

Wait — there's a subtle issue with `parseTryBlock`: I check `p.current.Is(Identifier, "is")` to decide whether the first identifier after `catch` is
the catch name or the `is` keyword. But what if the catch name itself is "is"? E.g., `catch is { ... }` — then my code treats `is` as the `is` keyword
(not a name), then tries to parse an expression before `{`, which would fail. That's an edge case; the spec syntax requires a name before `is`, so
`catch is {}` is ambiguous and I'll leave it. Actually, my check `!p.current.Is(Identifier, "is")` means if the token is `is`, we skip the name. Then
we see `is` and parse the filter expression. For `catch is { ... }`, it would try to parse `{ ... }` as expression and likely fail with unexpected
token. That's acceptable.

Now, there's another issue: `catch` with no name but with `is`: `catch is "sub" {}` — my code would parse name="is", then see String "sub" and expect
`{` -> fail. The spec requires name before is, so `catch is "sub" {}` would actually mean name is "is"? Hmm, ambiguous. I'll leave it.

Also, I need to handle `try` with pipe: `foo | try(bar, baz)` — in parseExpression pipe handling, it calls `p.parseCall(identToken, []Node{nodeLeft},
true)`. My parseCall special-case for try uses `p.parseArguments(arguments)` which handles the already-passed left arg as offset. Good — it will parse
remaining args after `(`. For try with pipe, `try(bar, baz)` would have 1 pre-arg + 1 inside = 2 total, correct. Nice.

Now, let me also think about `throw` with pipe: `foo | throw(...)` — would be weird but parseArguments handles offset. Fine.

```go
case *ast.TryNode:
    nt = v.tryNode(n)

case *ast.FinallyNode:
```

Methods:
```go
func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
    t := v.visit(node.Try)
    if node.Catch != nil {
        c := v.visit(node.Catch)
        // combine like conditional
        if t.Nil && !c.Nil { return c }
        if !t.Nil && c.Nil { return t }
        if t.Nil && c.Nil { return v.config.NtCache.NatureOf(nil) }
        if t.AssignableTo(c) { return t }
        return Nature{}
    }
    if node.Finally != nil {
        v.visit(node.Finally)
    }
    return t
}

func (v *Checker) catchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) Nature {
    if node.Name != "" {
        v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{node.Name, Nature{}})
    }
    if node.Is != nil {
        is := v.visit(node.Is)
        is = is.Deref(&v.config.NtCache)
        if !is.IsString() && !is.IsUnknown(&v.config.NtCache) {
            return v.error(node.Is, "catch is must be a string (got %v)", is.String())
        }
    }
    nt := v.visit(node.Node)
    if node.Name != "" {
        v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
    }
    return nt
}

func (v *Checker) finallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) Nature {
    return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) throwNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) Nature {
    v.visit(node.Node)
    return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)  // or Nature{}
}

func (v *Checker) retryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) Nature {
    // Could check we're inside catch, but spec says runtime error.
    return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}
```

Hmm, for throwNode type: since throw never returns, but the expression type matters for parent nodes. Using anyType is safe. But consider `try {
throw(1) } catch { 42 }` — the TryNode combines t=anyType, c=int. `AssignableTo`? anyType (interface{}) should be assignable from int, so
t.AssignableTo(c)? Actually we want common type. `if c.AssignableTo(t) return t`. Let me follow conditional logic exactly:
```go
if t.AssignableTo(c) { return t }
if c.AssignableTo(t) { return c }
return Nature{}
```
The existing conditional only checks `t1.AssignableTo(t2)`. Let me reuse a helper. Actually to keep it simple, I'll copy the conditional logic.

For retryNode: type anyType.

Now, there's a subtlety with catch variable scoping in the checker: the catch node visits its Is expression and Node while the variable is in scope. I
do that. Good.

But there's a bigger issue: in the compiler, catch variable needs a variable index. The compiler's variable handling uses `c.scopes`. For catch, I'll
add a variable in the compiler when compiling TryNode's catch. I'll implement in compiler directly rather than a separate CatchNode compile.

func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
	t := v.visit(node.Try)

	var c Nature
	if node.Catch != nil {
		c = v.visit(node.Catch)
	} else {
		c = t
	}
	if node.Finally != nil {
		v.visit(node.Finally)
	}

	if c.Nil {
		return t
	}
	if t.Nil {
		return c
	}
	if t.AssignableTo(c) {
		return t
	}
	if c.AssignableTo(t) {
		return c
	}
	return Nature{}
}

func (v *Checker) catchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) Nature {
	if node.Name != "" {
		v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{node.Name, Nature{}})
		defer func() {
			v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
		}()
	}

	if node.Is != nil {
		is := v.visit(node.Is)
		is = is.Deref(&v.config.NtCache)
		if !is.IsString() && !is.IsUnknown(&v.config.NtCache) {
			return v.error(node.Is, "catch is must be a string (got %v)", is.String())
		}
	}

	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) finallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) Nature {
	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

	v.visit(node.Node)
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

func (v *Checker) retryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) Nature {
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && cat > vm/try.go <<'EOF'
package vm

// TryInfo contains static bytecode addresses for a try/catch/finally block.
type TryInfo struct {
	TryIP     int // address of the try body
	CatchIP   int // address of the catch handler, -1 if no catch
	FinallyIP int // address of the finally block, -1 if no finally
}

type tryState byte

const (
	stateNormal tryState = iota
	stateCatch
	stateFinally
)

// tryFrame is a runtime frame pushed by OpTry.
type tryFrame struct {
	info         TryInfo
	stackSize    int
	scopeSize    int
	retries      int
	state        tryState
	pendingError any
}
EOF
echo done

```go
func (vm *VM) Run(program *Program, env any) (_ any, err error) {
    defer func() {
        if r := recover(); r != nil {
            e := runtime.NormalizePanic(r)
            var location file.Location
            if vm.ip-1 < len(program.locations) {
                location = program.locations[vm.ip-1]
            }
            f := &file.Error{
                Location: location,
                Message:  e.Error(),
            }
            f.Wrap(e)
            err = f.Bind(program.source)
        }
    }()
    // resets (same as before)
    ...
    vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:0] // reset
    vm.run(program, env)
    if len(vm.Stack) > 0 {
        return vm.pop(), nil
    }
    return nil, nil
}

func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
    for {
        handled := false
        func() {
            defer func() {
                if r := recover(); r != nil {
                    if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
                        handled = true
                    } else {
                        panic(r)
                    }
                }
            }()
            vm.execute(program, env)
        }()
        if !handled {
            return
        }
    }
}

func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any) {
    var fnArgsBuf []any
    for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) {
        ... same loop ...
    }
}
```

But `execute` needs the fnArgsBuf to be persistent across handlePanic restarts? Actually, if a panic is handled and we re-enter execute, fnArgsBuf is
reinitialized to nil. That's fine — getArgsForFunc lazily allocates. The only downside is losing preallocation after each catch, but correctness is
preserved. Good.

But there's a subtlety: after a handled panic, the loop was interrupted inside `execute` at some point. The deferred recover in `run`'s inner func
sets handled=true, then `execute` returns (panic recovered inside the anonymous func). Then `run` loops and calls `execute` again from current vm.ip.
Good.

bugs)? They also normalize to CustomError. That's okay — the behavior remains (returns error rather than crashing). But we might lose the stack trace?

old_run = '''func (vm *VM) Run(program *Program, env any) (_ any, err error) {
	defer func() {
		if r := recover(); r != nil {
			var location file.Location
			if vm.ip-1 < len(program.locations) {

			f := &file.Error{

				Message:  fmt.Sprintf("%v", r),

new_run = '''func (vm *VM) Run(program *Program, env any) (_ any, err error) {
	defer func() {
		if r := recover(); r != nil {
			e := runtime.NormalizePanic(r)
			var location file.Location
			if vm.ip-1 < len(program.locations) {
				location = program.locations[vm.ip-1]
			}
			f := &file.Error{
				Location: location,
				Message:  e.Error(),
			}
			f.Wrap(e)
			err = f.Bind(program.source)
		}
	}()

	if vm.Stack == nil {
		vm.Stack = make([]any, 0, 2)
	} else {
		clearSlice(vm.Stack)
		vm.Stack = vm.Stack[0:0]
	}
	if vm.Scopes != nil {
		clearSlice(vm.Scopes)
		vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[0:0]
	}
	vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:0]
	vm.scopePoolIdx = 0 // Reset pool index for reuse
	vm.currScope = nil
	if len(vm.Variables) < program.variables {
		vm.Variables = make([]any, program.variables)
	}
	if vm.MemoryBudget == 0 {
		vm.MemoryBudget = conf.DefaultMemoryBudget
	}
	vm.memory = 0
	vm.ip = 0

	vm.run(program, env)

	if len(vm.Stack) > 0 {
		return vm.pop(), nil
	}

	return nil, nil
}

func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
	for {
		handled := false
		func() {
			defer func() {
				if r := recover(); r != nil {
					if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
						handled = true
						return
					}
					panic(r)
				}
			}()
			vm.execute(program, env)
		}()
		if !handled {
			return
		}
	}
}

func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any) {
	var fnArgsBuf []any

	for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) {'''

func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
	for {
		handled := false
		func() {
			defer func() {
				if r := recover(); r != nil {
					if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
						handled = true
						return
					}
					panic(r)
				}
			}()
			vm.execute(program, env)
		}()
		if !handled {
			return
		}
	}
}

func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any) {
	var fnArgsBuf []any

	for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) {
		if debug && vm.debug {
			<-vm.step
		}
...
			vm.Scopes = append(vm.Scopes, s)
			vm.currScope = s

		case OpAnd:
			a := vm.pop()
			b := vm.pop()
			vm.push(a.(bool) && b.(bool))

		case OpOr:
			a := vm.pop()
			b := vm.pop()
			vm.push(a.(bool) || b.(bool))

		case OpEnd:
			vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:len(vm.Scopes)-1]
			if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
				vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
			} else {
				vm.currScope = nil
			}

		default:
			panic(fmt.Sprintf("unknown bytecode %#x", op))
		}

		if debug && vm.debug {
			vm.curr <- vm.ip
		}
	}

	if debug && vm.debug {
		close(vm.curr)
		close(vm.step)
	}

	if len(vm.Stack) > 0 {
		return vm.pop(), nil
	}

	return nil, nil
}

func (vm *VM) push(value any) {
	vm.Stack = append(vm.Stack, value)
}

func (vm *VM) current() any {
	if len(vm.Stack) == 0 {
		panic("stack underflow")
	}
	return vm.Stack[len(vm.Stack)-1]
}

func (vm *VM) pop() any {
	if len(vm.Stack) == 0 {
		panic("stack underflow")
	}
	value := vm.Stack[len(vm.Stack)-1]
	vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:len(vm.Stack)-1]
	return value
}

func (vm *VM) memGrow(size uint) {
	vm.memory += size
	if vm.memory >= vm.MemoryBudget {
		panic("memory budget exceeded")
	}
}

func (vm *VM) scope() *Scope {
	return vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]

# Add handlePanic method after execute function (before push)
anchor='''func (vm *VM) push(value any) {
	vm.Stack = append(vm.Stack, value)
}'''
add='''func (vm *VM) handlePanic(r any, program *Program) bool {
	for i := len(vm.tryStack) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
		f := &vm.tryStack[i]
		switch f.state {
		case stateCatch:
			if f.info.FinallyIP >= 0 {
				f.pendingError = r
				f.state = stateFinally
				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
				} else {
					vm.currScope = nil
				}
				vm.ip = f.info.FinallyIP
				return true
			}
			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
			continue

		case stateFinally:
			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
			continue

		case stateNormal:
			if f.info.FinallyIP >= 0 {
				f.pendingError = r
				f.state = stateFinally
				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
				} else {
					vm.currScope = nil
				}
				vm.ip = f.info.FinallyIP
				return true
			}
			if f.info.CatchIP >= 0 {
				f.state = stateCatch
				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
				} else {
					vm.currScope = nil
				}
				vm.push(runtime.NormalizePanic(r))
				vm.ip = f.info.CatchIP
				return true
			}
			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
			continue
		}
	}
	return false
}

func (vm *VM) push(value any) {
	vm.Stack = append(vm.Stack, value)
}'''

REASONING_CONTENT
Now there's a design issue: I made `OpPushError` panic "not supported" but I earlier planned to use it. Let me reconsider: in handlePanic I push the
error onto the stack when jumping to catch. So I don't need OpPushError. But the compiler may still emit it if I write it that way. I'll design the
compiler to NOT emit OpPushError; the error is already on the stack. So I can remove OpPushError entirely, or leave it as a no-op for safety. Let me
keep it as a panic marker (should never be emitted) — actually it's safer to just not use it. But the opcode remains in the list. That's fine.

Wait, but there's an issue: `handlePanic` pushes the error onto the stack. Then the compiler's catch block starts with `OpStore` (if named) or `OpPop`
(if unnamed). Good. But what about the `is` test? The error is on the stack at catch entry. If named, `OpStore` consumes it. Then for the `is` test, I
need to load it back via `OpLoadVar`. If unnamed but with is-test (which shouldn't happen per syntax), I'd need a hidden var. I'll handle by always
using a variable for the error when there's an is-test, even if no name (allocate hidden var).

Now, there's another subtle issue: the `OpPushError` opcode is now dead but still in opcodes list and disassembler. I'll add disassembler support for
all new opcodes, or just leave it. Let me add disassembly entries for the new opcodes to keep Disassemble working.

Now, let me also handle the issue of `handlePanic` when there are frames above the one being unwound in stateCatch/stateFinally. My loop `for i :=
len-1; i>=0; i--` examines top first. If top is stateCatch and has finally, it jumps to finally (returns true), so we never examine frames below.
Good. If top is stateCatch without finally, we pop it (`vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]`) and continue to i-1. But `i` is the index in the ORIGINAL
slice; after `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]`, the frame at index i-1 is now at index i-1 (still valid). Then `i--` gives i-1. Good.

Wait, but `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]` when i == len-1 removes the top frame. Then `i--` -> len-2. Good.

Now, one concern: when handlePanic jumps to finally for an uncaught error (stateNormal -> finally), it sets `f.state = stateFinally` and `vm.ip =
f.info.FinallyIP`. The finally block starts with `OpFinally` (emitted by compiler), which sets state=finally again (no-op). Then finally body, then
OpEndTry pops and re-panics pendingError. Good.

But what about nested try: if an inner try has finally and throws, handlePanic sets inner frame state=finally, jumps to inner finally. Inner finally
runs, OpEndTry pops and re-panics pendingError. That panic propagates to the run loop's recover again, which calls handlePanic again. Now the inner
frame is popped; the outer frame is on top. handlePanic processes it. Good.

Now, a critical issue: `OpEndTry` pops the frame and then if `pendingError != nil`, panics. This panic happens inside `execute`'s loop. The `run`
recover catches it and calls handlePanic again. Good.

Now, what about the `run` loop's anonymous function defer: when handlePanic returns true, we set handled=true and return from the deferred func. The
anonymous func returns normally (panic recovered). Then `if !handled return` is false, so we loop again. Good. When handlePanic returns false, we
`panic(r)` inside the defer, which re-panics; this propagates out of `run` to `Run`'s defer, which converts to file.Error. Good.

Now, there's a subtle issue with the `run` loop: after `execute` completes normally (no panic), handled=false, so run returns. But what if `execute`
returns normally while there are still try frames on the stack? That would mean OpEndTry wasn't reached, e.g., due to a jump over it. That's a
compiler bug. I'll make sure compiler emits correctly.

Now, let me also update the `OpThrow` for the `throw` node. But wait — I special-cased `throw` in parser to create ThrowNode, which will compile to
value + OpThrow. Good. The builtin throw is NOT added. So `throw(1)` compiles ThrowNode. Good.

Now, one issue: `throw` used as a method/property name, e.g., `foo.throw`? The parser's `parsePostfixExpression` treats `throw` as identifier
property, fine. And `throw` as a standalone identifier without `(`? My parseSecondary only special-cases `try` and `retry`, not `throw`. So `throw`
without `(` becomes an IdentifierNode named "throw". That could conflict with env if user has `throw` variable. Fine.

Now, the compiler. Let me add compiler cases:
- `TryNode`
- `CatchNode` (handled by TryNode)
- `FinallyNode`
- `ThrowNode`
- `RetryNode`

Let me think carefully about the bytecode layout for TryNode.

Given TryNode with Try body, Catch (CatchNode), Finally (FinallyNode):

```
OpTry <tryInfoConst>
<compile Try>
OpJump finallyOrEnd
catchAddr:
   ; error is on stack
   [store to errVar or pop]
   [if catch.Is:
       <compile Is>         ; pushes substring
       OpLoadVar errVar
       OpErrContains
       OpJumpIfFalse noMatch
       <compile catch body>
       OpJump endOrFinally
     noMatch:
       OpLoadVar errVar
       OpThrow
       ...]
   [else:
       <compile catch body>
       OpJump endOrFinally]
finallyOrEnd:
   [if finally: OpFinally; <compile finally>]
endOrEnd:
   OpEndTry
```

Wait, the success path: after Try body, we jump to finallyOrEnd (which is after the catch block). The catchAddr is patched to the first instruction
after the try body's jump. Let me be precise about patching:

```
tryStart:
    OpTry <tryInfo>          ; emit with placeholder arg? Actually TryInfo constant needs TryIP, CatchIP, FinallyIP.
    <compile Try body>       ; TryIP = address of this instruction (the first instruction after OpTry)
    jumpToFinally = OpJump placeholder
catchAddr:
    <catch block>
    jumpToEnd = OpJump placeholder  (after catch block)
finallyAddr:
    [if finally] OpFinally; <compile finally>
endAddr:
    OpEndTry
```

The TryInfo constant needs:
- TryIP = address of first instruction of try body (after OpTry). Since we compile Try body right after OpTry, TryIP = current bytecode length after
emitting OpTry.
- CatchIP = address of catchAddr (if catch present, else -1).
- FinallyIP = address of finallyAddr (if finally present, else -1).

But the TryInfo constant is emitted as an argument to OpTry BEFORE we know CatchIP/FinallyIP. So I'll create a mutable TryInfo object, add it as
constant, and update its fields as I discover addresses, then the VM reads the constant by reference at runtime. Since it's a pointer, updating after
emit is fine.

Compiler code:
```go
func (c *compiler) TryNode(node *ast.TryNode) {
    info := &TryInfo{TryIP: 0, CatchIP: -1, FinallyIP: -1}
    infoIdx := c.addConstant(info)

    tryAddr := c.emit(OpTry, infoIdx)  // OpTry at address tryAddr-1 (1-based)
    info.TryIP = len(c.bytecode)  // address of next instruction = len(c.bytecode) (0-based) after OpTry

    c.compile(node.Try)

    // success path jump to finally/end
    toFinally := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)

    // catch block
    if catch, ok := node.Catch.(*ast.CatchNode); ok && catch != nil {
        info.CatchIP = len(c.bytecode)  // 0-based address of catch block start
        c.compileCatch(catch)
        // after catch, jump to finally/end
        toEnd := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
        c.patchJump(toFinally)
        c.patchJump(toEnd)
    } else {
        // no catch: success path jumps to finally/end; patch toFinally
        c.patchJump(toFinally)
    }

    // finally
    if fin, ok := node.Finally.(*ast.FinallyNode); ok && fin != nil {
        info.FinallyIP = len(c.bytecode)
        c.emit(OpFinally)
        c.compile(fin.Node)
    }

    c.emit(OpEndTry)
}
```

Wait, there's a control flow issue when there's no catch: the success path `toFinally` must jump to finally (or OpEndTry). If no catch, we patch
toFinally right where we are (after try body), which is also where finally starts. Good.

But when there IS a catch, the catch block is between toFinally and finally. The success path must skip the catch. The catch block itself ends with a
jump to finally/end. So:
```
OpTry
<try body>
toFinally: OpJump -> finallyOrEnd
catchAddr:
<catch block>
toEnd: OpJump -> finallyOrEnd
finallyOrEnd:
[finally]
OpEndTry
```
Here `toFinally` jumps to finallyOrEnd. `toEnd` also jumps to finallyOrEnd. I patch both to the same target. Good.

Now, `compileCatch`:
```go
func (c *compiler) compileCatch(catch *ast.CatchNode) {
    // At entry, the error is on the stack.
    errVar := -1
    if catch.Name != "" || catch.Is != nil {
        errVar = c.addVariable(catch.Name)  // but if no name, need a hidden name
        c.emit(OpStore, errVar)
        c.beginScope(catch.Name, errVar)
        defer c.endScope()
    } else {
        c.emit(OpPop)
    }

    if catch.Is != nil {
        c.compile(catch.Is)
        c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
        c.emit(OpErrContains)
        noMatch := c.emit(OpJumpIfFalse, placeholder)
        c.compile(catch.Node)
        end := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
        c.patchJump(noMatch)
        c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
        c.emit(OpThrow)
        c.patchJump(end)
    } else {
        c.compile(catch.Node)
    }
}
```

Wait, the scope handling: I call `c.addVariable(catch.Name)` which registers a variable and returns index, and `c.beginScope(catch.Name, index)` so
that references to the name in the catch body resolve. For a hidden variable (no name), I shouldn't beginScope with empty name. Let me use a unique
hidden name like `""` or a generated one; but beginScope with empty name won't be referenced. Fine. Actually `addVariable` increments `c.variables`
and sets debugInfo. For a hidden var, I'll pass `""` as name. But `beginScope("", index)` won't be looked up. Fine.

But wait, the variable index must be consistent between checker and compiler. The checker doesn't assign variable indices; the compiler does via
addVariable. The checker only tracks names in varScopes. For catch name, checker pushes varScope; compiler adds variable when compiling catch. Good.

Now, `ThrowNode`:
```go
func (c *compiler) ThrowNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) {
    c.compile(node.Node)
    c.emit(OpThrow)
}
```

`RetryNode`:
```go
func (c *compiler) RetryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) {
    c.emit(OpRetry)
}
```

`FinallyNode` and `CatchNode` as standalone should never be compiled directly (they're only inside TryNode), but I'll add cases that panic or no-op
just in case. Actually the `compile` switch will see them only if they're reached as standalone nodes, which shouldn't happen. I'll add cases that
panic "unexpected".

Now, a critical concern: the catch variable's type in the checker is `Nature{}` (unknown). When the compiler compiles `catch e { e }`, the identifier
`e` resolves via lookupVariable and emits OpLoadVar. Good. When compiling `catch e { errtype(e) }`, errtype builtin accepts any, fine.

Now, another concern: `c.addVariable` increments `c.variables`, and the Program allocates `variables` slots. Good.

Let me also think about the `try(expr, fallback)` function form. My parser creates `TryNode{Try: args[0], Catch: CatchNode{Node: args[1]}}` with no
catch name and no is. So compileCatch will emit OpPop then compile fallback. Good.

Now, let me also consider the `checker.tryNode` for `try(expr, fallback)`: Try and Catch types combined. Good.

Now, let me write the compiler changes. First add cases to the compile switch.
MESSAGE
Now add compiler support for the new nodes.
CALL s58_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_jK5EXBhfm5lGO0HRoFHn4948

p='compiler/compiler.go'
s=open(p).read()

# Add cases in compile switch
old='''	case *ast.PairNode:
		c.PairNode(n)
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("undefined node type (%T)", node))
	}'''
new='''	case *ast.PairNode:
		c.PairNode(n)
	case *ast.TryNode:
		c.TryNode(n)
	case *ast.CatchNode:
		c.CatchNode(n)
	case *ast.FinallyNode:
		c.FinallyNode(n)
	case *ast.ThrowNode:
		c.ThrowNode(n)
	case *ast.RetryNode:
		c.RetryNode(n)
	default:
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("undefined node type (%T)", node))
	}'''
assert old in s
s=s.replace(old,new)

# Append method implementations after PairNode method
anchor='''func (c *compiler) PairNode(node *ast.PairNode) {
	c.compile(node.Key)
	c.compile(node.Value)
}'''
add='''func (c *compiler) PairNode(node *ast.PairNode) {
	c.compile(node.Key)
	c.compile(node.Value)
}

func (c *compiler) TryNode(node *ast.TryNode) {
	info := &TryInfo{TryIP: 0, CatchIP: -1, FinallyIP: -1}
	infoIdx := c.addConstant(info)

	c.emit(OpTry, infoIdx)
	info.TryIP = len(c.bytecode)

	c.compile(node.Try)

	toFinally := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)

	if catch, ok := node.Catch.(*ast.CatchNode); ok && catch != nil {
		info.CatchIP = len(c.bytecode)
		c.CatchNode(catch)
		toEnd := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
		c.patchJump(toFinally)
		c.patchJump(toEnd)
	} else {
		c.patchJump(toFinally)
	}

	if fin, ok := node.Finally.(*ast.FinallyNode); ok && fin != nil {
		info.FinallyIP = len(c.bytecode)
		c.emit(OpFinally)
		c.compile(fin.Node)
	}

	c.emit(OpEndTry)
}

func (c *compiler) CatchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) {
	errVar := -1
	if node.Name != "" || node.Is != nil {
		name := node.Name
		if name == "" {
			name = "!err"
		}
		errVar = c.addVariable(name)
		c.emit(OpStore, errVar)
		c.beginScope(name, errVar)
		defer c.endScope()
	} else {
		c.emit(OpPop)
	}

	if node.Is != nil {
		c.compile(node.Is)
		c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
		c.emit(OpErrContains)
		noMatch := c.emit(OpJumpIfFalse, placeholder)
		c.compile(node.Node)
		end := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
		c.patchJump(noMatch)
		c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
		c.emit(OpThrow)
		c.patchJump(end)
	} else {
		c.compile(node.Node)
	}
}

func (c *compiler) FinallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) {
	panic("unexpected FinallyNode")
}

func (c *compiler) ThrowNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) {
	c.compile(node.Node)
	c.emit(OpThrow)
}

func (c *compiler) RetryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) {
	c.emit(OpRetry)
}'''
assert anchor in s
s=s.replace(anchor, add)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")

The helpers functions return typed values (bool, interface{}, float64, int), but `Throw` panics and the compiler doesn't know it never returns, so
missing return. I need to add `return` statements after `Throw` calls, or make `Throw` return a value / use `panic` directly with a helper that's
recognized as noreturn? In Go, a function that always panics still needs a return after it if the enclosing function has a return type, because the
compiler doesn't do flow analysis for custom functions (unless the function is `panic` itself).

Options:
1. Add `return false/nil/0` after each Throw in helpers. That's many (9 in generated + 9 in template).
2. Make Throw generic with returns? Not possible.
3. Use a named return with explicit `return` at end. Since Throw panics, the return after is unreachable but satisfies the compiler.

I'll add `return` statements after each Throw in the generated helpers and template. For Less/More/LessOrEqual/MoreOrEqual return bool: `return
false`. For Add/Subtract/Multiply return interface{}: `return nil`. For Divide return float64: `return 0`. For Modulo return int: `return 0`.

`Throw(TypeError, ...)` line.
MESSAGE
Need to add returns after Throw calls in the helpers. Let me fix both generated file and template.
CALL s62_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_gfx3Wg1IliwMPZce31Uu1328

cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && python3 - <<'EOF'
import re

def fix(path, funcs):
    s=open(path).read()
    for pat, ret in funcs:
        # find "Throw(TypeError, ...)" lines and add return after
        # We'll match each function body by locating "func X" ... next "func"
        # Simpler: match the Throw line followed by blank line and closing brace
        # We'll use regex to replace 'Throw(TypeError, "...", a, b)\n\t}\n' with 'Throw(...)\n\treturn RET\n\t}\n'
        s=re.sub(r'(Throw\(TypeError, "invalid operation: [^\n]+\))\n\t\}\n', lambda m: m.group(1)+'\n\treturn '+ret+'\n\t}\n', s)
    open(path,'w').write(s)

# For generated file and template, same patterns with same return types per function.
# But the replacement above is generic and applies to all functions; need correct ret per op.
# Let's instead handle by function names.

def fix_by_func(path):
    s=open(path).read()
    # Less, More, LessOrEqual, MoreOrEqual return bool
    for fn in ['Less','More','LessOrEqual','MoreOrEqual']:
        # find the Throw inside the function (last Throw before next func)
        idx=s.find('func '+fn)
        if idx<0: continue
        end=s.find('\nfunc ', idx+1)
        if end<0: end=len(s)
        seg=s[idx:end]
        seg2=seg.replace('Throw(TypeError', 'Throw(TypeError', 1)
        # Replace the Throw(...) line with Throw(...)+return false
        seg=re.sub(r'(Throw\(TypeError, "[^\n]+\))\n\t\}\n', r'\1\n\treturn false\n\t}\n', seg)
        s=s[:idx]+seg+s[end:]
    # Add, Subtract, Multiply return interface{} -> nil
    for fn in ['Add','Subtract','Multiply']:
        idx=s.find('func '+fn)
        if idx<0: continue
        end=s.find('\nfunc ', idx+1)
        if end<0: end=len(s)
        seg=s[idx:end]
        seg=re.sub(r'(Throw\(TypeError, "[^\n]+\))\n\t\}\n', r'\1\n\treturn nil\n\t}\n', seg)
        s=s[:idx]+seg+s[end:]
    # Divide returns float64 -> 0
    idx=s.find('func Divide')
    end=s.find('\nfunc ', idx+1)
    if end<0: end=len(s)
    seg=s[idx:end]
    seg=re.sub(r'(Throw\(TypeError, "[^\n]+\))\n\t\}\n', r'\1\n\treturn 0\n\t}\n', seg)
    s=s[:idx]+seg+s[end:]
    # Modulo returns int -> 0
    idx=s.find('func Modulo')
    end=s.find('\nfunc ', idx+1)
    if end<0: end=len(s)
    seg=s[idx:end]
    seg=re.sub(r'(Throw\(TypeError, "[^\n]+\))\n\t\}\n', r'\1\n\treturn 0\n\t}\n', seg)
    s=s[:idx]+seg+s[end:]
    open(path,'w').write(s)

fix_by_func('vm/runtime/helpers[generated].go')
fix_by_func('vm/runtime/helpers/main.go')
print("done")
EOF

STEP 64 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
The Throw line has one tab indent, then `}` at no indent. My regex expected `\n\t}\n` after the Throw line, but it's `\n}\n`. Let me fix with a
simpler approach: just add a return after each `Throw(TypeError, ...)` line based on function name. Let me use a simpler script that processes each
function and inserts a return line after the Throw line.
MESSAGE
CALL s64_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_uR8gtJC9vEGx61FKR2Zf1495
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && python3 - <<'EOF'
def fix(path):
    s=open(path).read()
    # map function -> return statement
    funcs = {
        'Less': 'false', 'More': 'false', 'LessOrEqual': 'false', 'MoreOrEqual': 'false',
        'Add': 'nil', 'Subtract': 'nil', 'Multiply': 'nil',
        'Divide': '0', 'Modulo': '0',
    }
    lines=s.split('\n')
    out=[]
    i=0
    cur=''
    while i<len(lines):
        line=lines[i]
        if line.startswith('func '):
            cur=line.split(' ')[1].split('(')[0]
        out.append(line)
        # If this is a Throw line inside one of our functions and next line is "}", insert return
        if 'Throw(TypeError' in line and cur in funcs:
            # find next non-empty line
            j=i+1
            while j<len(lines) and lines[j].strip()=='':
                out.append(lines[j]); j+=1
            if j<len(lines) and lines[j].strip()=='}':
                ret = funcs[cur]
                out.append('\treturn '+ret)
                i=j-1
        i+=1
    open(path,'w').write('\n'.join(out))

fix('vm/runtime/helpers[generated].go')
fix('vm/runtime/helpers/main.go')
print("done")
EOF

package expr_test

import (
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func evalStr(t *testing.T, code string) string {
	t.Helper()
	program, err := expr.Compile(code)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("compile %q: %v", code, err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run %q: %v", code, err)
	}
	return stringify(out)
}

func evalErrStr(t *testing.T, code string) string {
	t.Helper()
	program, err := expr.Compile(code)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("compile %q: %v", code, err)
	}
	_, err = expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err == nil {
		t.Fatalf("expected error from %q", code)
	}
	return err.Error()
}

func stringify(v any) string {
	switch x := v.(type) {
	case string:
		return x
	case int:
		return string(rune('0' + x))
	default:
		return ""
	}
}

func TestTryBasic(t *testing.T) {
	// success path
	if got := evalStr(t, `try { 1 / 0 } catch { 42 }`); got != "42" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
	// function form
	if got := evalStr(t, `try(1 / 0, 42)`); got != "42" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
	// success returns try result
	if got := evalStr(t, `try { 1 + 1 } catch { 42 }`); got != "2" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
}

func TestTryCatchName(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { 1 / 0 } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "type" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestTryCatchIndex(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { [1,2,3][5] } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "index" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestThrow(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { throw("boom") } catch e { e }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(out.(error).Error(), "boom") {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestErrTypeNone(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`errtype(nil)`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "none" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestRetry(t *testing.T) {
	env := map[string]any{
		"counter": 0,
	}
	// use a closure that fails until counter reaches 2
	env["fail"] = func() any {
		env["counter"] = env["counter"].(int) + 1
		if env["counter"].(int) < 3 {
			panic("still failing")
		}
		return env["counter"].(int)
	}
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch { retry }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestRetryExhausted(t *testing.T) {
	env := map[string]any{
		"fail": func() any {
			panic("always failing")
		},
	}
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch e { retry; errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	_, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	if err == nil {
		t.Fatal("expected error")
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), "retry") {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", err)
	}
}
EOF

output:
		v.visit(pair)
	}
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(mapType)

func (v *Checker) pairNode(node *ast.PairNode) Nature {
	v.visit(node.Key)
	v.visit(node.Value)
	return v.config.NtCache.NatureOf(nil)
}

func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
	t := v.visit(node.Try)

	var c Nature
	if node.Catch != nil {
		c = v.visit(node.Catch)
	} else {
		c = t
	}
	if node.Finally != nil {
		v.visit(node.Finally)
	}

	if c.Nil {
		return t
	}
	if t.Nil {
		return c
	}
	if t.AssignableTo(c) {
		return t
	}
	if c.AssignableTo(t) {
		return c
	}
	return Nature{}
}

func (v *Checker) catchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) Nature {
	if node.Name != "" {
		v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{node.Name, Nature{}})
		defer func() {
			v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
		}()
	}

	if node.Is != nil {
		is := v.visit(node.Is)
		is = is.Deref(&v.config.NtCache)
		if !is.IsString() && !is.IsUnknown(&v.config.NtCache) {
			return v.error(node.Is, "catch is must be a string (got %v)", is.String())
		}
	}

	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) finallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) Nature {
	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) throwNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) Nature {
	v.visit(node.Node)
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

So with config, `node.Finally` is non-nil and is a `*FinallyNode` with nil Node. But with plain parser.Parse, it's nil. The difference must be in

Wait — let me check: in `parseTryBlock`, I create finallyNode only if `p.current.Is(Identifier, "finally")`. For `try { 1 / 0 } catch { 42 }`, after
parsing catch, current is EOF. So finallyNode stays nil. Then `TryNode{Finally: finallyNode}` where finallyNode is a `*FinallyNode` nil pointer. In
Go, assigning a nil `*FinallyNode` to an `ast.Node` interface yields a non-nil interface with a nil typed pointer! That's the bug.

`var finallyNode *FinallyNode` — when not set, it's nil. Then `&TryNode{Finally: finallyNode}` where `Finally` is `ast.Node` interface. Assigning a
nil `*FinallyNode` to `ast.Node` makes the interface non-nil (it has a type but nil value). So `node.Finally != nil` is true, and
`v.visit(node.Finally)` calls finallyNode with a nil *FinallyNode, and `node.Node` is nil.

But wait, why did the plain `parser.Parse` dump show `Finally: nil`? Because `ast.Dump` uses reflect and for a nil typed pointer stored in interface,
reflect.ValueOf returns a nil pointer, and dump prints nil? Let me check: `dump` for interface calls `reflect.ValueOf(v.Interface())` — if
v.Interface() is a nil *FinallyNode, reflect.ValueOf returns a Value of kind Ptr with IsNil true. Then dump's `case reflect.Ptr: return dump(v.Elem(),
ident)` — dereferences a nil pointer -> panic? Actually reflect.Value.Elem() on nil ptr returns zero Value, and dump returns "nil". So dump shows nil.
But the interface is non-nil.

So the fix: in parser, ensure nil pointers are converted to nil interface. Use a helper:
```go
if p.current.Is(Identifier, "finally") { ... finallyNode = ... } // else leave as nil interface
```
Since `finallyNode` is declared as `*FinallyNode` and assigned to `Node` interface field, I should declare it as `ast.Node` (interface) instead, or
explicitly set `finallyNode = nil` typed interface. Let me change the local variables to `Node` interface type:

```go
var finallyNode ast.Node
```

package expr_test

import (
	"fmt"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func evalStr(t *testing.T, code string) string {
	t.Helper()
	program, err := expr.Compile(code)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("compile %q: %v", code, err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run %q: %v", code, err)
	}
	return fmt.Sprintf("%v", out)
}

func TestTryBasic(t *testing.T) {
	if got := evalStr(t, `try { int("abc") } catch { 42 }`); got != "42" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
	if got := evalStr(t, `try(int("abc"), 42)`); got != "42" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
	if got := evalStr(t, `try { 1 + 1 } catch { 42 }`); got != "2" {
		t.Fatalf("got %q", got)
	}
}

func TestTryCatchName(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { int("abc") } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "conversion" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestTryCatchIndex(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { [1,2,3][5] } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "index" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestThrow(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { throw("boom") } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "custom" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestErrTypeNone(t *testing.T) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(`errtype(nil)`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != "none" {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestRetry(t *testing.T) {
	env := map[string]any{
		"counter": 0,
		"fail": func() any {
			env["counter"] = env["counter"].(int) + 1
			if env["counter"].(int) < 3 {
				panic("still failing")
			}
			return env["counter"].(int)
		},
	}
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch { retry }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	if out != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", out)
	}
}

func TestRetryExhausted(t *testing.T) {
	env := map[string]any{
		"fail": func() any {
			panic("always failing")
		},
	}
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch e { retry; errtype(e) }`)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	_, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	if err == nil {
		t.Fatal("expected error")
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), "retry") {
		t.Fatalf("got %v", err)
	}
}
EOF

STEP 81 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
The closure references `env` before it's declared (closure captures the variable but declared after). In Go, the variable `env` is declared on line
91, and the closure is inside the literal — actually it's `env := map[string]any{ "fail": func() any { ... env ... } }` — inside the composite
literal, `env` isn't defined yet at function literal creation time, but Go's scoping: the closure captures the outer `env` variable; however, since
the literal is being assigned to `env`, the variable isn't in scope inside its own initializer. Need to use a `var env map[string]any` first. Let me
fix.
MESSAGE

old='''	env := map[string]any{
		"counter": 0,
		"fail": func() any {
			env["counter"] = env["counter"].(int) + 1
			if env["counter"].(int) < 3 {

			return env["counter"].(int)
		},

	env = map[string]any{
		"counter": 0,
		"fail": func() any {
			env["counter"] = env["counter"].(int) + 1
			if env["counter"].(int) < 3 {
				panic("still failing")
			}
			return env["counter"].(int)
		},
	}'''
assert old in s
s=s.replace(old,new)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")

STEP 82 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Now basic try/catch works (TestTryBasic passed), throw works, errtype(nil) works, retry works, retry exhausted works. But errtype(e) for
conversion/index returns "custom" instead of "conversion"/"index". This means the caught error is not being classified correctly.

The issue: when the VM catches a panic, `handlePanic` pushes `runtime.NormalizePanic(r)` onto the stack. The panic value from
`runtime.Throw(ConversionError, ...)` is a `*runtime.Error` with Type=ConversionError. NormalizePanic should return it as-is. So the catch variable
should be a *runtime.Error with Type conversion.

But `errtype(e)` returns "custom". Why? Let me check the `errtype` builtin. The builtin `ErrType` calls `runtime.ErrType(arg)`. That checks `if e, ok
:= v.(*Error); ok { return e.Type.String() }`. If the arg is a *runtime.Error, it should work.

func Int(x any) any {
	switch x := x.(type) {
	case float32:
		return int(x)
	case float64:
		return int(x)
	case int:
		return x
	case int8:
		return int(x)
	case int16:
		return int(x)
	case int32:
		return int(x)
	case int64:
		return int(x)
	case uint:
		return int(x)
	case uint8:
		return int(x)
	case uint16:
		return int(x)
	case uint32:
		return int(x)
	case uint64:
		return int(x)
	case string:
		i, err := strconv.Atoi(x)
		if err != nil {
			panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: int(%s)", x))
		}
		return i
	default:
		val := reflect.ValueOf(x)
		if val.CanConvert(integerType) {
			return val.Convert(integerType).Interface()
		}
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("invalid operation: int(%T)", x))
	}
}

func get(params ...any) (out any, err error) {
	if len(params) < 2 {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("invalid number of arguments (expected 2, got %d)", len(params))
	}
	from := params[0]
	i := params[1]
	v := reflect.ValueOf(from)

	if from == nil {
		return nil, nil
	}

	if v.Kind() == reflect.Invalid {
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("cannot fetch %v from %T", i, from))
	}

	// Methods can be defined on any type.
	if v.NumMethod() > 0 {
		if methodName, ok := i.(string); ok {
			method := v.MethodByName(methodName)
			if method.IsValid() {

p='builtin/lib.go'
s=open(p).read()
# Add return after runtime.Throw for functions that return values
# len returns any -> nil; abs/ceil/floor/round return any -> nil; int returns any -> nil; float returns any -> nil; get returns (out any, err error) -> return nil, nil
lines=s.split('\n')
out=[]
cur=''
for line in lines:
    if line.startswith('func '):
        cur=line.split(' ')[1].split('(')[0]
    out.append(line)
    if 'runtime.Throw(' in line and cur in ('Len','Abs','Ceil','Floor','Round','Int','Float'):
        # insert return nil
        out.append('\treturn nil')
    elif 'runtime.Throw(' in line and cur=='get':
        out.append('\treturn nil, nil')
s='\n'.join(out)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")
EOF

STEP 92 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
The caught `e` is a proper `*runtime.Error` with Type=1 (ConversionError) and Type=0 (IndexError). But `errtype(e)` returns "custom". So the builtin
`errtype` is not classifying correctly. Why?

The `errtype` builtin calls `runtime.ErrType(arg)`. But wait — when the compiler compiles `errtype(e)`, it sees `e` is an unknown Nature (from catch
var), and the builtin type is `func(any) string`. The compiler might dereference or something. Let me check: `ErrType` in builtin/lib.go:
```go
func ErrType(arg any) any {
    return runtime.ErrType(arg)
}
```
This should return `e.Type.String()` = "conversion". But output is "custom".

Wait — maybe the issue is that `errtype` is not using my builtin at all! Since I special-cased `errtype`... no, I didn't special-case errtype in
parser; it goes through builtin.Index. Let me check if `errtype` is actually in builtin.Index. Let me test `errtype(nil)` — that test passed ("none"),
so errtype builtin is being called. So why does `errtype(e)` return custom?

Hmm, maybe the compiler emits a `OpDeref` before calling errtype because `e`'s nature is unknown and the builtin's default Deref behavior dereferences
pointers. Look at compiler BuiltinNode lines 1150-1165:
```go
for i, arg := range node.Arguments {
    c.compile(arg)
    argType := arg.Type()
    if argType.Kind() == reflect.Ptr || arg.Nature().IsUnknown(c.ntCache) {
        if f.Deref == nil {
            c.emit(OpDeref)   // default deref
        }
    }
}
```
So for `errtype(e)`, since `e`'s nature is unknown (catch var), it emits OpDeref! `OpDeref` calls `deref.Interface(a)` which dereferences a pointer.
For `*runtime.Error`, it dereferences to `runtime.Error` value, then wraps in interface. Then `runtime.ErrType` receives a `runtime.Error` value (not
pointer), and my check `if e, ok := v.(*Error)` fails because it's `Error` value, not `*Error`. So it returns "custom".

Fix options:
1. Make `runtime.ErrType` also handle `Error` value (not just pointer).
2. Set `Deref` field on errtype builtin to return false, so no OpDeref is emitted.

The cleanest: make ErrType handle both pointer and value. Also NormalizePanic always produces *Error, so the catch variable is a *Error. But after
OpDeref it becomes Error value. Let me update `runtime.ErrType` to handle both:
```go
func ErrType(v any) string {
    if v == nil { return "none" }
    switch e := v.(type) {
    case *Error:
        return e.Type.String()
    case Error:
        return e.Type.String()
    case error:
        return CustomError.String()
    }
    return CustomError.String()
}
```

But also, the `catch e { e }` returns the *Error pointer (no deref because it's just identifier load). Good.

Let me also update `runtime.ErrorString` to handle Error value (for OpErrContains). And `runtime.NormalizePanic` handles *Error already.

Let me fix ErrType and ErrorString.

pops errVar (a *runtime.Error) and re-panics it. Since we're inside the catch block with state=catch (set by handlePanic), the rethrow propagates
outward (handlePanic sees stateCatch, runs finally if any, then pops). Good.

rethrown error propagates outward. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: the parser's `parseCall` special-case for `try` handles the case when `try` is called with pipe: `x | try(fallback)` would
be `try(x, fallback)` — exactly two args. Good.

Now, another subtle issue: `try` as a block form inside a larger expression, e.g., `try { int("a") } catch { 1 } + 1`. My parser's `parseSecondary`
returns `p.parsePostfixExpression(node)` after parseTryBlock, so it can be used in postfix. But what about binary operators? `parseExpression` calls

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func run(code string, env any) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(code)
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("compile %q: err=%v\n", code, err)
		return
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Printf("%q => out=%#v err=%v\n", code, out, err)
}

func main() {
	// finally with success
	run(`try { 1 } catch { 2 } finally { 3 }`, nil)
	// finally with catch
	run(`try { int("a") } catch { 2 } finally { 3 }`, nil)
	// finally with uncaught error -> finally runs then error propagates
	run(`try { int("a") } finally { 3 }`, nil)
	// finally throwing overrides prior result
	run(`try { 1 } catch { 2 } finally { int("boom") }`, nil)
	// catch substring match
	run(`try { int("abc") } catch e is "int" { "matched" }`, nil)
	// catch substring no match -> propagates
	run(`try { int("abc") } catch e is "nomatch" { "matched" }`, nil)
	// catch substring no match with finally
	run(`try { int("abc") } catch e is "nomatch" { "matched" } finally { "cleanup" }`, nil)
	// retry exhaustion classification
	run(`try { throw("x") } catch e { retry; errtype(e) }`, nil)
	// retry outside catch
	run(`retry`, nil)
	// throw message string conversion
	run(`try { throw(42) } catch e { e }`, nil)
	// errtype of error value returned by catch var
	run(`try { [1,2,3][9] } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	// nil error classification
	run(`try { nil } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	// arg count checks
	run(`try(1)`, nil)
	run(`try(1,2,3)`, nil)
	run(`throw()`, nil)
	run(`throw(1,2)`, nil)
	run(`errtype()`, nil)
	run(`errtype(1,2)`, nil)
	// try block no catch/finally
	run(`try { 1 }`, nil)
}
EOF

REASONING_CONTENT
Several issues:

1. **Finally with success returns 3 instead of 1!** `try { 1 } catch { 2 } finally { 3 }` returned 3, but it should return 1 (the try result), with
finally's result discarded. The bug: my bytecode layout makes the success path fall through the finally, and the finally body's result stays on the
stack on top of the try result. Let me think:
   - OpTry
   - compile Try -> pushes 1
   - OpJump toFinally
   - catch block...
   - toFinally: compile finally -> pushes 3 (now stack has [1, 3])
   - OpEndTry
   - At the end of program, Run pops top -> 3. Wrong!

   I need to pop the finally's result before OpEndTry, or arrange the stack so the try result is on top after finally. Since finally always executes
and its result is discarded, I should emit `OpPop` after compiling finally. But wait — the finally may throw; if it throws, the try result should be
discarded anyway. If finally succeeds, pop its result. So after `<compile finally>`, emit `OpPop`.

   But there's a subtlety: on the uncaught-error path (pendingError), the stack was restored to stackSize (empty for a simple try), then finally runs
and pushes 3, then OpEndTry pops frame and re-panics. The 3 is left on the stack but then the panic propagates out; the stack state doesn't matter.
Good.

   For the success path: try pushes 1, jump to finally, finally pushes 3, then OpPop removes 3, leaving 1. Then OpEndTry. Good.

   For the catch path: catch pushes 2, jump to finally, finally pushes 3, OpPop removes 3, leaving 2. Good.

   So I need `c.emit(OpPop)` after `c.compile(fin.Node)` in TryNode. But what if the finally body is a sequence that leaves no value? Sequence always
leaves last value. Fine.

2. **`try { int("a") } catch { 2 } finally { 3 }` returned the error** instead of 2! Wait — it returned `err=invalid operation: int(a)`. So the catch
didn't catch the error? But earlier `try { int("abc") } catch { 42 }` worked (TestTryBasic passed). Why does adding `finally` break it?

   Let me think. With finally, my TryNode bytecode:
   ```
   OpTry info
   <try body>          ; int("a") -> panics
   toFinally: OpJump placeholder
   catchAddr:
       catch block     ; catches -> push 2, then OpJump toEnd
   finallyAddr:
       OpFinally
       <finally body>  ; push 3
   OpEndTry
   ```
   When int("a") panics, handlePanic sees frame state=normal, finallyIP >= 0, so it sets pendingError=r, state=finally, jumps to finally! It does NOT
jump to catch because the finally check comes before the catch check in my handlePanic. That's the bug: if both catch and finally exist and the catch
matches, we should go to catch, then finally. If the catch doesn't match (or no catch), we should go to finally with pending error.

   In handlePanic's stateNormal case, I check finally first, then catch. That's wrong. I should check catch first (if it exists), and if catch exists,
go to catch. If no catch, and finally exists, go to finally with pending error.

   But what about the case where catch exists but the `is` test doesn't match? Then the catch block itself rethrows (OpThrow), and since state=catch,
handlePanic sees stateCatch and if finally exists jumps to finally with pendingError. So the catch-first approach handles substring mismatch
correctly.

   So fix handlePanic: in stateNormal:
   ```go
   if f.info.CatchIP >= 0 {
       f.state = stateCatch
       restore stack/scopes
       vm.push(NormalizePanic(r))
       vm.ip = f.info.CatchIP
       return true
   }
   if f.info.FinallyIP >= 0 {
       f.pendingError = r
       f.state = stateFinally
       restore
       vm.ip = f.info.FinallyIP
       return true
   }
   pop and continue
   ```

   Wait, but there's a case where catch exists AND finally exists AND the error should NOT be caught (e.g., the catch has an `is` test that doesn't
match). In that case, we enter catch, the test fails, and rethrow — the stateCatch handler then jumps to finally with pendingError. Good. So
catch-first is correct.

3. **`catch e is "int"` failed with "invalid operation: catch is *runtime.Error, expected string"** — The OpErrContains error message indicates the
stack operands were wrong. Let me look at my compiler's CatchNode:
   ```go
   if node.Is != nil {
       c.compile(node.Is)       ; pushes substring
       c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar) ; pushes error
       c.emit(OpErrContains)     ; pops substring, error
       ...
   }
   ```
   And OpErrContains does:
   ```go
   sub := vm.pop()
   err := vm.pop()
   ```
   So after compile: stack [substring, error] (error on top). OpErrContains pops error into `sub` (wrong!) and substring into `err`. The error message
says "catch is *runtime.Error, expected string" — `sub` was the error, so `sub.(string)` failed. I need to swap: pop `err` first, then `sub`. Let me
fix:
   ```go
   err := vm.pop()
   sub := vm.pop()
   ```
   Wait: stack top is error. `vm.pop()` returns error. Next is substring. So `err := vm.pop(); sub := vm.pop()`. Yes.

4. **`try { nil } catch e { errtype(e) }` returned out=nil err=nil** — The try body `nil` doesn't throw, so catch doesn't run; result is nil. That's
correct actually. errtype(nil) test passed separately.

5. **`try { int("a") } catch e is "nomatch" { ... }` with finally** returned the original int error, and the final string cleanup was discarded —
that's correct: finally ran (cleanup) and then the original error propagated. Good, though I should verify cleanup actually ran (I'll test with a side
effect).

6. **`try { throw("x") } catch e { retry; errtype(e) }`** returned "retry limit exceeded" — correct exhaustion. But the spec says "raises a distinct
exhaustion error" whose errtype is "retry". I should test `errtype` on the exhaustion error — but it propagated out of the catch, so `expr.Run`
returns the error. That's fine; the returned error should have Type=RetryError. Let me verify by catching it externally. Actually the returned error
from Run is a `file.Error` wrapping the runtime.Error, so `err.Error()` contains "retry limit exceeded". Good enough, but I should also test `try {

		info.FinallyIP = len(c.bytecode)

		c.emit(OpPop)
	}

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func main() {
	env := map[string]any{"log": []string{}, "counter": 0}
	env["cleanup"] = func() { env["log"] = append(env["log"].([]string), "cleanup") }
	env["fail"] = func() any { panic("boom") }

	// finally runs after uncaught error
	program, _ := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } finally { cleanup() }`)
	_, _ = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("uncaught finally log:", env["log"])

	// finally runs after catch
	env["log"] = []string{}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch { 1 } finally { cleanup() }`)
	out, _ := expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("catch finally log:", env["log"], "out:", out)

	// finally runs after successful try
	env["log"] = []string{}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { 1 } finally { cleanup() }`)
	out, _ = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("success finally log:", env["log"], "out:", out)

	// retry exhaustion caught by outer catch -> errtype retry
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { try { throw("x") } catch { retry } } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("retry exhaustion type:", out, err)

	// retry exhaustion with errtype from inside (but it propagates)
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { try { throw("x") } catch e { retry; errtype(e) } } catch e2 { errtype(e2) }`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("retry exhaustion type2:", out, err)
}
EOF

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func main() {
	env := map[string]any{"log": []string{}, "counter": 0}
	env["cleanup"] = func() any { env["log"] = append(env["log"].([]string), "cleanup"); return true }
	env["fail"] = func() any { panic("boom") }

	// finally runs after uncaught error
	program, _ := expr.Compile(`try { fail() } finally { cleanup() }`)
	_, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("uncaught finally log:", env["log"], "err:", err)

	// finally runs after catch
	env["log"] = []string{}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { fail() } catch { 1 } finally { cleanup() }`)
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("catch finally log:", env["log"], "out:", out, "err:", err)

	// finally runs after successful try
	env["log"] = []string{}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { 1 } finally { cleanup() }`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("success finally log:", env["log"], "out:", out, "err:", err)

	// retry exhaustion caught by outer catch -> errtype retry
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { try { throw("x") } catch { retry } } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("retry exhaustion type:", out, err)

	// retry exhaustion with errtype from inside (but it propagates)
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { try { throw("x") } catch e { retry; errtype(e) } } catch e2 { errtype(e2) }`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("retry exhaustion type2:", out, err)

	// nested finally: inner catch throws, inner finally runs, outer catches
	env["log"] = []string{}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { try { fail() } catch { throw("inner") } finally { cleanup() } } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("nested catch-finally log:", env["log"], "out:", out, "err:", err)
}
EOF
