Now I need to think about more edge cases and robustness:

1. **Retry semantics**: The spec says "automatic limit of three retries". My implementation allows 3 retries (total 4 attempts). Let me verify the
exact count. `OpRetry` checks `if f.retries >= 3` then throws. So:
   - Attempt 1: throw -> catch -> retry: retries=1 -> try
   - Attempt 2: throw -> catch -> retry: retries=2 -> try
   - Attempt 3: throw -> catch -> retry: retries=3 -> try
   - Attempt 4: throw -> catch -> retry: retries>=3 -> exhaustion
   So 4 attempts, 3 retries. Good.

2. **Retry counter reset per try block**: Each try frame has its own retries counter. If retry succeeds and the try body succeeds, the frame is popped
at OpEndTry. If the same try block is re-entered (e.g., in a loop), a new frame is created with retries=0. Good.

3. **The `catch is` substring**: I require a name before `is`. But what about `catch e is "sub"` where the substring expression is not a constant?
It's evaluated lazily each time an error is caught. Good.

4. **Type checking of `catch is`**: The checker requires it to be a string. Good.

5. **errtype with non-error values**: e.g., `errtype("foo")` should return "custom". `errtype(1)` -> "custom". `errtype(nil)` -> "none". Good.

6. **The `throw` message**: "the error message is its string conversion". `throw(42)` -> message "42". Good.

7. **Error type for nil-pointer dereference**: e.g., accessing field on nil. Let me test `try { nil.foo } catch e { errtype(e) }`. Actually `nil.foo`
might be a compile error or runtime nil error. Let me test a few classification scenarios:
   - index: `[1,2,3][5]` -> "index"
   - conversion: `int("abc")` -> "conversion"
   - type: `1 + "a"` -> "type"? Actually checker may reject at compile time because types are statically known. Need a runtime type error like `"a" +
1` where types unknown. Let me test with env returning `any`.
   - nil: `nil.foo` or `map` access on nil.
   - custom: `throw(1)`.

Let me test these classification scenarios via the VM. Since the checker is strict with typed env, I'll use `expr.AllowUndefinedVariables()` or map
env with any values to trigger runtime type errors.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func run(code string, env any) {
	program, err := expr.Compile(code, expr.Env(env), expr.AllowUndefinedVariables())
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("%-45q => compile err: %v\n", code, err)
		return
	}
	out, err := expr.Run(program, env)
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("%-45q => err: %v\n", code, err)
		return
	}
	fmt.Printf("%-45q => %v\n", code, out)
}

func main() {
	env := map[string]any{
		"a": 1,
		"s": "x",
		"n": nil,
	}
	run(`try { [1,2,3][5] } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	run(`try { int("abc") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	run(`try { 1 + s } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env) // type mismatch
	run(`try { n.foo } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env) // nil deref
	run(`try { throw(1) } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	run(`try { throw("boom") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	run(`errtype(nil)`, nil)
	run(`errtype("x")`, nil)
	run(`errtype(42)`, nil)
	// non-strict unknown value causing call error
	env2 := map[string]any{"f": 42}
	run(`try { f() } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env2)
}
EOF

Now let me consider some remaining issues:

1. **`OpPushError`** is dead code. I could remove it from opcodes, but it's already there. It's harmless.

2. **The `run` loop recursion/restart**: When a panic is handled, `execute` is called again. But `fnArgsBuf` is reinitialized each time. This is fine
functionally, but there's a subtle issue: if the panic happened inside `getArgsForFunc` after it already advanced `fnArgsBuf`, the buffer is just
discarded. Fine.

3. **Debug mode**: The `debug` channels. In `execute`, at the end there's `close(vm.curr)` and `close(vm.step)`. If a panic is handled and execute is
restarted, these channels may have been closed prematurely? Let me check: the loop runs, and only closes at the very end when `vm.ip >=
len(program.Bytecode)`. If a panic is handled, execute's loop is interrupted and it returns via the panic, not reaching the close. So close only
happens on normal completion. Good. But if a panic is handled and then the restarted execute completes, it closes. Fine.

Wait — actually there's a bug: if `debug` is true, the deferred recover in `run`'s anonymous func handles the panic, and `execute` never reaches the
close. Then restart. When finally complete, close. Good.

4. **`handlePanic` restores Scopes**: I restore `vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]` and set currScope. But I don't reset `scopePoolIdx`. The scope pool index
keeps growing, which is fine (it's just a pool). But after restoring scopes, the next `allocScope` will allocate from the pool at the current idx,
which may be beyond the restored scopes. That's fine — pool is a free list by index, not stack-coupled. Good.

5. **`OpEndTry` when pendingError is set and there are outer frames**: It pops the frame and panics. The run loop's recover calls handlePanic again,
which processes outer frames. Good.

6. **A subtle issue with `finally` + `retry`**: If the catch calls retry, we reset state to normal and jump to try start, but the finally hasn't run
yet. Is that the desired semantics? The spec doesn't specify. Most likely retry re-executes the try body without running finally (like a loop). I'll
keep it.

7. **A subtle issue: `retry` inside a nested catch** should retry the innermost try. My `OpRetry` uses `vm.tryStack[len-1]`. If we're inside a catch
body that contains another try/catch, the top frame is the inner one only if we're inside the inner catch. Correct.

8. **`catch e is "sub"` with retry**: If the test matches and catch body calls retry, the frame state is catch, retry works. Good.

9. **`try(expr, fallback)` with `retry`?** No catch name, so retry isn't available in fallback (retry would refer to the outer try if any; if the
try(expr, fallback) is inside a catch, retry would refer to that outer catch's try — hmm, actually the try(expr, fallback) creates a try frame, so if
fallback uses retry, `OpRetry` would target the top frame which is the try(expr, fallback) frame in state catch. That would retry the expr. Is that
desired? It's an edge case. The spec says retry usable inside catch blocks — the fallback of try() is effectively a catch block, so it's arguably
valid. Fine.

10. **Error message for catch substring type**: I throw TypeError. Good.

Now, let me also handle the `OpErrContains` when the error is a dereferenced `runtime.Error` value (not pointer). `runtime.ErrorString` handles both
now. Good.

11. **Potential issue: `try` block with a `catch` but no `finally`, and the catch itself throws — should propagate.** Let me test:
    `try { throw("a") } catch { throw("b") }` should propagate "b". And `try { try { throw("a") } catch { throw("b") } } catch e { e }` should catch
"b". Let me test.

12. **`throw` inside catch with the same try having finally** — the catch throws, finally runs, then error propagates. Tested nested case already.

STEP 101 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
Almost all good. But the last case: `try { retry } catch e { "caught" }` returned "caught"! That's wrong — `retry` in the try body (not catch) should
raise a runtime error, not be caught by the same catch.

Why did it return "caught"? Because `OpRetry` in the try body: the top frame is in state `normal` (the try body is executing). My `OpRetry` checks:
```go
f := &vm.tryStack[len(vm.tryStack)-1]
if f.state != stateCatch {
    panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, "retry is not allowed outside catch block"))
}
```
It panics. But then handlePanic sees the frame state=normal and CatchIP>=0, so it catches the error and jumps to catch, which returns "caught". So the
runtime error is caught by the same try — which is arguably correct semantics? The spec says "Using retry outside a catch block raises a runtime
error." If you use retry in a try body and there's a catch, should the catch catch it? The error "retry is not allowed outside catch block" is a
runtime error; in a language with try/catch, errors in the try body are catchable. So `try { retry } catch { "caught" }` catching the "retry not
allowed" error is actually reasonable and consistent. The spec says it "raises a runtime error" — it doesn't say it must be uncatchable. So this is
fine.

But wait — there's a subtle concern: if `retry` is used in a catch body of an inner try but there's an outer try, `OpRetry` targets the innermost
frame (which is state catch). That's correct. If `retry` is used in a finally block (state finally), my check `state != stateCatch` panics. The error
would be caught by outer try (if any), not the current one (since state=finally pops). Good.

Now, let me think about whether `retry` in a try body should be allowed to retry the try body itself? No — retry is only in catch. The error is
appropriate.

Now, another important scenario: `retry` in a catch when the try block has no error yet? That's not possible — catch only runs on error. Good.

Now, let me consider the **checker for `retry`**: I return anyType. The compiler emits OpRetry. Good.

Now, let me consider **the `try(expr, fallback)` with exactly two args requirement**. My parser checks `len(args) != 2`. But what about `try` used as
a regular function in env? Since I special-case `try` in parseCall regardless of `isOverridden`, users can't override `try`. That's probably intended
(it's a language keyword now). Good.

Now, let me consider **the `throw` special-case in parseCall and `throw` as env function**. Since I special-case throw, users can't override it. Fine.

Now, **`errtype` as builtin can be overridden** by user config (since it's in builtin.Index and parser checks isOverridden). The spec doesn't mention
overriding; fine.

Now, let me think about **compile-time errors for `catch is` requiring a string**. I have that in checker. Good.

Now, let me run the full test suite to ensure no regressions. But first, I should remove the scratch test file and create a proper test file. Let me
create a comprehensive `error_handling_test.go` in the root package and delete `errorhandling_scratch_test.go`.

Actually, let me think about whether to include tests. The task says "commit everything when you are done" — adding tests is good practice. I'll
include a proper test file.

Let me also consider the **disassembler** and **debug** support: I added OpTry to disassembler with `constant("OpTry")`. But `TryInfo` is a pointer to
struct; the disassembler's `constant` function prints it via `fmt.Sprintf("%v", c)` which would print `&{...}`. That's fine.

Now, let me think about **the optimizer's fold** and whether new AST nodes could cause issues. `ast.Walk` now traverses
TryNode/CatchNode/FinallyNode/ThrowNode. The optimizer's `fold` visitor won't match these types, so no optimization happens inside try bodies?
Actually `ast.Walk` calls `v.Visit(node)` on every node including TryNode, and recursively walks children. The fold visitor's `Visit` switch only
handles known node types; for TryNode it does nothing (no case), but still recurses via Walk. So constant folding inside try bodies still works
because Walk visits the child BinaryNode and fold patches it. Good.

REASONING_CONTENT
All tests pass. Now let me create a proper comprehensive test file for the new feature. I'll write `error_handling_test.go` in the root package
(`expr_test`).

Let me design tests covering all spec requirements:
1. `try(expression, fallback)`:
   - returns expression result on success
   - returns fallback on error
   - requires exactly two arguments (compile error)
   - fallback lazily evaluated (only evaluated on error)
2. `try { expr } catch { handler }`:
   - success returns expr
   - error returns handler
3. `catch <name> { ... }`:
   - binds the error
   - error can be used with errtype and throw message
4. `catch <name> is "substring"`:
   - catches only errors whose message contains substring
   - non-matching propagates
5. `finally { cleanup }`:
   - always executes after try/catch
   - if finally throws, propagates overriding prior result
   - result of finally discarded
6. `throw(value)`:
   - throws custom error with string conversion message
   - requires exactly one arg
7. `retry`:
   - inside catch re-executes try body
   - automatic limit of 3 retries before exhaustion
   - outside catch raises runtime error
8. `errtype(err)`:
   - index/conversion/type/nil/retry/custom/none
   - requires exactly one arg

Let me write this test file. I'll use the internal testify require or plain testing. The existing tests use `require`. I'll use plain testing with
helper functions to avoid import issues (internal/testify is accessible from root package).

Let me write it.

cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && cat > error_handling_test.go <<'EOF'
package expr_test

import (
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/internal/testify/require"
)

func runCode(t *testing.T, code string, env any) (any, error) {
	t.Helper()
	program, err := expr.Compile(code, expr.Env(env), expr.AllowUndefinedVariables())
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	return expr.Run(program, env)
}

func mustRun(t *testing.T, code string, env any) any {
	t.Helper()
	out, err := runCode(t, code, env)
	require.NoError(t, err)
	return out
}

func mustErr(t *testing.T, code string, env any) error {
	t.Helper()
	_, err := runCode(t, code, env)
	require.Error(t, err)
	return err
}

func TestTryFunctionForm(t *testing.T) {
	require.Equal(t, 2, mustRun(t, `try(1 + 1, 42)`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, 42, mustRun(t, `try(int("abc"), 42)`, nil))

	// Fallback must be lazily evaluated.
	require.Equal(t, 42, mustRun(t, `try(int("abc"), 42)`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, 1, mustRun(t, `try(1, int("abc"))`, nil))

	// Exactly two arguments are required.
	_, err := runCode(t, `try(1)`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
	_, err = runCode(t, `try(1, 2, 3)`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
}

func TestTryBlockForm(t *testing.T) {
	require.Equal(t, 2, mustRun(t, `try { 1 + 1 } catch { 42 }`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, 42, mustRun(t, `try { int("abc") } catch { 42 }`, nil))

	// A try block requires a catch or finally clause.
	_, err := runCode(t, `try { 1 }`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
}

func TestCatchBindsError(t *testing.T) {
	out := mustRun(t, `try { int("abc") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	require.Equal(t, "conversion", out)

	out = mustRun(t, `try { throw("boom") } catch e { e }`, nil)
	require.Error(t, out.(error))
	require.Contains(t, out.(error).Error(), "boom")
}

func TestCatchIsSubstring(t *testing.T) {
	require.Equal(t, "matched", mustRun(t, `try { throw("error happened") } catch e is "happened" { "matched" }`, nil))

	// Non-matching substring propagates the original error.
	err := mustErr(t, `try { throw("error happened") } catch e is "missing" { "matched" }`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "error happened")

	// The substring expression must be a string.
	_, err = runCode(t, `try { throw("x") } catch e is 42 { "matched" }`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
}

func TestFinally(t *testing.T) {
	// Finally runs after a successful try and its result is discarded.
	require.Equal(t, 1, mustRun(t, `try { 1 } finally { 2 }`, nil))

	// Finally runs after a handled catch.
	require.Equal(t, 2, mustRun(t, `try { int("a") } catch { 2 } finally { 3 }`, nil))

	// Finally runs even when the error is uncaught, then the error propagates.
	log := []string{}
	env := map[string]any{
		"log":     &log,
		"fail":    func() any { panic("boom") },
		"cleanup": func() any { log = append(log, "cleanup"); return true },
	}
	err := mustErr(t, `try { fail() } finally { cleanup() }`, env)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "boom")
	require.Equal(t, []string{"cleanup"}, log)

	// If the finally body throws, that error overrides any prior result.
	err = mustErr(t, `try { 1 } finally { int("boom") }`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "invalid operation: int(boom)")

	err = mustErr(t, `try { throw("original") } catch { 2 } finally { int("boom") }`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "invalid operation: int(boom)")
	require.NotContains(t, err.Error(), "original")
}

func TestThrow(t *testing.T) {
	// throw(value) uses string conversion of the value as the message.
	out := mustRun(t, `try { throw(42) } catch e { e }`, nil)
	require.Error(t, out.(error))
	require.Equal(t, "42", out.(error).Error())

	out = mustRun(t, `try { throw("custom") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil)
	require.Equal(t, "custom", out)

	// Requires exactly one argument.
	_, err := runCode(t, `throw()`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
	_, err = runCode(t, `throw(1, 2)`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)

	// Uncaught throw propagates out of the expression.
	err = mustErr(t, `throw("boom")`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "boom")
}

func TestRetry(t *testing.T) {
	var counter int
	env := map[string]any{
		"counter": &counter,
		"fail": func() any {
			counter++
			if counter < 3 {
				panic("not yet")
			}
			return counter
		},
	}
	require.Equal(t, 3, mustRun(t, `try { fail() } catch { retry }`, env))
	require.Equal(t, 3, counter)

	// More than three retries raises a retry-exhaustion error.
	counter = 0
	env["fail"] = func() any { counter++; panic("always") }
	err := mustErr(t, `try { fail() } catch { retry }`, env)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "retry")

	// The exhaustion error is classified as "retry" by an outer catch.
	out := mustRun(t, `try { try { fail() } catch { retry } } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env)
	require.Equal(t, "retry", out)

	// Using retry outside a catch block raises a runtime error.
	err = mustErr(t, `retry`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "retry")
}

func TestErrType(t *testing.T) {
	require.Equal(t, "index", mustRun(t, `try { [1,2,3][5] } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, "conversion", mustRun(t, `try { int("abc") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil))

	env := map[string]any{"s": "x", "n": nil}
	require.Equal(t, "type", mustRun(t, `try { 1 + s } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env))
	require.Equal(t, "nil", mustRun(t, `try { n.foo } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env))
	require.Equal(t, "custom", mustRun(t, `try { throw("x") } catch e { errtype(e) }`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, "none", mustRun(t, `errtype(nil)`, nil))

	// errtype requires exactly one argument.
	_, err := runCode(t, `errtype()`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
	_, err = runCode(t, `errtype(1, 2)`, nil)
	require.Error(t, err)
}

func TestTryCatchNested(t *testing.T) {
	// An error thrown from a catch body propagates to the outer handler.
	out := mustRun(t, `try { try { throw("a") } catch { throw("b") } } catch e { e }`, nil)
	require.Error(t, out.(error))
	require.Equal(t, "b", out.(error).Error())

	// Inner finally still runs when the inner catch body throws.
	log := []string{}
	env := map[string]any{
		"log":     &log,
		"cleanup": func() any { log = append(log, "cleanup"); return true },
	}
	out = mustRun(t, `try { try { throw("a") } catch { throw("b") } finally { cleanup() } } catch e { errtype(e) }`, env)
	require.Equal(t, "custom", out)
	require.Equal(t, []string{"cleanup"}, log)
}

func TestTryCatchOptimized(t *testing.T) {
	// The optimizer should still produce a correct program.
	program, err := expr.Compile(`try { int("abc") } catch e { errtype(e) }`)
	require.NoError(t, err)
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	require.NoError(t, err)
	require.Equal(t, "conversion", out)
}
EOF

STEP 106 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
All new tests pass. Let me also run `go vet` on the changed packages to catch issues, and run the full test suite again. But first, let me think about
a few more important design points and potential bugs:

1. **The `try` function form when used with the pipe operator**: `x | try(fallback)` — let me test. The pipe handler calls `parseCall(identToken,
[]Node{nodeLeft}, true)` with `try` and 1 pre-arg. My parseCall special-case: `args := p.parseArguments(arguments)` where arguments=[left].
parseArguments expects `(` and parses remaining. So `x | try(f)` = try(x, f). Good.

2. **`throw` with pipe**: `x | throw(...)` — weird but would be throw(x, ...) with wrong count.

3. **`errtype` with pipe**: `x | errtype()` — errtype builtin with 1 pre-arg. parseCall with builtin path: it calls `p.parseArguments(arguments)` for
BuiltinNode. Should work.

4. **The `try` block form in a sequence**: `let x = 1; try { int("a") } catch { x }`. The catch references x defined outside. My compiler's catch
variable scope: I call `c.beginScope(name, errVar)` in CatchNode. But x is already in c.scopes from the let. lookupVariable searches from top; catch
name is top, x is below. Good.

5. **Variable name collision**: if catch name is the same as an existing variable, e.g., `let e = 1; try { throw("x") } catch e { e }` — the checker's
catchNode pushes a varScope "e" on top, so `e` refers to the caught error. The compiler's addVariable creates a NEW variable index for e, and
beginScope("e", newIndex) puts it on top, so references resolve to the new index. Good. But `addVariable` increments variables count and allocates a
slot — the outer `e` variable is shadowed. Good.

6. **`retry` with counter shared across retries**: My OpRetry increments f.retries. But the try body's own state (variables) is not reset on retry.
For example, `let i = 0; try { i = ... }` — wait, expr has no assignment. But variables set via `let` are scoped inside. Retry jumps back to the try
body start, but `let` variables declared inside the try body would be re-executed? Since we jump back to the start of the try body, the `let` would
re-execute and store to the same variable slots. That's fine. But the `Variables` array values from the previous attempt are overwritten when the let
re-executes. Good.

   However, there's a subtle issue: if the try body contains a `let` that increments... no, no mutable state except through env. Fine.

7. **Scopes restoration on retry**: I restore `vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]`. But the `Variables` array is not restored. If the try body set some variables
(via let) before throwing, those remain on retry. But since retry re-executes the try body, those lets will overwrite. Fine.

8. **Memory budget**: `vm.memGrow` increments `vm.memory` and is not reset on retry. That's probably fine (budget is cumulative).

9. **Error location**: When an error is caught and handled, the program continues; no issue. When an error propagates out, the outer Run's recover
uses `vm.ip-1` to determine location. After unwinding through finallys, vm.ip might point somewhere unexpected. But the location is just for error
reporting; acceptable.

10. **`OpEndTry` pendingError re-panic**: This re-panic happens inside execute, then run's recover calls handlePanic. If there are no more frames,
handlePanic returns false and re-panics to Run's recover, which wraps with location = vm.ip-1. But vm.ip at that point is the instruction after
OpEndTry (since we already did `vm.ip += 1` at the top of the loop). So the error location might be slightly off. Not critical.

11. **A significant concern**: In `handlePanic`, when I restore `vm.Scopes`, I use `vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]`. But what about
`vm.currScope`? I set it to the last scope or nil. Good. But there's another subtlety: `OpBegin` allocates a scope from the pool and appends to
vm.Scopes; `OpEnd` pops. If an error occurs inside a loop and we restore scopes, the scopePoolIdx is not reset, so subsequent allocations get fresh
slots. Fine.

12. **Another significant concern**: The `run` loop's restart means the `fnArgsBuf` is reallocated each time. But there's also `vm.fnArgsBuf`? No,
it's local. Fine.

13. **`OpErrContains` when catch has `is` but no name**: My parser requires name before is, so this won't happen. But the compiler handles it via
hidden var. Good.

14. **Compiler's `CatchNode` hidden var name `"!err"`**: `addVariable("!err")` sets debugInfo. Fine.

15. **Potential issue with `addConstant(TryInfo)`**: The TryInfo pointer is added to constants. But the optimizer might run before compilation and
constant-fold try bodies. Since TryInfo is only added at compile time, no issue.

16. **Potential issue with `compile` and optimizer for `try` containing constants**: `try { 1 + 2 } catch { 3 }` — optimizer folds 1+2 to 3. Then
compile TryNode. Fine.

Now, let me also consider the **`try` keyword in other contexts**. For example, `try` as a map key `{try: 1}` — the parser's parseSecondary for
identifier `try` would error because after `try` comes `:` not `(` or `{`. Actually in a map, `{try: 1}` — the map parsing calls parseExpression for
the key, which calls parsePrimary -> parseSecondary, sees `try` identifier, and my code errors "unexpected token after try". That breaks using `try`
as a map key or property name. But `try` is now a reserved keyword, so that's acceptable. Similarly `retry` as a map key would become a RetryNode,
which would be wrong. Hmm, but `retry` as a property `foo.retry` — parsePostfixExpression treats `retry` as property string, not RetryNode, because
it's after a dot. Good. `{retry: 1}` — parseSecondary sees identifier `retry` not followed by `(`, creates RetryNode, then parsePostfixExpression
returns it; the map key is a RetryNode. That would compile to OpRetry and error at runtime. That's a regression for anyone using `retry` as a map key.
But `retry` is now a keyword per the spec, so it's acceptable. Same for `throw` as a map key: `{throw: 1}` — parseSecondary sees `throw` not followed
by `(`, creates IdentifierNode "throw", which is fine (it's only special-cased in parseCall when followed by `(`). So `throw` as identifier/map key
still works. `try` is special-cased always, so `{try: 1}` breaks. That's a tradeoff; keywords are usually reserved. I think it's acceptable.

Actually wait — `{try: 1}`: when parsing map expression, does it call parseExpression which calls parseSecondary? Let me verify parseMapExpression. It
likely calls parseExpression for keys. So yes, `try` as key would error. To be safe, I could make `try` only special-cased when followed by `(` or
`{`, and otherwise fall through to IdentifierNode. Let me adjust: in parseSecondary, for `try`, if current is `{` or `(`, handle; otherwise treat as
identifier (default). That way `try` can still be used as an identifier in non-call contexts, which is more backwards-compatible. Let me update.

Similarly for `retry`: if not followed by `(`, currently always RetryNode. To preserve `retry` as identifier in non-call contexts, I could only create
RetryNode... but `retry` alone is supposed to be the retry keyword. The spec says `retry` is a language feature. If someone uses `retry` as a variable
name, they'd be broken. But since the spec explicitly introduces `retry`, it's reasonable to reserve it. However, to be safe and match "usable inside
catch blocks", I'll keep RetryNode for standalone `retry`. Actually, if `retry` is used as a map key `{retry: 1}`, it'd create RetryNode and then at
runtime the map construction would compile OpRetry, which errors. That's a behavioral change but acceptable for a new keyword.

Let me at least make `try` fall through to identifier when not followed by `(` or `{`, to avoid breaking `{try: 1}`. Actually, `try` as a variable
name is unlikely, but let me be conservative. Let me update parseSecondary's try case: if not followed by `(` or `{`, fall through to create
IdentifierNode (default). This means `try` alone as an expression is just an identifier (probably unknown -> compile error in strict mode), which is
fine.

Let me make that change.

func (p *Parser) parseSecondary() Node {
	var node Node
	token := p.current

	switch token.Kind {

	case Identifier:
		p.next()
		switch token.Value {
		case "true":
			node = p.createNode(&BoolNode{Value: true}, token.Location)
			if node == nil {
				return nil
			}
			return node
		case "false":
			node = p.createNode(&BoolNode{Value: false}, token.Location)
			if node == nil {
				return nil
			}
			return node
		case "nil":
			node = p.createNode(&NilNode{}, token.Location)
			if node == nil {

CALL s111_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_eYiJOKcDqAbSIgbXPBUi8833
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && cat > /tmp/debug_edge.go <<'EOF'
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
)

func main() {
	// pipe into try
	program, _ := expr.Compile(`int("abc") | try(42)`)
	out, err := expr.Run(program, nil)
	fmt.Println("pipe try:", out, err)

	// dynamic substring
	env := map[string]any{"sub": "happ"}
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`try { throw("happened") } catch e is sub { "matched" }`, expr.AllowUndefinedVariables())
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env)
	fmt.Println("dynamic sub:", out, err)

	// nested finally unwinding
	log := []string{}
	env2 := map[string]any{"log": &log}
	env2["c1"] = func() any { log = append(log, "c1"); return true }
	env2["c2"] = func() any { log = append(log, "c2"); return true }
	env2["fail"] = func() any { panic("boom") }
	code := `try { try { fail() } finally { c1() } } finally { c2() }`
	program, _ = expr.Compile(code, expr.AllowUndefinedVariables())
	out, err = expr.Run(program, env2)
	fmt.Println("nested finally:", log, out, err)

	// catch with no name and is test - should not parse (name required)
	program, err = expr.Compile(`try { throw("x") } catch is "x" { 1 }`)
	fmt.Println("no-name is:", program, err)

	// try as identifier
	program, _ = expr.Compile(`{try: 1}`)
	out, err = expr.Run(program, nil)
	fmt.Println("try as key:", out, err)
}
EOF

name (because I check `!p.current.Is(Identifier, "is")`). Then it sees `is`, so it treats it as the `is` keyword and parses the expression `"x"`, then

parses as catch with is-test and no name. The output is a Program, so it compiled. That's a reasonable interpretation (name optional before is). The
spec says `catch <name> is "substring"` but doesn't explicitly forbid omitting name. My implementation supports it. Fine.

Now, let me think about whether I should also update the **checker to validate retry usage at compile time**. The spec says "Using retry outside a
catch block raises a runtime error." So runtime is correct.

Let me also consider the **`catch` variable type**: In the checker, I push `Nature{}` (unknown). The compiler's `CatchNode` adds a variable and
beginScope. But the checker and compiler must agree on variable indices? No, the compiler assigns indices. But there's a subtlety: the checker visits
the catch node and its body, and the catch body may contain `let` variables etc. The compiler compiles the catch body with `beginScope` for the catch
name. Good.

However, there's a potential bug: the checker's `catchNode` pushes a varScope and pops it after visiting node.Node. But the `defer` pops after
returning. Good.

`Nature{}` (unknown) which compiles to any. That's fine.

Let me now also verify **`finally` result discarded with sequence**: `try { 1 } finally { cleanup(); 3 }` should return 1. My compiler compiles
finally as a sequence, which pushes 3 (and pops cleanup's result), then OpPop removes 3. Good.

    55	func (vm *VM) Run(program *Program, env any) (_ any, err error) {
    56		defer func() {
    57			if r := recover(); r != nil {
    58				e := runtime.NormalizePanic(r)
    59				var location file.Location
    60				if vm.ip-1 < len(program.locations) {
    61					location = program.locations[vm.ip-1]
    62				}
    63				f := &file.Error{
    64					Location: location,
    65					Message:  e.Error(),
    66				}
    67				f.Wrap(e)
    68				err = f.Bind(program.source)
    69			}
    70		}()
    71	
    72		if vm.Stack == nil {
    73			vm.Stack = make([]any, 0, 2)
    74		} else {
    75			clearSlice(vm.Stack)
    76			vm.Stack = vm.Stack[0:0]
    77		}
    78		if vm.Scopes != nil {
    79			clearSlice(vm.Scopes)
    80			vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[0:0]
    81		}
    82		vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:0]
    83		vm.scopePoolIdx = 0 // Reset pool index for reuse
    84		vm.currScope = nil
    85		if len(vm.Variables) < program.variables {
    86			vm.Variables = make([]any, program.variables)
    87		}
    88		if vm.MemoryBudget == 0 {
    89			vm.MemoryBudget = conf.DefaultMemoryBudget
    90		}
    91		vm.memory = 0
    92		vm.ip = 0
    93	
    94		vm.run(program, env)
    95	
    96		if len(vm.Stack) > 0 {
    97			return vm.pop(), nil
    98		}
    99	
   100		return nil, nil
   101	}
   102	
   103	func (vm *VM) run(program *Program, env any) {
   104		for {
   105			handled := false
   106			func() {
   107				defer func() {
   108					if r := recover(); r != nil {
   109						if vm.handlePanic(r, program) {
   110							handled = true
   111							return
   112						}
   113						panic(r)
   114					}
   115				}()
   116				vm.execute(program, env)
   117			}()
   118			if !handled {
   119				return
   120			}
   121		}
   122	}
   123	
   124	func (vm *VM) execute(program *Program, env any) {
   125		var fnArgsBuf []any
   126	
   127		for vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode) {
   128			if debug && vm.debug {
   129				<-vm.step
   130			}
   131	
   132			op := program.Bytecode[vm.ip]
   133			arg := program.Arguments[vm.ip]
   134			vm.ip += 1
   135	
   136			switch op {
   137	
   138			case OpInvalid:
   139				panic(runtime.NewError(runtime.CustomError, "invalid opcode"))
   140	
   141			case OpPush:
   142				vm.push(program.Constants[arg])
   143	
   144			case OpInt:
   145				vm.push(arg)
   146	
   147			case OpPop:
   148				vm.pop()
   149	
   150			case OpStore:
   151				vm.Variables[arg] = vm.pop()
   152	
   153			case OpLoadVar:
   154				vm.push(vm.Variables[arg])
   155	
   156			case OpLoadConst:
   157				vm.push(runtime.Fetch(env, program.Constants[arg]))
   158	
   159			case OpLoadField:
   160				vm.push(runtime.FetchField(env, program.Constants[arg].(*runtime.Field)))

violates "custom error from any value".

it matches and catch body has retry, it works. Let me not worry.

Now, let me think about **the `OpEndTry` and `pendingError` re-panic with the finally's own stack state**. When handlePanic sets pendingError and
jumps to finally, the stack is restored to stackSize. Then finally body executes, possibly pushing values, then `OpPop` removes the finally result,
then `OpEndTry` re-panics. Good. But what if finally itself throws? Then handlePanic sees state=finally, pops frame and propagates (without running
any further finally for this frame). But wait — if the finally is part of a nested try and an outer try also has finally, the outer finally should
still run. In handlePanic, after popping the inner frame (stateFinally), we `continue` to i-1 (outer). If outer is stateNormal, it'll run its
catch/finally. Good.

that's overridden — that's correct (finally error overrides). Good.

could be a plain string or error. OpEndTry re-panics it. The run loop's recover calls handlePanic, which NormalizePanics as needed. Good.

from scratch on each handled panic, the buffer is recreated. This is correct but slightly less efficient. Fine.

func (p *Parser) parseTryBlock(tryToken Token) Node {
	p.expect(Bracket, "{")
	tryBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
	p.expect(Bracket, "}")

	var catchNode Node
	var finallyNode Node

	if p.current.Is(Identifier, "catch") {
		p.next()
		catch := &CatchNode{}

		// Optional catch name: catch <name> is "substring" { ... }
		if p.current.Is(Identifier) && !p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
			catch.Name = p.current.Value
			p.next()
		}

		// Optional substring filter: catch <name> is "substring" { ... }
		if p.current.Is(Identifier, "is") {
			p.next()
			catch.Is = p.parseExpression(0)
		}

		p.expect(Bracket, "{")
		catch.Node = p.parseSequenceExpression()
		p.expect(Bracket, "}")

		catchNode = p.createNode(catch, p.current.Location)
	}

	if p.current.Is(Identifier, "finally") {
		p.next()
		p.expect(Bracket, "{")
		finallyBody := p.parseSequenceExpression()
		p.expect(Bracket, "}")
		finallyNode = p.createNode(&FinallyNode{Node: finallyBody}, p.current.Location)
	}

	if catchNode == nil && finallyNode == nil {
		p.error("try block requires catch or finally clause")
		return nil
	}

	return p.createNode(&TryNode{
		Try:     tryBody,
		Catch:   catchNode,
		Finally: finallyNode,
	}, tryToken.Location)
}


func (p *Parser) parseConditional(node Node) Node {
	var expr1, expr2 Node
	for p.current.Is(Operator, "?") && p.err == nil {
		p.next()

		if !p.current.Is(Operator, ":") {
			expr1 = p.parseExpression(0)
			p.expect(Operator, ":")
			expr2 = p.parseExpression(0)
		} else {
			p.next()
			expr1 = node
			expr2 = p.parseExpression(0)
		}

		node = p.createNode(&ConditionalNode{
			Ternary: true,
			Cond:    node,
			Exp1:    expr1,
			Exp2:    expr2,
		}, p.current.Location)
		if node == nil {
			return nil
=== parseCall ===
	return p.createNode(&IntegerNode{Value: int(number)}, p.current.Location)
}

func (p *Parser) toFloatNode(number float64) Node {
	if number > math.MaxFloat64 {
		p.error("float literal is too large")
		return nil
	}
	return p.createNode(&FloatNode{Value: number}, p.current.Location)
}

func (p *Parser) parseCall(token Token, arguments []Node, checkOverrides bool) Node {
	var node Node

	switch token.Value {
	case "try":
		args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
		if len(args) != 2 {
			p.error("try requires exactly two arguments")
			return nil
		}
		catchNode := p.createNode(&CatchNode{Node: args[1]}, token.Location)
		if catchNode == nil {
			return nil
		}
		node = p.createNode(&TryNode{Try: args[0], Catch: catchNode}, token.Location)
		if node == nil {
			return nil
		}
		return node
	case "throw":
		args := p.parseArguments(arguments)
		if len(args) != 1 {
			p.error("throw requires exactly one argument")
			return nil
		}
		node = p.createNode(&ThrowNode{Node: args[0]}, token.Location)
		if node == nil {
			return nil
		}
		return node
	}

	isOverridden := false
	if p.config != nil {
		isOverridden = p.config.IsOverridden(token.Value)
	}
	isOverridden = isOverridden && checkOverrides

	if b, ok := predicates[token.Value]; ok && !isOverridden {
		p.expect(Bracket, "(")

OTHER STEP FIELDS

Now, let me consider a **major remaining concern**: the `run` loop's `handlePanic` re-entry. When `handlePanic` returns true, it has set `vm.ip` and
possibly `vm.tryStack` state. Then the anonymous func's defer returns, and `execute` was interrupted. But `execute`'s local `fnArgsBuf` is gone. We
loop and call `execute` again. However, `vm.ip` is now the catch/finally address. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: the `execute` function's `for` loop condition checks `vm.ip < len(program.Bytecode)`. When handlePanic sets `vm.ip` to a
catch address, and we restart execute, it starts at that address. Good.

Now, one more concern: **the `run` loop uses a `func()` literal with a defer each iteration**. If a program has many handled errors (e.g., a loop that
throws and catches thousands of times), this creates many goroutine stack frames but should be fine (they're just function calls). Each handled error
adds a small allocation for the closure/defer. Acceptable.

Now, let me think about **whether `try` should be disabled by `DisableIfOperator` or similar config**. There's no `DisableTry` config. Fine.

and returns on attempt 3. Let me also test that with counter that succeeds on attempt 4 (after 3 retries). Let me add a test.

Actually, let me think: with `fail` that panics while counter < 4, and catch `retry`:
- attempt 1: counter=1, panic
- catch -> retry (retries=1)
- attempt 2: counter=2, panic
- catch -> retry (retries=2)
- attempt 3: counter=3, panic
- catch -> retry (retries=3)
- attempt 4: counter=4, succeeds -> returns 4.
So `try { fail() } catch { retry }` with fail panicking while counter < 4 should return 4. If fail panics while counter < 5, then attempt 5 would be
needed but retries already 3, so retry throws exhaustion at attempt 4's catch. Let me add a test for exactly 3 retries.

func TestRetryLimit(t *testing.T) {
	// Exactly three retries are allowed before exhaustion.
	var counter int
	env := map[string]any{
		"counter": &counter,
		"fail": func() any {
			counter++
			if counter < 4 {
				panic("not yet")
			}
			return counter
		},
	}
	require.Equal(t, 4, mustRun(t, `try { fail() } catch { retry }`, env))
	require.Equal(t, 4, counter)

	// A fourth retry is rejected with a distinct retry error.
	counter = 0
	env["fail"] = func() any {
		counter++
		if counter < 5 {
			panic("not yet")
		}
		return counter
	}
	err := mustErr(t, `try { fail() } catch { retry }`, env)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "retry")
	require.Equal(t, 4, counter)
}'''
assert anchor in s

	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "boom")
}'''

	err = mustErr(t, `throw("boom")`, nil)
	require.Contains(t, err.Error(), "boom")

	// throw(e) of a caught error still produces a custom error.
	out = mustRun(t, `try { try { throw("a") } catch e { throw(e) } } catch e2 { errtype(e2) }`, nil)

assert anchor in s

The disassembly shows the layout is correct:
- OpTry with TryInfo {TryIP:1, CatchIP:4, FinallyIP:14}
- Try body: push abc, call int (throws)
- Jump to 14 (finally) on success

Looks good. But wait — the `OpTry` disassembly prints `&{1 4 14}` — the TryInfo pointer. Fine.

Now, there's a subtle issue in the disassembly: at index 8, `OpJumpIfFalse <2> (11)` jumps to 11 (the noMatch rethrow). At index 10, `OpJump <2> (13)`
jumps to 13, then 13 is `OpJump <0> (14)`? Wait, index 13 is `OpJump <0> (14)`. That's the `toEnd` jump patched to 14? Actually let me trace:
- index 9: OpLoadVar e (this is the matched path — wait no).

Hmm, let me re-read. The compiler's CatchNode for `catch e is "int" { e }`:
```
OpStore e                    ; 4
<compile is expr>            ; 5: push "int"
OpLoadVar e                  ; 6
OpErrContains                ; 7
OpJumpIfFalse noMatch        ; 8 -> noMatch
<compile catch body: e>      ; 9: OpLoadVar e
OpJump end                   ; 10 -> end
noMatch:                     ; 11
OpLoadVar e                  ; 11
OpRethrow                    ; 12
end:                         ; 13
OpJump toFinally             ; 13 -> 14
```
Wait, but the CatchNode method itself doesn't emit the jump to finally; the TryNode emits `toEnd := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)` after
`c.CatchNode(catch)`. So the sequence should be:
```
OpStore
OpPush "int"
OpLoadVar e
OpErrContains
OpJumpIfFalse noMatch
OpLoadVar e      ; catch body
OpJump end        ; from CatchNode
noMatch:
OpLoadVar e
OpRethrow
end:
OpJump toFinally  ; from TryNode (toEnd)
```
That matches: index 9 OpLoadVar e (catch body), index 10 OpJump end(13), 11 OpLoadVar e, 12 OpRethrow, 13 OpJump toFinally(14).

Wait, but index 13 is OpJump <0> (14). The compiler's `toEnd` jump placeholder was at index 13, patched to 14 (next instruction after the jump). And
`end` from CatchNode's internal `end` placeholder at index 10 patched to 13. Correct.

Now, there's a subtle issue: on the success path, after try body (index 3 OpJump to 14), it jumps to 14 (finally). Good. On catch matched path: index
9 loads e (the result), index 10 jumps to 13, then 13 jumps to 14 (finally). The stack has [e] then finally pushes 3, OpPop removes 3, leaving e. Then
OpEndTry. Result is e (the error). Good.

Now, let me consider **whether `OpTry`'s TryInfo constant gets the correct `TryIP`**. The compiler sets `info.TryIP = len(c.bytecode)` after emitting
OpTry. At that point, len(c.bytecode) = 1 (OpTry at index 0). So TryIP=1. Correct — retry jumps to index 1 (start of try body). Good.

All good. Now let me think about a potential issue with the **`try` keyword in the `parseSecondary` when followed by `(` but it's actually a member
call like `foo.try()`**. `parsePostfixExpression` handles `foo.try()` — after the dot, `try` is treated as property string, not as the `try` keyword.

Now, let me think about **whether the `run` loop's `handlePanic` correctly handles a panic that occurs in the `OpTry` instruction itself** (e.g.,
malformed constant). It would propagate out, which is fine.

Now, let me consider **the `retry` counter and nested retries**: If catch A calls retry, and the retried try body throws again, and catch A calls
retry again, the counter is in the same frame. Good. But what if the catch body calls a function that internally has its own try/catch with retry? The
inner frame is separate. Good.

Now, let me think about a tricky scenario: **`retry` in a catch where the try has a finally**. On retry, I reset state to normal and jump to TryIP
without running finally. Is that semantically acceptable? The spec doesn't define it. But consider `try { fail() } catch { retry } finally { cleanup()
}`. If we never run finally during retries, cleanup only runs when the try ultimately succeeds or the exhaustion error propagates. If the try succeeds
after a retry, the success path jumps to finally and cleanup runs. If exhaustion, the retry error propagates out of catch (state=catch -> if finally
exists, handlePanic jumps to finally with pendingError, so cleanup runs). So finally does run eventually. During intermediate retries, finally doesn't
run — which is a reasonable interpretation (retry is like a loop continuation, not a normal completion). I'll keep it.

Actually wait — let me reconsider. When `OpRetry` sets state=normal and jumps to TryIP, the finally hasn't run. But if the try body had side effects
(e.g., env mutations), they're not rolled back. That's expected for retry.

Now, let me look at **the checker's handling of `finally`**: In `tryNode`, I visit Finally but discard its type. Good. But what if finally has a type
error? It's still visited and errors are reported. Good.

Now, let me think about **the `try` block inside a predicate**. E.g., `filter(arr, try { # } catch { 0 })`. The predicate scope includes `#`. The try
block's body references `#` — the compiler compiles the try body, which contains PointerNode -> OpPointer, reading vm.currScope. If an error is caught
inside the predicate's try, handlePanic restores scopes to frame.scopeSize. But the frame.scopeSize was captured at OpTry time, which is AFTER OpBegin
(the loop scope was pushed before evaluating the predicate). So restoring scopes to scopeSize preserves the loop scope, so `#` still works in the
catch. Good. Let me test this.

Actually, this is an important test: try/catch inside a predicate loop where `#` is used in both try and catch. Let me test.

CALL s130_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_tUGyN1HLT7CN9IeWi7zp4372
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='vm/runtime/error.go'
s=open(p).read()
old='''// NormalizePanic converts any panic value into a classified runtime error.
func NormalizePanic(v any) *Error {
	if e, ok := v.(*Error); ok {
		return e
	}
	if e, ok := v.(error); ok {
		return &Error{
			Type:    CustomError,
			Message: e.Error(),
			Err:     e,
		}
	}
	return &Error{
		Type:    CustomError,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", v),
	}
}'''
new='''// NormalizePanic converts any panic value into a classified runtime error.
func NormalizePanic(v any) *Error {
	switch e := v.(type) {
	case *Error:
		return e
	case Error:
		ee := e
		return &ee
	case error:
		return &Error{
			Type:    CustomError,
			Message: e.Error(),
			Err:     e,
		}
	}
	return &Error{
		Type:    CustomError,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", v),
	}
}'''
assert old in s
s=s.replace(old,new)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")
EOF

func (v *Checker) pairNode(node *ast.PairNode) Nature {
	v.visit(node.Key)
	v.visit(node.Value)
	return v.config.NtCache.NatureOf(nil)
}

func (v *Checker) tryNode(node *ast.TryNode) Nature {
	t := v.visit(node.Try)

	var c Nature
	if node.Catch != nil {
		c = v.visit(node.Catch)
	} else {
		c = t
	}
	if node.Finally != nil {
		v.visit(node.Finally)
	}

	if c.Nil {
		return t
	}
	if t.Nil {
		return c
	}
	if t.AssignableTo(c) {
		return t
	}
	if c.AssignableTo(t) {
		return c
	}
	return Nature{}
}

func (v *Checker) catchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) Nature {
	if node.Name != "" {
		v.varScopes = append(v.varScopes, varScope{node.Name, Nature{}})
		defer func() {
			v.varScopes = v.varScopes[:len(v.varScopes)-1]
		}()
	}

	if node.Is != nil {
		is := v.visit(node.Is)
		is = is.Deref(&v.config.NtCache)
		if !is.IsString() && !is.IsUnknown(&v.config.NtCache) {
			return v.error(node.Is, "catch is must be a string (got %v)", is.String())
		}
	}

	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) finallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) Nature {
	return v.visit(node.Node)
}

func (v *Checker) throwNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) Nature {
	v.visit(node.Node)
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

func (v *Checker) retryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) Nature {
	return v.config.NtCache.FromType(anyType)
}

func (c *compiler) MapNode(node *ast.MapNode) {
	for _, pair := range node.Pairs {
		c.compile(pair)
	}

	c.emitPush(len(node.Pairs))
	c.emit(OpMap)
}

func (c *compiler) PairNode(node *ast.PairNode) {
	c.compile(node.Key)
	c.compile(node.Value)
}

func (c *compiler) TryNode(node *ast.TryNode) {
	info := &TryInfo{TryIP: 0, CatchIP: -1, FinallyIP: -1}
	infoIdx := c.addConstant(info)

	c.emit(OpTry, infoIdx)
	info.TryIP = len(c.bytecode)

	c.compile(node.Try)

	toFinally := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)

	if catch, ok := node.Catch.(*ast.CatchNode); ok && catch != nil {
		info.CatchIP = len(c.bytecode)
		c.CatchNode(catch)
		toEnd := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
		c.patchJump(toFinally)
		c.patchJump(toEnd)
	} else {
		c.patchJump(toFinally)
	}

	if fin, ok := node.Finally.(*ast.FinallyNode); ok && fin != nil {
		info.FinallyIP = len(c.bytecode)
		c.emit(OpFinally)
		c.compile(fin.Node)
		c.emit(OpPop)
	}

	c.emit(OpEndTry)
}

func (c *compiler) CatchNode(node *ast.CatchNode) {
	errVar := -1
	if node.Name != "" || node.Is != nil {
		name := node.Name
		if name == "" {
			name = "!err"
		}
		errVar = c.addVariable(name)
		c.emit(OpStore, errVar)
		c.beginScope(name, errVar)
		defer c.endScope()
	} else {
		c.emit(OpPop)
	}

	if node.Is != nil {
		c.compile(node.Is)
		c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
		c.emit(OpErrContains)
		noMatch := c.emit(OpJumpIfFalse, placeholder)
		c.compile(node.Node)
		end := c.emit(OpJump, placeholder)
		c.patchJump(noMatch)
		c.emit(OpLoadVar, errVar)
		c.emit(OpRethrow)
		c.patchJump(end)
	} else {
		c.compile(node.Node)
	}
}

func (c *compiler) FinallyNode(node *ast.FinallyNode) {
	panic("unexpected FinallyNode")
}

func (c *compiler) ThrowNode(node *ast.ThrowNode) {
	c.compile(node.Node)
	c.emit(OpThrow)
}

func (c *compiler) RetryNode(node *ast.RetryNode) {
	c.emit(OpRetry)
}

func (c *compiler) derefInNeeded(node ast.Node) {
	if node.Nature().Nil {
		return
	}
	switch node.Type().Kind() {
	case reflect.Ptr, reflect.Interface:
		c.emit(OpDeref)
	}
}

func (c *compiler) derefParam(in reflect.Type, param ast.Node) {
	if param.Nature().Nil {
		return
	}
	if param.Type().AssignableTo(in) {
		return
	}
	if in.Kind() != reflect.Ptr && param.Type().Kind() == reflect.Ptr {
		c.emit(OpDeref)
	}
}

}

STEP 136 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
`errtype(try { throw("x") } catch e { e })` returns "custom" — correct (throw produces custom). The middle test had a syntax issue (the outer try's
catch was consumed by the inner try), which is expected since `try { ... }` inside `errtype(...)` followed by `catch` — the parser interpreted the
outer catch as part of the inner try. This is a grammar ambiguity: `try { errtype(try { ... } catch { ... }) } catch { ... }` — the parser greedily
matches the inner try with the first catch, leaving the outer without catch. That's standard nested parsing behavior; to fix, users would need
parentheses or the outer catch would need to be disambiguated. This is acceptable — nested try/catch with function-call boundaries works when properly
written. My test was malformed.

All good. Now let me think about **whether the `errtype` should be added to the docs/functions.md** — not required.

Now, let me consider one more edge case: **`try` with `catch` and `finally` where the catch body returns a value that is also a string, and finally
pushes a value** — already tested.

Now, let me think about **memory safety / infinite loops**: `retry` has a limit of 3, so no infinite retry. Good.

Now, let me consider **the `run` loop and `handlePanic` when an error occurs during `OpEndTry` re-panic**: The re-panic from OpEndTry (pendingError)
is caught by `run`'s recover, handlePanic processes outer frames. Good.

Now, let me think about a **potential stack corruption**: when handlePanic restores the stack to `f.stackSize`, but the stack had values pushed by
`OpTry`? `OpTry` doesn't push anything. Good. The stackSize is captured before compiling the try body (after OpTry executes), so it's the depth at try
entry. Good.

Now, let me consider **the `OpRetry` when there are frames above the current one**: If catch body contains a nested try that is currently active
(state normal), then OpRetry inside the nested try's... no, retry in nested catch uses top frame (nested). If retry is in a catch body and there's a
nested try/finally currently NOT active (e.g., the catch body calls a function that starts a try), by the time retry is reached, the nested try has
completed and its frame popped. So top frame is the catch's try. Good.

Now, let me think about **whether `try` should be usable as an expression in a sequence with `finally`**. E.g.:
```
try { int("a") } catch { 2 } finally { 3 };
4
```
The sequence pops the try result, then evaluates 4. Should work. Let me test quickly.

Let me now think about a subtle but important issue: **the `run` loop's `handled` variable and the deferred recover**. There's a subtle Go gotcha: in
the anonymous func, the defer calls `vm.handlePanic`. If `handlePanic` returns true, we set `handled = true` and return. But `handlePanic` modifies
`vm.ip`, `vm.tryStack`, etc. Then the anonymous func returns. Then the outer loop checks `if !handled { return }` — handled is true, so it loops.
Good.

But wait — there's a potential issue: `vm.handlePanic` might panic itself (e.g., if it tries to restore stack with an invalid index). That would
propagate through the defer as a new panic, replacing the original. But that should never happen with correct compiler output.

Now, let me think about **whether `OpTry` should be added to `opArgLenEstimation`** in vm/vm.go. That array estimates function args count for the
fnArgsBuf optimization. New opcodes that push/pop stack could affect the estimate, but the estimate is just an optimization heuristic; missing opcodes
default to 0. It doesn't affect correctness. Good.

Now, let me consider **the `vm/func_types[generated].go`** — no changes needed.

Now, one more important consideration: **the `try` function form should be lazily evaluated fallback**. My implementation: `try(expr, fallback)`
compiles TryNode with Catch = CatchNode{Node: fallback}. The catch body only runs on error. So fallback is lazy. Tested with `try(1, int("abc"))`
returning 1 — if fallback were eager, it would error. My TestTryFunctionForm includes `require.Equal(t, 1, mustRun(t, `try(1, int("abc"))`, nil))` —
passed. Good.

Types. Good.

func Len(x any) any {
	v := reflect.ValueOf(x)
	switch v.Kind() {
	case reflect.Array, reflect.Slice, reflect.Map:
		return v.Len()
	case reflect.String:
		return utf8.RuneCountInString(v.String())
	default:
		runtime.Throw(runtime.TypeError, "invalid argument for len (type %T)", x)
		return nil
	}
}

func ErrType(arg any) any {
	return runtime.ErrType(arg)
}

	case uint32:
		return x
	case uint64:
		return x
	}
	runtime.Throw(runtime.TypeError, "invalid argument for abs (type %T)", x)
	return nil
}

func Ceil(x any) any {
	switch x := x.(type) {
	case float32:
		return math.Ceil(float64(x))
	case float64:
		return math.Ceil(x)
	case int, int8, int16, int32, int64, uint, uint8, uint16, uint32, uint64:
		return Float(x)
	}
	runtime.Throw(runtime.TypeError, "invalid argument for ceil (type %T)", x)
	return nil
}

func Floor(x any) any {
	switch x := x.(type) {
	case float32:
		return math.Floor(float64(x))
	case float64:
		return math.Floor(x)
	case int, int8, int16, int32, int64, uint, uint8, uint16, uint32, uint64:
		return Float(x)
	}
	runtime.Throw(runtime.TypeError, "invalid argument for floor (type %T)", x)
	return nil
}

func Round(x any) any {
	switch x := x.(type) {
	case float32:
		return math.Round(float64(x))
	case float64:
		return math.Round(x)
	case int, int8, int16, int32, int64, uint, uint8, uint16, uint32, uint64:
		return Float(x)
	}
	runtime.Throw(runtime.TypeError, "invalid argument for round (type %T)", x)
	return nil
		return int(x)
	case uint8:
		return int(x)
	case uint16:
		return int(x)
	case uint32:
		return int(x)
	case uint64:
		return int(x)
	case string:
		i, err := strconv.Atoi(x)
		if err != nil {
			runtime.Throw(runtime.ConversionError, "invalid operation: int(%s)", x)
			return nil
		}
		return i
	default:
		val := reflect.ValueOf(x)
		if val.CanConvert(integerType) {
			return val.Convert(integerType).Interface()
		}
		return float64(x)
	case uint16:
		return float64(x)
	case uint32:
		return float64(x)
	case uint64:
		return float64(x)
	case string:
		f, err := strconv.ParseFloat(x, 64)
		if err != nil {
			runtime.Throw(runtime.ConversionError, "invalid operation: float(%s)", x)
			return nil
		}
		return f
	default:
		runtime.Throw(runtime.ConversionError, "invalid operation: float(%T)", x)

Empty blocks fail at parse time with "unexpected token }" — this is because `parseSequenceExpression` calls `parseExpression(0)` on `}` and errors.
This is a pre-existing behavior for all block expressions (e.g., `if true {}` would also fail). Let me verify: `if true {}` probably also errors. So
empty catch/finally blocks are not supported, which is consistent with the rest of the language. The spec doesn't require empty blocks. Fine.

Now, one more important thing: **the `checker`'s `tryNode` visits Finally even when Catch is nil, but if Finally exists and Try succeeds, the result
is Try's type. If Try throws and there's no catch, the error propagates — type is irrelevant. Good.**

Now, let me consider **the `retry` counter reset when retry is used in a catch that's inside a loop**. Each iteration creates a new try frame (OpTry),
so retries reset per iteration. Good.

"retry". Good.

would run. That's reasonable.

func minMax(name string, fn func(any, any) bool, depth int, args ...any) (any, error) {
	if depth > MaxDepth {
		return nil, ErrorMaxDepth
	}
	var val any
	for _, arg := range args {
		// Fast paths for common typed slices - avoid reflection and allocations
		switch arr := arg.(type) {
		case []int:
			if len(arr) == 0 {
				continue
			}
			m := arr[0]
			for i := 1; i < len(arr); i++ {
				if fn(m, arr[i]) {
					m = arr[i]
				}
			}
			if val == nil || fn(val, m) {
				val = m
			}
			continue
		case []float64:
			if len(arr) == 0 {
				continue
			}
			m := arr[0]

					m = arr[i]
				}
			}
			if val == nil || fn(val, m) {
				val = m
			}
			continue
		case []any:
			// Fast path for []any with simple numeric types
			for _, elem := range arr {
				switch e := elem.(type) {
				case int, int8, int16, int32, int64,
					uint, uint8, uint16, uint32, uint64,

package vm

// TryInfo contains static bytecode addresses for a try/catch/finally block.
type TryInfo struct {
	TryIP     int // address of the try body
	CatchIP   int // address of the catch handler, -1 if no catch
	FinallyIP int // address of the finally block, -1 if no finally
}

type tryState byte

const (
	stateNormal tryState = iota
	stateCatch
	stateFinally
)

// tryFrame is a runtime frame pushed by OpTry.
type tryFrame struct {
	info         TryInfo
	stackSize    int
	scopeSize    int
	retries      int
	state        tryState
	pendingError any
}
=== runtime error ===
package runtime

import (
	"fmt"
)

// Type represents the classification of a runtime error.
type Type int

const (
	// IndexError represents out-of-range/bounds errors.
	IndexError Type = iota
	// ConversionError represents type-conversion failures.
	ConversionError
	// TypeError represents type-mismatch/assertion errors.
	TypeError
	// NilError represents nil-pointer/reference errors.
	NilError
	// RetryError represents retry-exhaustion errors.
	RetryError
	// CustomError represents all other errors, including errors from throw().
	CustomError
)

func (t Type) String() string {
	switch t {
	case IndexError:
		return "index"
	case ConversionError:
		return "conversion"
	case TypeError:
		return "type"
	case NilError:
		return "nil"
	case RetryError:
		return "retry"
	case CustomError:
		return "custom"
	default:
		return "unknown"
	}
}

// Error is a runtime error carrying a classification.
type Error struct {
	Type    Type
	Message string
	Err     error
}

func (e *Error) Error() string {
	if e.Message != "" {
		return e.Message
	}
	if e.Err != nil {
		return e.Err.Error()
	}
	return "runtime error"
}

func (e *Error) Unwrap() error {
	return e.Err
}

// NewError creates a new classified runtime error.
func NewError(t Type, format string, args ...any) *Error {
	return &Error{
		Type:    t,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf(format, args...),
	}
}

// Throw panics with a classified runtime error.
func Throw(t Type, format string, args ...any) {
	panic(NewError(t, format, args...))
}

// NormalizePanic converts any panic value into a classified runtime error.
func NormalizePanic(v any) *Error {
	switch e := v.(type) {
	case *Error:
		return e
	case Error:
		ee := e
		return &ee
	case error:
		return &Error{
			Type:    CustomError,
			Message: e.Error(),
			Err:     e,
		}
	}
	return &Error{
		Type:    CustomError,
		Message: fmt.Sprintf("%v", v),
	}
}

// ErrorString returns the string conversion of an error-like value.
func ErrorString(v any) string {
	if v == nil {
		return ""
	}
	switch e := v.(type) {
	case *Error:
		return e.Error()
	case Error:
		return e.Error()
	case error:
		return e.Error()
	}
	return fmt.Sprintf("%v", v)
}

// ErrType classifies a caught error value.
func ErrType(v any) string {
	if v == nil {
		return "none"
	}
	switch e := v.(type) {
	case *Error:
		return e.Type.String()
	case Error:
		return e.Type.String()
	case error:
		return CustomError.String()
	}
	return CustomError.String()
}

CALL s165_c0 TOOL_ID call_00_jSYZVlhoCvGixLRrpCLA1438
FUNCTION bash
ARGUMENTS
command:
cd $(git rev-parse --show-toplevel) && python3 - <<'EOF'
p='docs/language-definition.md'
s=open(p).read()
anchor='''## Variables

Variables can be declared with the `let` keyword.'''
add='''## Error Handling

Runtime errors can be caught and handled with `try`/`catch`. Errors are reported
as messages, and a caught error can be inspected with `errtype()`.

### try/catch

The block form evaluates the `try` body and, if it throws, evaluates the `catch`
handler:

```expr
try {
  int("abc")
} catch {
  42
}
```

The caught error can be bound to a name:

```expr
try {
  int("abc")
} catch e {
  errtype(e) // "conversion"
}
```

A catch clause can filter errors by a substring of their message using
`catch <name> is "substring"`:

```expr
try {
  throw("connection refused")
} catch e is "refused" {
  "handled"
}
```

The function form `try(expr, fallback)` returns the expression result on success,
or the lazily-evaluated fallback on error:

```expr
try(int("abc"), 42) // 42
try(1 + 1, 42)      // 2
```

### finally

An optional `finally` clause always executes after the `try`/`catch`, and its
result is discarded. If the `finally` body throws, that error propagates and
overrides any prior result:

```expr
try {
  1
} catch {
  2
} finally {
  3
}
```

### throw

`throw(value)` throws a custom error whose message is the string conversion of
the value:

```expr
try {
  throw("boom")
} catch e {
  e // error with message "boom"
}
```

### retry

`retry` can be used inside a `catch` block to re-execute the `try` body. Up to
three retries are allowed; a fourth retry raises a distinct retry-exhaustion
error. Using `retry` outside a catch block raises a runtime error:

```expr
try {
  fail()
} catch {
  retry
}
```

### errtype

`errtype(err)` classifies a caught error. It returns:

* `"index"` for out-of-range/bounds errors
* `"conversion"` for type-conversion failures
* `"type"` for type-mismatch/assertion errors
* `"nil"` for nil-pointer/reference errors
* `"retry"` for retry-exhaustion errors
* `"custom"` for all other errors, including those from `throw`
* `"none"` when the input is nil

```expr
try {
  [1, 2, 3][10]
} catch e {
  errtype(e) // "index"
}
```

## Variables

Variables can be declared with the `let` keyword.'''
assert anchor in s
s=s.replace(anchor, add)
open(p,'w').write(s)
print("done")
EOF

REASONING_CONTENT
Now let me do a final review of the complete diff for correctness, then commit. Let me look at the key parser/checker/compiler/vm diffs once more to
ensure no issues. Let me also run the full test suite once more with the docs change (docs don't affect tests).

Let me also think about one more potential issue: **the `try` function form `try(expr, fallback)` with `catch` node's location**. In parseCall, I
create CatchNode with `token.Location` (the try token location). Minor.

Now, let me consider **the `errtype` builtin and `DisableBuiltin`**. Since `errtype` is added to builtin.Builtins, `conf.CreateNew` will add it to
`c.Builtins`. If a user calls `DisableAllBuiltins()`, errtype is disabled, and `errtype(...)` would be treated as an unknown function. That's
consistent with other builtins. The spec probably expects errtype to always be available, but disabling builtins is an explicit opt-out. Fine.

Now, one more thought: **should `throw` be disabled by `DisableAllBuiltins`?** Since throw is special-cased in the parser (not a builtin), it remains
available even if all builtins are disabled. Is that correct? The spec says `throw(value)` is a language feature; it should probably always work. The
`DisableAllBuiltins` option disables builtins like len/type/map but `throw` is a core language construct now. I think it's fine for throw to remain.
Similarly `try`/`catch`/`finally`/`retry` are language syntax, not builtins.

Now, let me consider **whether `errtype` should be special-cased too** so it can't be overridden by env. The spec says `errtype(err)` is a function.
It's reasonable for it to be a builtin that can be overridden. Fine.

Now, let me run the full test suite once more, then commit.

+func (vm *VM) handlePanic(r any, program *Program) bool {
+	for i := len(vm.tryStack) - 1; i >= 0; i-- {
+		f := &vm.tryStack[i]
+		switch f.state {
+		case stateCatch:
+			if f.info.FinallyIP >= 0 {
+				f.pendingError = r
+				f.state = stateFinally
+				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
+				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
+				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
+					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
+				} else {
+					vm.currScope = nil
+				}
+				vm.ip = f.info.FinallyIP
+				return true
+			}
+			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
+			continue
+
+		case stateFinally:
+			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
+			continue
+
+		case stateNormal:
+			if f.info.CatchIP >= 0 {
+				f.state = stateCatch
+				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
+				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
+				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
+					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
+				} else {
+					vm.currScope = nil
+				}
+				vm.push(runtime.NormalizePanic(r))
+				vm.ip = f.info.CatchIP
+				return true
+			}
+			if f.info.FinallyIP >= 0 {
+				f.pendingError = r
+				f.state = stateFinally
+				vm.Stack = vm.Stack[:f.stackSize]
+				vm.Scopes = vm.Scopes[:f.scopeSize]
+				if len(vm.Scopes) > 0 {
+					vm.currScope = vm.Scopes[len(vm.Scopes)-1]
+				} else {
+					vm.currScope = nil
+				}
+				vm.ip = f.info.FinallyIP
+				return true
+			}
+			vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]
+			continue
+		}
 	}

REASONING_CONTENT
The diff looks correct. One thing I want to verify: in `handlePanic`, when `stateCatch` or `stateFinally` and we do `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]`,
we continue to `i-1`. But `f` points into the original slice; after reslicing, the next iteration accesses `&vm.tryStack[i-1]` which is valid. Good.

Now, there's a subtle concern: in `stateCatch` case with no finally, `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:i]` removes frames from i onward. But there might be
frames ABOVE i? Since we iterate from top, any frames above i have already been examined. If the top frame was stateCatch without finally, we pop it
(i = len-1). If there were frames above i that were in stateCatch/stateFinally, they'd have been popped in previous iterations. So `vm.tryStack[:i]`
correctly removes the current frame and any already-removed... wait, no. Let me think carefully.

Suppose tryStack = [F0(normal), F1(catch)]. We start at i=1 (F1 catch). F1 has no finally, so `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:1]` -> [F0]. Then `i--` ->
0. Now we examine F0. Good.

Suppose tryStack = [F0(normal), F1(catch), F2(finally)]. Start i=2 (F2 finally): `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:2]` -> [F0, F1]. i-- -> 1. Examine F1
catch: if no finally, `vm.tryStack = vm.tryStack[:1]` -> [F0]. i-- -> 0. Examine F0. Good.

So the reslicing works correctly.

Now, one more concern: in `stateFinally`, I pop the frame and continue, but the pendingError in that frame (if any) is discarded — correct, because
the finally error overrides.

Now, let me also consider the case where `handlePanic` handles an error by jumping to catch, but the error's stack is restored to `f.stackSize` and
then `vm.push(NormalizePanic(r))`. The compiler's catch block starts with OpStore or OpPop. Good.

Now, let me think about **the `vm.run` loop with `handled` and the deferred recover**: if `handlePanic` returns true, the deferred function returns
after setting handled=true. But there's a subtlety: the deferred function itself calls `vm.handlePanic`, which might call `vm.push` and modify the
stack. If `handlePanic` panics (internal error), the defer would propagate that new panic. But that's fine.

Now, let me also verify that **the `run` loop terminates** if `execute` returns normally (no panic, program done) — handled stays false, loop returns.
Good.

the final test file briefly.

package expr_test

import (
	"testing"

	"github.com/expr-lang/expr"
	"github.com/expr-lang/expr/internal/testify/require"
)

func runCode(t *testing.T, code string, env any) (any, error) {
	t.Helper()
	program, err := expr.Compile(code, expr.AllowUndefinedVariables())
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	return expr.Run(program, env)
}

func mustRun(t *testing.T, code string, env any) any {
	t.Helper()
	out, err := runCode(t, code, env)
	require.NoError(t, err)
	return out
}

func mustErr(t *testing.T, code string, env any) error {
	t.Helper()
	_, err := runCode(t, code, env)
	require.Error(t, err)
	return err
}

func TestTryFunctionForm(t *testing.T) {
	require.Equal(t, 2, mustRun(t, `try(1 + 1, 42)`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, 42, mustRun(t, `try(int("abc"), 42)`, nil))

	// Fallback must be lazily evaluated.
	require.Equal(t, 42, mustRun(t, `try(int("abc"), 42)`, nil))
	require.Equal(t, 1, mustRun(t, `try(1, int("abc"))`, nil))

MESSAGE
I've completed the implementation. Here's a summary of what was done:

## Summary

Implemented comprehensive error handling in the expr language:

### Language features
- **`try(expression, fallback)`** — function form; lazily-evaluates fallback on error; requires exactly two args.
- **`try { ... } catch { ... }`** — block form with optional `catch <name> { ... }` error binding.
- **`catch <name> is "substring" { ... }`** — catches only errors whose message contains the substring; non-matches rethrow the original error.
- **`finally { ... }`** — always executes after try/catch; its result is discarded; if it throws, that error overrides the prior result.
- **`throw(value)`** — throws a custom error whose message is the string conversion of the value; requires exactly one arg.
- **`retry`** — usable in catch blocks; re-executes the try body with an automatic limit of 3 retries before a distinct exhaustion error; raises a
runtime error outside catch.
- **`errtype(err)`** — classifies errors as `"index"`, `"conversion"`, `"type"`, `"nil"`, `"retry"`, `"custom"`, or `"none"` for nil.

### Implementation
- Added `TryNode`, `CatchNode`, `FinallyNode`, `ThrowNode`, `RetryNode` AST nodes with visitor/print support.
- Extended the parser, type checker, compiler, and VM.
- Added a typed runtime error system (`vm/runtime/error.go`) and converted existing runtime panics (index, conversion, type mismatch, nil reference)
into classified errors.
- Added VM opcodes `OpTry`, `OpEndTry`, `OpRetry`, `OpErrContains`, `OpFinally`, `OpRethrow` with a try-frame stack that supports nested
try/catch/finally, proper scope/stack restoration, and finally-on-error unwinding.
- Added `errtype` as a builtin.
- Added documentation and a comprehensive test suite.

All existing tests plus new tests pass, and `go vet` is clean. All changes are committed on the `error-handling` branch.
